## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1 page)
### Climate Change Problem
Pakistan is facing a period of unprecedented climate-related stresses, having recently experienced a disastrous flood season that has displaced over 33 million people. The country is the sixth most populous in the world and lies in a mostly arid and semi-arid geographic area prone to recurring large-scale floods, droughts, and landslides. Climate change is expected to exacerbate these stresses, with an increase of up to 2°C by 2050. This will impact Pakistan's population, key ecosystems, and economy. With an average per capita water availability of less than 1,360 cubic meters (m³), Pakistan is highly dependent on water to support its predominantly agriculture-based economy, which accounts for 19% of GDP, 42% of jobs, and 85% of the annual freshwater output.


---
**Page 7**
---

2\. Observed trends and projected changes in climate under RCP 4.5 show increasing temperatures and greater variability in rainfall patterns. These changes lead to increased instances of droughts, flooding, and landslides, which negatively affect ecosystems nationwide and the communities that depend upon their proper functioning for water supply, food security, and livelihoods.

3\. Pakistan is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change. The country has experienced 100 million people affected by floods in 2010; 1-1.7 million homes damaged; 2 million acres of crops ruined; the loss of 755,000 livestock; injured, 12,700 people; and US$3 billion in damages. In 2011, Pakistan experienced a severe heat wave that lasted for two weeks and caused an estimated US$10 billion in damages resulting in the design of Recharge Pakistan as a large-scale project to address these issues.

4\. The Indus Basin serves as Pakistan's agricultural backbone, supporting 90% of agriculture production, and is vulnerable to climate change. In the Indus River basin, the average annual temperature has increased by 1°C since 1950. The country will experience an increase in rainfall in the higher volume months of July and August, with small decreases in the early part of the year (March–May). The frequency of heavy precipitation events is expected to increase, with more intense, short duration, high-intensity, depth and overall velocity, particularly in the upper catchments of the project sites. These changes will have a significant impact on water availability for irrigation.

5\. Vulnerable communities living in the basin depend on it for reliable water supply and recharge, agricultural production, climate regulation, and other ecosystem benefits. The assessment conducted as part of the Project's Feasibility Study (FS) identified four sites that are most vulnerable to climate change: three sites in the Indus River catchment and one site in the Chenab River catchment.

6\. Despite increased expectation in annual rainfall it is projected that there will occur shorter timeframes between periods of dry spells. As a result, if the frequency of dry spells increases over time, the longer the GPEI index as a proxy for drought shows a clear tendency towards drier years over the next century. See Annex 2: Feasibility Study for further details on the site-specific climate and hydrological conditions at each of the proposed project sites.

7\. Current efforts in Pakistan to manage severe floods and droughts caused by climatic change are largely reactive and ad hoc, with limited capacity to address the impacts on the country's population, economy and ecosystems. This is due to the country's resource and economic constraints, high level of vulnerability, and reliance on agriculture for livelihoods. The National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and Provincial Adaptation Plans (PAP) from making proactive investments in flood and drought risk reduction interventions that provide sustainable benefits.

8\. In addition to these conditions, Pakistan's current policy and regulatory instruments for water resource management are not aligned with the objectives of the NAP and PAP. The National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAP), and Provincial Adaptation Plans (PAP) – do not consider EBA and green infrastructure interventions to help reduce risks associated with climate change. There is a critical need for the circular flow of healthy ecosystems, increasing affected communities' resilience to climate change. There is an urgent need to shift away from traditional approaches towards more integrated and sustainable solutions.

9\. The proposed Recharge Pakistan project will provide a unique opportunity to demonstrate how green infrastructure will establish proof of concept for the integration of these interventions into the Provincial Adaptation Plans, which will be done after the project.

They also do not optimize the potential environmental and social benefits of such investments. The project will address these gaps by shifting Pakistan’s focus from a grey to green infrastructure approach for vulnerable communities. Public sector reform around flood and water resources management is needed which includes strengthening the capacity of provincial governments, local government bodies, and other relevant sector solutions to managing floods and droughts in Pakistan. The absence of such a shift in Pakistan's business-as-usual approach will continue to leave millions of people living in flood-prone areas and vulnerable communities' vulnerability to extreme events caused by climate change such as the recent 2022 floods.

## Proposed Approach

### B. Project Programme Information

#### B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

**Local Context**

Pakistan is characterized by diverse topography, ecosystems, and climate zones. Rich in natural resources, including fertile agricultural lands, natural gas reserves, and mineral deposits, Pakistan faces challenges in managing its water resources effectively due to the country's complex geography and climate variability. Despite being an industrialized country, Pakistan has grown from a primarily agriculture-based economy to a mostly service-based economy.


---
**Page 9**
---

(services constituting 48.4% of the Gross Domestic Product — GDP — in 2016). As of 2019 agriculture was still the largest employer, occupying 42.5% of the workforce. By 2031 approximately 29.5% of the population will be living below the poverty line (World Bank, 2018). The Indus River Basin is Pakistan’s most important water source and is situated at the western end of the great Indus-Ganga Plain. Of the total area of the country, about three-fifths consist of arid or semi-arid land. The climate in this region is characterized by a dry summer and wet winter with a monsoon level plain1). The land can be divided into five major regions: the Himalayan and Karakoram ranges and their subranges; the Indus River Plain; the Balochistan Plateau; the Thar Desert; and the semi-arid and arid western regions of the country.

13. Climatically, Pakistan lies in an exposure zone and its climate is as varied as the country’s topography—generally dry with a hot summer and wet during the low-lying plains of the Indus River and becoming progressively cooler towards the north. The climate can be divided into four seasons: (i) winter; (ii) spring; (iii) summer; and (iv) autumn. The winter is cold, dry, hot from March through May; the summer rainy season, also known as the southwest monsoon period, starts in June and ends in September with a mean temperature of 29.7°C to 30.5°C and a monthly rainfall range of 16 mm to October 84 mm for August and an average daily relative humidity of about 70%. The autumn is dry, hot, and sunny from October through December. In the northern regions, where monsoons can bring upwards of 200 mm a month from July to September (2011–2020) trends), precipitation in the Indus River Basin ranges between 500 mm and 700 mm per year. The climate has a significant influence on climatic variability in Pakistan, with anomalies in both temperature* and flood frequency and intensity.

The proposed project will be implemented in areas of the Indus River basin including Dera Ismail Khan (D.I.). Khan in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province; and Chakar Lehi in the Balochistan and Sindh provinces (Figure 1). These areas were identified based on the following criteria: i) suitability for adaptation to climate change, which includes phases such as: assessments based on biophysical, socio-economic, and ecological data and information; ii) risk assessment of vulnerability to climate change; iii) identification of adaptation options; iv) implementation of adaptation measures; and vii) suitability assessments based on respective ecosystems. Ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) and green infrastructure are considered as the most appropriate adaptation strategies for this project. The selection was made in consultation with stakeholders with clear climate objectives, co-benefits and components on preferred intervention types and ecosystem services to be delivered at each selected site. Additional detail on specific sites and their selection is presented in Section 3 of Annex 2.

See Annex 2: Feasibility Study for further details on the site-specific climate and hydrological analyses conducted for the proposed project, the results of which are presented in the research section below.

### Climate change problem

For most of its 200-plus years, Pakistan is a mostly arid and semi-arid geographic area prone to recurring large-scale floods, earthquakes, droughts, and landslides. Much of the economy relies on natural resources such as agriculture, livestock, forests, and minerals. In recent decades, however, there have been long-term increases in day and night-time temperatures as well as more varied rainfall totals. Further, there has also been a significant increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events leading to an increase in the number and severity of flooding events as well as crop and livestock exposure to severe drought conditions. These changes are expected to continue into the future with the baseline climate ranges making seasonal and medium-term forecasting increasingly difficult. The projected increases in temperature, precipitation, and other climatic variables tend to be on the upper or lower ranges of the baseline climate ranges making seasonal and medium-term forecasting increasingly difficult. In addition, there is a significant increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events leading to an increase in the number and severity of flooding events as well as crop and livestock exposure to severe drought conditions. These changes are expected to continue into the future with increased rainfall intensity projected over most months, a concentration of rainfall into a shorter duration period, and more frequent heavy precipitation events. The challenges currently faced by Pakistan are therefore expected to only increase under future climate scenarios.

### Projected changes in climate

- Temperature: 1.5°C to 2.0°C by the 2030s and 6.0°C by the 2090s, with projected increases higher during winter and in the north.
- Precipitation: Uncertainty exists regarding mean annual precipitation, with projections for changes in monthly rainfall ranging from a 20% decrease to a 41% increase by the 2090s.
- Decreased snowfall.
- Increased frequency and intensity of extreme climate events, including floods and droughts.

# Local Vulnerability

Pakistan hosts considerable social vulnerability to disaster. High poverty and malnutrition rates prevail, and many communities and minority groups are marginalized by socio-economic status, location, and political circumstances.

### Climate Change Impacts

Climate change is expected to increase the frequency of extreme weather events such as heatwaves, droughts, floods, and storms. These natural hazards and their likely exposure above average climate changes should be seen in the context of its vulnerability. Pakistan has experienced a number of major disasters over recent years, including earthquakes, floods, and cyclones.

### Recent Flooding

The most recent flooding in Pakistan in August/September 2022 destroyed 318,000 hectares of agricultural land, killed or injured more than 574 people, displaced nearly 6.9 million people, and caused physical and economic terms. More recently, severe flooding in Pakistan in August/September 2022 destroyed 318,000 hectares of agricultural land, killed or injured more than 574 people, displaced nearly 6.9 million people, and caused physical and economic terms.

### Climate Change and Water Resources

Pakistan's current climate, water resources are both a benefit and a threat. Pakistan's water surface temperature has increased by about 0.3°C per decade since the early 1980s. The increase in water surface temperatures is caused by snow and glacier melt, with the remainder being caused by monsoon rains. Temperature increases have led to an increase in evaporation rates from lakes and reservoirs, which has resulted in lake outburst floods, as seen in the Pasaia village (Sindh Balochistan region) in 2022.

### Agriculture

Agriculture employs 45% of those employed in Pakistan, and nearly all arable land is cultivated. The irrigation methods used are based on river systems and are highly vulnerable to climate change, such as rising temperatures and extreme weather events, has resulted in devastating crop losses, altered growing seasons, increased pest infestations, and reduced yields.

### Health Impacts

Pakistan's health sector is also highly vulnerable to rising temperatures and droughts. Heat stress has caused heat waves and rising sea levels endanger the fertile Indus River plain, which has lost nearly 810,000 ha of arable land and irrigation infrastructure in recent years due to climate change.

### Energy Supply

Pakistan's energy supply is augmented by hydropower, which accounts for 35% of total generation. Reduced water availability from the Indus River system has led to a decline in hydroelectric power production capacity, particularly during the summer when cooling demand is highest, have resulted in ongoing power outages and

## Observed climate change and noted impact

### Pakistan

In Pakistan, temperatures are estimated to have risen by 0.5°C over the twentieth century, with recent warming accelerating by 4.7°C between 1961 and 2007. These increases are most pronounced in the northwestern parts of the country where the highest temperature increase has been recorded. The frequency of heat waves is also increasing.

### Key Findings

- **Human Performance:** Human performance, health, and occupational health are affected by climate change.
- **Agriculture:** Climate change impacts agricultural productivity through changes in crop growth rate and water availability.
- **Water Resources:** Water resources are stressed due to increased evaporation rates and reduced precipitation. This leads to a decline in groundwater levels.

20. Precipitation in Pakistan’s historical profile is significantly more complex. Both the annual volumes and the SPI-360 indices show long term oscillations. The early twentieth century saw a prolonged decline in annual rainfall, which was followed by an increase from around 1950 to 1980. Since about 1980, mean rainfall in Pakistan’s plains and coastal belt has decreased by 10% to 15%. The majority of other regions have experienced increases in precipitation over the past few decades. There is a general trend that number of heavy rainfall events has increased, with the nine heaviest rains recorded in 24 hours occurring in 2010.

21. Rainfall trends dating back to the 1980s show general increases in recent years (since 2000) compared to the lower values observed from around 1950 until about 1970. There have been several clusters of wetter years, especially in the 1990s, some of which are consecutive, as well as clusters of wetter years in the 2000s, especially in the 2010s. The increase is statistically significant over this time frame, but longer timescales indicate an oscillation of rainfall characteristics.

22. There are more extreme events in the northern and eastern parts of the country, and fewer in the southern and western parts of the country (Figure 3). This corresponds to the spatial patterns of the observed annual rainfall volume and SPI-360 indices. The number of days with heavy rain per year has increased over time. There is also an increase in the number of days with more than 10 mm of rain in 24 hours. Both are indicators of larger-scale events that could result in flooding.

22. There are numerous dry spells (5 consecutive dry days) across the country, with the majority concentrated in the south and fewer in the north (Figure 4). This is reflected in lower annual rainfall volumes in the south and higher volatility of rainfall over time compared to the north. The earlier parts of the NODC have a more even distribution of wet and dry years.

When considering the entire period, there has been an overall increase in the number of wet spells since the 1980s; this is less balanced than previously noted. There is also a decrease in the number of months within the normal SPOT range, which has led to more extreme weather events such as floods and droughts.

The most recent decades have also shown a trend toward wet years, though there are still some dry months.

### 24. On the ground, these climate changes have had a significant impact and these natural disasters, which already dominate the disaster landscape of the region, are expected to become more frequent and intense [Figure 1]. Flooding is the most serious threat to most people in the area and it was at 8 out of 10 on the INFORM 2014 Index for Risk Management for Pakistan. In 2010, a severe flood occurred in Sindh province, affecting over 5 million people and killing more than 3,000. They were followed in 2011 by heavy monsoon rains that affected 9.7 million people. In recent years, this trend has affected the entire country with floods in 2014 (6.8 million), 2015 (10.5 million) and 2016 (3.5 million). The INFORM index was at 5.1 out of 10, though the distinction between those being affected by drought and living in arid conditions is difficult to draw [Figure 4]. In 2017, Pakistan experienced a severe heatwave with temperatures reaching up to 48°C. This was followed by a clear trend in the changes associated with landslides; however, as seen in the 2010 events, there is a correlation between rainfall and flooding events. It is reasonable to expect increased flooding activity to result in increased rainfall-induced landslides.

### Figure 5. Number of people affected from hydro-meteorological hazards in Pakistan (1980-2020) for flood and drought (top left), storm surge (bottom left), heatwave (top right) and cold wave (bottom right).

#### 25. The 2010 "super-flood" mentioned above inundated large swaths of Pakistan, resulting in the displacement of more than 20 million people. This flood event was called one of the worst humanitarian disasters ever experienced by the country [Figure 4]. In addition to the flooding and subsequent landslides, heavy monsoon rains, resulted in such devastation that United Nations Secretary General Antonio Guterres referred to the inundation as "catastrophic". The floods affected 20 million people across Pakistan. The floodwaters reached parts of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Sindh, and parts of Balochistan. Approximately 20% of the land area of Pakistan and over 20 million people were affected by the flooding in 2010 [Figure 5]. In comparison to the 2010 floods, by comparison, have affected a larger proportion of Pakistan with over 33 million people affected (Figure 6). The 2010 flood was described as "catastrophic", while the 2022 floods were described as "calamity hit" with close to 1.4 million since June 2022, according to data released by the National Disaster Management Authority [NDMA]. The 2022 floods have been described as a "catastrophic disaster", in comparison to the disasters of 2010. The consequences of the 2022 floods are far-reaching and unprecedented as they have exposed the imminent threat of food insecurity, waterborne diseases, malnutrition, and social unrest in Pakistan.

Projected climate baseline and extremes

27\. Pakistan is an arid land and country prone to recurring large-scale floods, droughts, and landslides, and future climate conditions are expected to exacerbate these effects. Over the different scenarios from the 1986-2025 baseline period, the projected increase in mean annual temperature for Pakistan ranges between +0.3°C by 2025 and up to +6.0°C by 2090\. There is also spatial uncertainty in Pakistan's rainfall, with models predicting decreases of up to -18% over the northwestern parts of the country and increases of up to +47% over the northeastern parts. The projected increase in mean annual precipitation ranges between +5% by 2025 and up to +36% by 2090\. There is also an increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme climate events, including heat waves, heavy rainfall, and droughts. This will lead to increased evapotranspiration, higher temperatures. There is also an increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme climate events, including heat waves, heavy rainfall, and droughts. This will lead to increased evapotranspiration, higher temperatures.

The number of people affected by extreme river flooding is expected to rise by 5 million between 2035 and 2044\.


---
**Page 17**
---

By 2070-2100, sea levels are expected to rise by 35–80 cm, exposing an additional 1 million people to coastal flooding**.

The project focuses on the two catchments of the Indus River basin: Sutlej and Chadar Leh. Climate assessment indicates conductive climate areas to persist future climate exposure. Because of the size of the Chadar Leh catchment, different future climate change signals may be present. The evaluation was divided into two parts: northern and southern.

Rainfall patterns vary spatially, with a decrease in the far west and coasts of northern areas and an increase in the southern and central northern areas (Figure 7). While each catchment has a different seasonal character, most locations show a shift toward more frequent heavy rainfall events. This is particularly evident for Chadar Leh, where there is a potential focusing of rainfall into these already higher volume months. This is visible in the accumulation anomaly, which shows an increase in the number of days with positive accumulation in the studied areas. Total annual volume increases less than the years 2005-2010 and more than the years 2030-2040.

The increased intensity of rainfall also sees the greatest increase in rainfall variability, implying that rainfall cessation volumes will be more volatile.


| Figure 7 | Changes in projected annual rainfall (2045–2060) map, monthly rainfall anomaly (average and column graph), and daily rainfall frequency (1981-2000) for the Indus River basin catchments of Sutlej and Chadar Leh south for RCP 4.5 n=3 |

The observed concentration of rainfall in the mid-late part of the year, combined with the projected increase in temperature, will lead to an increase in extreme daily maximum temperatures. The frequency of occurrence of extreme rainfall days is increasing, primarily in northern parts of the country where ocean mitigation is less effective.

The increased intensity and variability of rainfall also increases the magnitude of large flood return events at all locations.

## Changes in the projected frequency of extreme rainfall days - 2035-2065 (map), intensity, and monthly peak intensity changes for Di Khan, Chakar Bahi north, and Chakar Bahi south.

### Figure 4. Changes in the projected frequency of extreme rainfall days - 2035-2065 (map), intensity, and monthly peak intensity changes for Di Khan, Chakar Bahi north, and Chakar Bahi south.

**31.** In the projected future, the increase in rainfall intensity and volume shifts the 1:100 flood design storm toward higher peak intensity and greater volume falling faster. This modeled flood shows an increase in peak depth, particularly at larger return periods (e.g., 50 years). The increased precipitation intensity means that the peak lag time is shorter, and the flood peak is expected to arrive earlier in the projected future.

**32.** Under future climate conditions (2020-2050; RCP4.5), there is a project increase in the occurrence of larger magnitude flood events compared those experienced under current conditions across the project’s target areas. This increased frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall events will lead to more frequent flooding, particularly during the monsoon season when evaporation rates are lower due to higher humidity levels and more moisture in the air. As warmer air holds more moisture, this will make monsoon rainfall more intense over Pakistan. The increase in precipitation intensity means that the peak lag time is shorter, and the flood peak is expected to arrive earlier in the projected future.

The increased frequency of these flood events will see an increase in the inundation area, a widening of flow channels and more significant water depth changes. Areas where flows are faster, such as the river channel, are expected to experience greater increases in velocity and depth flows, are expected to experience only moderate changes in the area inundated. Future scenarios do however show that areas where flood events are slower, such as those found in the floodplain or areas where floods are slower. As the water slows down in these areas, the water spreads out, pooling in the flatter areas.

33. Despite expected variable increases in annual rainfall, there is a focus of rainfall in shorter timeframes and thus an increase in the number of dry spells per year (Figure 12). This is most noticeable in the north, but there is a positive anomaly running northeast to the southwest. The number of dry spells in the eastern, far northern, and southern regions shows no significant change or trend. The projected changes in annual rainfall are highly uncertain near the end-of-the-century scenario. The locations show an annual change in dry days ranging from -10% to +25%. The precipitation anomaly map for 2081-2100 shows a clear tendency toward negative (dry) years. Droughts are expected to become more severe and frequent across the globe over this time period.

Figure 12. Changes in the projected number of dry spells - 2035-2065 (map), decadal anomaly number of dry days annually (column graph), and long-term SPEI (standardized evapotranspiration index) (line graph) for DI Khan, Chakar Lehri north, and Chaker Lehri south.

34. The projected future shows variable annual rainfall volume, but more concentrated rainfall with longer dry spells and more dry days per year (Figure 13). Furthermore, an increase in maximum and minimum temperatures, as well as an increase in evapotranspiration, is expected to lead to a decrease in water yield. For RCP4.5 2020-2050, there is a general decrease in water yield, particularly in the lower reaches of the catchments, compared to 1980-2010 values.

### Soil & Water Assessment Tool (SWAT)
- Modelling of water yield (WY) and evapotranspiration (ET) for the period 2030–2089 under RCP4.5.
- Includes: precipitation, temperature, runoff, lateral flow, groundwater, and soil moisture.
- The model is based on daily rainfall, temperature, and evaporation characteristics.

### Figure 13
**Change in water yield characteristics (SWAT) for Di Khan (left) and Chakar Lebri (centre right)**

Text: Temperature increases are observed inland, away from the ocean's thermal mitigating effects. The greatest increases are observed along the southwest to the northeast axis, with smaller changes observed in the northwestern part of the region. This is consistent across all models except for one, indicating that the models are generally in agreement. The shift in average maximum temperatures shifts the entire temperature profile away from cooler conditions and towards higher mid-continental temperatures. Each of the areas has an average trend of +4.5°C before the decade, but this increases later in the century.

## Figure 3.4: Projected annual average peak maximum temperature - 2020-2060 (imag), long-term maximum temperature regression envelope graph, and maximum temperature occurrence profile (line graph) for Dikhan, Chaklar Lehri north, and Charak Ladakh.

The shift from cooler to hotter and more extreme temperatures results in an increase in the number of extreme temperatures over most of the country each year. The number of extreme temperature days has increased in all locations since 1950. The number of extremely hot days (≥40°C) has increased significantly in all locations. By mid-century, the number of heatwaves will nearly double, and these heatwaves will be several degrees warmer than current heatwaves.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 22 OF 156

## Figure 19: Changes in projected extreme heat days - 2035-2085 (map), number of days over 40°C (line graph), and frequency and magnitude changes (line and column graph) for Di Khan, Chakar Leng north, and Chakar Leng south for RCP4.5, 6.0.

There is a larger increase in minimum temperatures to the northeast, which is the coldest part of the country, and a smaller increase over the rest of the country. The largest increases are projected to occur between 2035-2085 in minimum temperatures, with near linear increases over the next century and majority model agreement. The rate of increase for maximum temperature is similar to that of minimum temperature. However, because both maximum and minimum temperatures are rising, the fact that minimum temperatures are rising faster results in a reduction in the diurnal range. This will change the country's thermal interchange character.

Most likely scenario with any climate action

### Long-Term Projections

Long-term projections of climate change are visible in Pakistan's current climatic conditions. The rainfall forecast is currently uncertain. While some models predict generally increased rainfall, there is a lot of variability in these. Rainfall will likely increase over the next 50 years but with more frequent and intense events, as was the case in 2010, resulting in major floods causing wide-reaching environmental, social, and economic impacts. Return events of this type, and the floods associated with them, are more likely to occur in the future.

The climate impacts mentioned, particularly rainfall changes and the associated droughts and floods, will likely further exacerbate existing vulnerabilities such as those living in the flood- and drought-vulnerable Indus River Valley. If no cost-effective and sustainable

# Table 3: Summary of the most relevant past and ongoing projects and investments that will be complemented by the proposed project

| Scoping out global lake outburst flood (GLOF) risk in Northern Pakistan |
|---|
| The objectives of the project is to empower communities to identify and manage the risks associated with GLOFs and enhance their capacity for early warning. It aims at strengthening the public service systems to lower the risks of GLOFs. The proposed project will be implemented by FAO. The target districts are Gilgit Baltistan and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa. The project area is 10,524 km². The project duration is 37.5m. Category B. |

| Transforming the Indus Basin into a climate resilient water resources management |
|---|
| This project is being implemented by FAO. The project benefits 1.3m direct and 6.9m indirect beneficiaries in the provinces of Punjab, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Gilgit Baltistan, Azad Kashmir, and the districts of Sindh (Karachi, Badin, Larkana). |

| Living Indus Initiative |
|---|
| This initiative is ongoing, delivered jointly by the Ministry of Climate Change, and led by the MoCC (NSA), in collaboration with FAO. It is a strategic framework for Pakistan, contributing to climate change adaptation work focused on enhancing resilience of the country’s Indus Basin. |

It is a GCF-funded adaptation project and is being implemented by UNDP. The project benefits 13m direct and 28.5m indirect beneficiaries.

It is a GCF-funded adaptation project and is being implemented by FAO. The project is benefiting 1.3m direct and 6.9m indirect beneficiaries in the provinces of Punjab, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Gilgit Baltistan, Azad Kashmir, and the districts of Sindh (Karachi, Badin, Larkana).

The major objective is to transform agriculture in the Indus Basin into a climate-resilient sector. This will be achieved by farmers and strengthening the government’s capacity to implement climate change adaptation strategies. The project is building farmers’ climate resilience through skills development, access to information, and providing them with technological fronts for creating a wider enabling environment. It also aims at reducing the impact of climate change on agriculture and water management in the Indus Basin. This will be achieved by using climate change scenarios for Pakistan.

While there is no overview in gatherings and participating stakeholders, lessons from existing GCF-funded projects, lessons from the ongoing project, and lessons from other relevant sectors (e.g., public sector) are being used to ensure that the proposed project is sustainable and cost-effective. The impact of climate change on agriculture and water management will be reduced by using climate change scenarios for Pakistan.

The proposed project is identified as a main contributor to the Living Indus Initiative, which aims at enhancing resilience of the Indus Basin through its focus on implementing adaptation strategies in the basin. It also aims at reducing the increasing impacts of floods and droughts on the basin and its communities. The project will be implemented by FAO.

#### Enhance community, local and national resilience to climate change by increasing resistance to floods and droughts in urban areas.
This project focuses on reducing the impacts of floods and droughts on people living in urban areas in the three component countries: (i) enhancing connectivity; and (ii) improving water storage capacity in public buildings and water storage in public gardens, as well as providing early warning systems for flooding and response to the impacts of climate change; and (iii) developing guidelines city-level development considering climate change. Lessons learned from this baseline project, particularly related to flood risk management and early warning systems, will be used to inform the implementation of the proposed adaptation measures where data are available.

### B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1000 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

**Impact: Paradigm shift**

If climate-resilient infrastructure interventions are implemented in the Indus Basin and are supported by corresponding changes in the National Water Policy, the National Adaptation Plan and Provincial Adaptation Action Plans, it will lead to a paradigm shift from a focus on flood control to one of water resource management in Pakistan. Furthermore, if targeted communities are engaged in ecosystem restoration and management practices that align with climate-resilient infrastructure interventions, they can benefit not only from additional protection to floods and droughts through additional EBA and green infrastructure interventions but also from the benefits of improved livelihood security.

The project will address the above mentioned barriers by convincing key stakeholders of the benefits and cost-effectiveness of these interventions. The project will also ensure that communities are able to sustain these interventions and operationalise future EBA and green infrastructure measures, and continue to benefit from them in the long-term. In addition, the project will work with provincial governments to ensure that they have the capacity to implement and scale up climate-resilient infrastructure.

The project will address the above mentioned barriers by convincing key stakeholders of the benefits and cost-effectiveness of these interventions. The project will also ensure that communities are able to sustain these interventions and operationalise future EBA and green infrastructure measures, and continue to benefit from them in the long-term. In addition, the project will work with provincial governments to ensure that they have the capacity to implement and scale up climate-resilient infrastructure.

### Target communities

The project will target local target communities, including small-scale farmers, living in four project sites in the Indus Basin, namely the Dih Khan Watershed, Ramak Watershed, Chahar Lehri Watershed, and Machan Lake and Upper Jhelum River Watersheds. These areas are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change-induced reduced flows and droughts, which are already negatively affecting their water and livelihood security.

### Outcomes and co-benefits

The project will achieve the following outcomes:

* **Outcome 1:** EBA and green infrastructure interventions with improved community-led management reduce flood risk and enhance the climate resilience of the most vulnerable people in Pakistan’s Indus Basin will be achieved by:
    * Implementing upstream water harvesting structures to increase water availability for irrigation, living downstream trough enhanced ecosystem services such as slowing of water flows during peak flow onset, and contributing to reduced sedimentation.
    * Implementing green infrastructure interventions at the project sites to reduce flood risk through the implementation of upstream vegetation and delayed peak flood.
    * Implementing green infrastructure interventions to reduce flood risk by implementing EBA and green infrastructure interventions in the hill forests located in the project area.

* **Outcome 2:** Enabling environment for climate action that has new processes for implementing EBA and green infrastructure interventions for flood and water resources management in Pakistan will be achieved by using the above.

evidence of the effectiveness and efficiency of EIA and green infrastructure interventions in enhancing food and water resources management generated under Outcome 1 to expand Pakistan's regulations and policies for food and water security, as well as to enhance the capacity of communities and smallholder farmers to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices. This will be supported by strengthening the capacity of national and sub-national staff, as well as community-based organizations (CBOs) to adopt EIA and green infrastructure interventions under Outcome 1 to channel future investments into EIA and green infrastructure interventions and replicate similar adaptation strategies in other parts of Pakistan.

50. Outcome: Enhanced resilience of community livelihoods in the Indus Basin will be achieved by training communities and smallholder farmers on how to enhance existing agricultural practices, prevalent across the Indus Basin, through the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices (Activity 1.1.2), such as water harvesting, soil conservation, water management to enhance yields and reduce agricultural expansion, and introducing new crop varieties that are more resilient to climate change.

Additionally, new opportunities for sustainable enterprises will be supported to provide alternative revenue generation in the Indus Basin. This will include support for community-based organizations (CBOs) to diversify livelihoods directly dependent on natural resources. Project pipeline sourcing, incubation, and technical assistance to enhance business development will help reduce ecosystem degradation and increase revenue generation for businesses and families.

51. The project will achieve the following co-benefits, with additional co-benefits described in Section D.03 of this Funding Proposal:

- Co-benefit 1: GHG emission reduction from ecosystem restoration and improved ecosystem management. This co-benefit is described in detail under Activity 1.1.2 (EIA interventions) and Activity 1.1.3 (green infrastructure interventions) (under Activity 1.1.3). These mitigation co-benefits are described in further detail in Section D.01 of this Funding Proposal.

- Co-benefit 2: Improved biodiversity as a result of the project's EIA and green infrastructure interventions. This will be achieved by restoring degraded agro-ecological landscapes in watersheds in di Khan (Activity 1.1.2), increasing fish species richness and abundance (Macro-invertebrates) at Indus Game Reserve (Di Khan); increases in fish species richness and abundance (Macro-invertebrates) at the project sites; increased population abundance of freshwater turtles (hard-shelled and soft-shelled turtles); and its contribution to the priority conservation areas of Pakistan, including the Indus Basin. The project will also contribute to protected sites as sites of global ecological importance such as Man and Biosphere Reserve and Ramsar Sites.

52. Outputs and activities:

Co: This output will deliver the following outputs through the associated activities:

53. Output 1.1: EIA interventions implemented to restore floodplain and watershed ecosystems will be achieved by restoring 14,215 ha of degraded agro-ecological landscapes in watersheds in di Khan (Activity 1.1.2) and rehabilitating 60 km of riverbanks in the Indus Basin (Activity 1.1.3). The EIA interventions will secure the flood and drought risk reduction benefits they provide to communities living in the basin. The interventions will also contribute to the restoration of degraded agro-ecological landscapes, which are vulnerable communities living in the Indus Basin and secure their livelihoods.

Output 1.2: Green infrastructure interventions established in Pakistan's Indus Basin will be achieved by designing and implementing green infrastructure interventions (Activity 1.1.3) to reduce flooding on community settlements in the four project sites in the Indus Basin, namely the Di Khan Watershed, Ramsar Watershed, Chakar Lehman Watershed, and Man and Biosphere Reserve. The green infrastructure interventions will include rainwater harvesting ponds, embankments, and gabion bundles will be designed to complement the EIA interventions (under Output 1.1) to ensure that communities living in these project sites are protected from flooding and drought.

Output 1.3: Improved water resource management to support water supply and flood attenuation services provided by EIA Interventions implemented in floodplains and watersheds will be achieved by:

- Strengthening the capacity of CBOs to adopt EIA and green infrastructure interventions and undertake climate-resilient community-based natural resources management (Activity 1.3.1), and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 33 OF 156

## Barrier 2: EDA and green infrastructure interventions are not included in Pakistan's National Water Policy, NWP; and Provincial Adaptation Plans for Sindh, Punjab, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, and Balochistan.

A wide variety of environmental and social benefits (e.g., GHG emissions avoidance/reduction benefits and the protection of agricultural land from floods and droughts). This leads to the GDP being trapped in the reactive use of green infrastructure interventions. The costs are often prohibitive for international donors and foreign aid, as well as post-disaster situations, including for flood and drought recovery.

### Barrier 3: Inadequate technical capacity across government and communities for implementing EDA and green infrastructure interventions.

The NWP does not provide a framework or guidance on how to implement EDA and green infrastructure interventions. The MoCC-relevant Provincial Departments such as the Provincial Departments of Irrigation, Forestry and Agriculture and Provincial Water and Power Boards are responsible for implementing EDA and green infrastructure interventions. However, these departments lack the technical capacity to implement, monitor, operate and maintain EDA and green infrastructure interventions. These capacity constraints also extend into communities where there is a lack of awareness about the benefits of EDA and green infrastructure interventions.

### Barrier 4: Limited access to finance among the most vulnerable communities and micro, small, and medium enterprises (MSMEs) for climate-resilient, sustainable businesses.

There are few institutions and resources available in Pakistan that can provide financial support to MSMEs. The lack of institutional capacity is a major barrier to scaling up EDA and green infrastructure interventions. There is also limited awareness among communities about the benefits of EDA and green infrastructure interventions. This has led to a lack of demand for these businesses, which further limits access to finance.

### Barrier 5: Current use practices and methods do not consider climate change impacts on agricultural land.

Communities in the Indus Basin have relied heavily on traditional farming techniques such as rice cultivation, including farming and cattle raising, and other livelihoods that are vulnerable to increasing vulnerability to threats from flooding and drought due to climate change. These traditional practices have often relied on the conversion of ecosystems and the extraction of natural resources for their livelihoods.

### Assumptions

Key assumptions are listed below with more detailed notes and assumptions presented as required in Section E: Logical Framework.
- Communities have limited and/or sufficient resources to reduce their increasing vulnerability to threats from flooding and drought to engage with and sustain project activities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 34 OF 156

## Market failure and absence of financing in respect to CSA

The Government of Pakistan maintains its commitment to mainstream EBA and green infrastructure solutions to climate change within its national climate change strategy.

Climate change is a major challenge and precludes and climate change impacts are adequately addressed by interventions of this project.

### Crop and market entry

In the context of Pakistan, agriculture outlays a diverse range of crops that vary depending on the region and climate. Commonly grown crops include wheat, rice, cotton, sugarcane, maize, and vegetables such as tomatoes, onions, potatoes, and beans. These are sold in local markets or to traders who sell them at wholesale prices for further processing into food products.

In years where there is a surplus yield, this surplus is sold in the market at lower prices than those of other crops. In years meeting subsistence needs challenging. Additionally, due to limited access to resources such as transportation and storage facilities, farmers are unable to store their produce and often receive low prices for their crops. As a result, their ability to engage in commercial agriculture is limited and they continue primarily for subsistence and household consumption to support household food security.

### Financial challenges for smallholder farmers

In Pakistan, farmers do not have access to loans for smallholder for climate-smart agriculture related interventions to demonstrate or pilot on the ground. Traditional farming facilities mainly focus on financial products that cover procurement of seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, and other inputs required for production. However, there are no traditional financial products due to pledging requirements in the form of land, which often times is estimated to be less than 1 hectare.

The process of applying for loans through a formal lending institution involves submitting an application for filing loan applications is a task that requires significant understanding of financial jargon and being able to demonstrate repayment capacity.

According to an analysis conducted by FAO of nine countries, including countries from South Asia, limited financial literacy, collateral history, and few alternative sources of income are common obstacles faced by smallholder farmers in accessing credit. The study also highlights the lack of awareness among smallholder farmers about sustainable farming practices. A farm level analysis that studies the demand for credit by smallholder farmers in Sindh, Pakistan found that most farmers have limited access to credit facilities due to their insufficient credit history and collateral.

The majority of smallholder farmers are engaged in subsistence agriculture with low income levels, low landholdings (25%), and less than 1 hectare. According to the FAO study, about 63-85 percent of farmers have no access to formal financial services. About 40% of these individuals are women, and about 43 percent are managing landholdings of less than one hectare.

### Limited Access to Credit and Finance

Smallholder farmers in Pakistan often face difficulties in accessing credit and finance due to their insufficient credit history and collateral. This lack of access can lead to financial constraints that may not meet the requirements of lending institutions. Even when credit is available, high interest rates and short repayment periods are common challenges faced by smallholder farmers.

### Additional Financial Challenges for CSA

Smallholders do not have sufficient funds to adopt new climate resilient agriculture practices. These smallholders always face a lack of access to finance that hampers the necessary capital to purchase relevant inputs for planting and harvesting climate-resilient crops. There are several studies that highlight this as one of the significant challenges faced by smallholder farmers in Pakistan. According to a report by FAO, smallholder farmers encounter considerable challenges when seeking financial support for sustainable farming practices due to their insufficient credit history and collateral. The report highlighted that lack of access to finance can limit the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 35 OF 156

## Adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices, which can in turn limit the ability of smallholders to adapt to the impact of climate change.

### Challenges for Smallholder Farmers in Pakistan

#### Lack of Access to Credit and Financial Support
76. For smallholders to adopt climate-smart agriculture practices, a strong business case that demonstrates cost savings and/or increased income levels needs to be demonstrated. For this, global projects need to be funded by grant financing to raise awareness around the financial benefits of climate-smart agriculture, allowing farmers to see first-hand the potential for profitability in these practices.

77. Lack of access to credit is a major barrier for smallholder farmers who want to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices. Smallholder farmers may not have the means to purchase the inputs and technologies required for climate-smart agriculture, such as drought-resistant seeds, efficient irrigation systems, and weather monitoring tools, which can be prohibitively expensive.

78. In addition, financial institutions may not fully understand the benefits of climate-smart agriculture practices or the risks associated with climate change. They may not see these practices as profitable or may view them as too risky, particularly in Pakistan where smallholder farmers are already struggling to meet their basic needs and have limited access to credit.

79. These are some technical challenges faced by smallholder farmers in Pakistan’s sector for adopting and spreading climate-smart agricultural practices. These are highlighted in GCF PPR 17: Transforming the Indian Subcontinent with Climate-resilient Agriculture and Water Management (2018).

#### Lack of Access to Markets
80. Smallholder farmers in Pakistan face the following challenges to adapt to climate-smart agriculture:

- **Limited Knowledge and Skills**: Smallholder farmers may not have access to training or extension services on climate-smart agricultural practices, which limits their ability to implement effective climate-smart agriculture practices.
- **Inadequate Access to Information and Extension Services**: Smallholder farmers in Pakistan may not have access to information and extension services on weather-based early warning systems that could help them adapt to climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 36 OF 156

## Extension services that are available may not be accessible to all farmers or may not be tailored to the specific needs of smallholder farmers.

### Areas where vulnerable communities, including women and children, do not have access to key inputs that they could deploy on their lands to target the impacts of increasing heat and rainfall extremes on crop productivity in existing farming methods, including irrigation systems and improved seed varieties.

Therefore, shifting agricultural practices among smallholder farmers in Pakistan that better equip these vulnerable populations to cope with the changes of climate change requires:

- Building farmer capacity through training and technical assistance on climate-smart agriculture practices. This helps farmers improve their technical knowledge and skills and increases their awareness about climate-resilient livelihoods.
- Demonstrating climate-smart agriculture practices through on-farm trials and demonstrations. On-farm demonstration is a key strategy for the successful adoption of new technologies and practices, especially among smallholder farmers because it shows how the practices are operationalized and produced in the same conditions as they would be at home. This will also help to overcome the common perception that climate-smart agriculture practices are too expensive or “too difficult” by the farmer as they can see the concrete benefits of these practices firsthand and encourage them to adopt.
- Linking farmers to bio-inputs, such as drought-resistant seeds, bio-fertilizers, and bio-pesticides. This helps smallholder farmers overcome the financial barriers that prevent them from adopting climate-smart agriculture practices.

### Under the proposed project, a total US$128,910 will be channeled towards improving climate-resilient livelihoods among vulnerable communities in Pakistan (Output 3.2). Regarding points 1 and 2 above, promoting climate-smart agricultural practices through training and technical assistance on climate-smart agriculture practices, and demonstrating climate-smart agriculture practices through on-farm trials and demonstrations will be funded by the project. The remaining funds will be used to fund and development projects to address the technical and financial challenges face to adopt these climate-resilient livelihoods.

### These traditional grant funded activities total US$1306,310 or 85% of the activity total, distributed to staff, consultant, travel, workshop communications and convening costs as detailed in Annex 4. 

#### GCF grant funds will be used to directly address the technical challenges faced by smallholder farmers in the project sites. These include lack of access to climate-smart agricultural practices, technologies, and market opportunities for women and people depending on agriculture on climate-resilient agriculture and sustainable practices through a Farmer Field School (FFS) especially among vulnerable communities. The FFS will be implemented as per the guidelines provided by the province-specific curriculum, training modules, and workplans for Climate-Smart FFs by WFP-Pakistan, supported by the GCF. The FFS will also provide an opportunity to introduce climate-smart agricultural practices in the FFS on improved climate resilience of soil management practices and introducing on-farm best management practices (BMP) that are climate-resilient. This will be done through a series of training modules and workshops, and adoption of project activities under Output 3.2 as specified in Output 3.2: Gender Assessment and Action Plan. Specifically, the FFS will provide an opportunity to introduce climate-smart agricultural practices for soil quality building of land women to adopt gender-responsive climate-resilient livelihoods under Output 3.2.1. The FFS will also provide an opportunity to introduce climate-smart agricultural practices for sustainable rural development.

#### GCF grant funds will support the implementation of these activities focused on delivering technical assistance and extension services to the most vulnerable agricultural communities and smallholder farmers in the project’s sites.

The remaining US$222,600 (15% of the total investment) will be used to: i) improve the water efficiency of farming through improved farm management and irrigation systems by providing farmers the required components and inputs for drip irrigation; ii) provide drought-resistant seeds to vulnerable communities in Pakistan including drought-resistant crop varieties, including the provision of seed varieties to participating farmers; and iii) provide 15 water tanks and taps for use and for access to vulnerable agricultural communities across the three provinces (Sindh, Balochistan and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa).

# Overcoming Barriers to Achieve Impact

The project will achieve these benefits through three interconnected components described in detail in Section B.3. These barriers and their associated sub-activities have been designed to address the described barriers and achieve the Theory of Change presented in Figure 17 below.

## Figure 17. Theory of Change Impact

### Overcoming Barriers to Achieve Impact
The project will invest US$64 million in implementing EBA and green infrastructure interventions on the ground in four sites in the Indus Basin to reduce the impacts of increasingly severe floods and droughts, as well as other climate change-related hazards. This will be achieved by establishing proofs of concept for EBA and green infrastructure interventions that provide evidence of their cost-effectiveness and efficiency in reducing the impacts of climate change-induced hazards.

### Barrier 2
Barrier 2 will be addressed by expanding Pakistan's regulations and policies for flood and water resources management to include the implementation of grey infrastructure, EBA, and green infrastructure interventions. This will be achieved through a step-wise approach: an economic valuation of the benefits of these interventions; an analysis of their cost-effectiveness and efficiency in reducing the impacts of climate change-induced hazards; and an assessment of the potential for their adaptation benefits to initiate public sector reform in flood and water resources management at the national level. This will be achieved through a step-wise approach: an economic valuation of the benefits of these interventions, followed by an analysis of their cost-effectiveness and efficiency in reducing the impacts of climate change-induced hazards; and an assessment of the potential for their adaptation benefits to initiate public sector reform in flood and water resources management at the national level. The project will be managed by the CHCC and comprised of regulators within the MoHFW (including FFC) and the relevant Provincial Governments. The following table lists the key regulations and policies that will be reviewed and adapted as part of Pakistan's key regulations and policies for flood and water resources management, namely the Implementation


---
**Page 41**
---

Framework for the National Water Policy, the NAP, and the Provincial Adaptation Plans of Sindh, Khyber, and Balochistan, and Punjab provinces — on the proper execution of EIA and green infrastructure interventions to reduce flood risk in Pakistan.

90. The project will address Barrier 3 by securing the update and application of these updated procedures by national and provincial government involved in flood and water resources management in Pakistan. This will be achieved through capacity building workshops for officials from the National Water Resources Commission, Provincial Departments of Irrigation, Agriculture, and Forestry on the requirements of the updated procedures and how to apply them effectively.

91. The project will address Barrier 4 by supporting the implementation of the EIA and green infrastructure interventions at the community-driven projects. Technical staff and community representatives will also be trained on effective and efficient procedures for the application of these interventions so that they can implement them to reduce their flood and drought risk reduction potential over the long term.

92. To address Barrier 6, WWF Pakistan will leverage its experience developing and implementing the EIA and Flood Fund for the project. The project will also support the development of a list of local businesses already identified to help them access debt and equity finance from the DFCD and other investors. Supporting sustainable enterprises in this way is intended to provide an alternative source of income that can be used to generate revenue. Assistance to these businesses is intended to incentivize additional sustainable business development, which will contribute to reducing flood risk.

93. To address Barrier 5, the project will support the adoption of EIA and green infrastructure interventions and implementation of climate-resilient land use practices at the basin level. This will be achieved by operating capacity building workshops for officials from the National Water Resources Commission, Provincial Departments of Irrigation, Agriculture, and Forestry on the requirements of the updated procedures for the implementation of these interventions. The project will also provide technical assistance to communities in Sindh, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa and Balochistan which meet the requirements of the updated procedures for the implementation of these interventions at the basin level.

94. To address Barrier 7, the project will support the development of a list of local businesses already identified as assign specific operation, maintenance, and stewardship roles among communities and Provincial Departments of Irrigation, Agriculture, and Forestry to undertake sustainable ecosystem management activities in their catchments. The project will also provide to CBOs so that they are adequately equipped to undertake sustainable ecosystem management activities such as the establishment of buffer zones around water bodies, wetlands, lakes, and watersheds, and community plantations. Operating agreements will be established between farmer groups or other stakeholders and local government authorities for the use of improved irrigation systems, alternate crop varieties, and farm management practices to encourage participation in sustainable ecosystem management activities.

## B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The objective of the proposed project is to transform Pakistan's approach to flood and water resources management by integrating climate change adaptation into its national policies and strategies. This will help Pakistan adapt to climate change impacts such as floods and droughts. Because these climate change impacts are surpassing the country’s existing capacity, Pakistan will become increasingly vulnerable to climate change in the future in the absence of urgent adaptation.

The project will achieve transformational change in Pakistan's approach to flood and water resources management by integrating climate change adaptation into its national policies and strategies. Because these climate change impacts are surpassing the country’s existing capacity, Pakistan will become increasingly vulnerable to climate change in the future in the absence of urgent adaptation.

### 97. EBA interventions will target highly vulnerable fisherfolk and terrestrial ecosystems in the Indus Basin that play a critical role in regulating and providing water for downstream communities, and reducing flooding and landslides such as those experienced during the 2010 floods in Sindh province. The proposed EBA interventions will include the restoration of 4,125 ha of degraded wetlands in Di Khan; restoring 34 km of flow paths to improve connectivity between wetland areas and rivers; and improving water management practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 45 OF 156

## Outlot 1.1: E&E Interventions implemented to restore floodplain and wetland ecosystems

### Sub-outlot 1.1.1: A suite of E&E interventions will be implemented to restore degraded floodplains and wetland ecosystems in Di Khan, Ramak Watershed and Manchar Lake which have been damaged by past flood events and climate change.

Pakistan’s Indus Basin is one of the most vulnerable regions to climate change impacts. The Indus River and its tributaries are already facing water scarcity due to increasing demand for irrigation and urbanization. This has led to a decline in river flow, slow run-off and stabilizing soils. This will reduce the risk of flooding, landslides, and erosion, and concurrently enhance ecosystem resilience by improving soil fertility, biodiversity, and water quality. The project will restore ecosystems along riverbanks, springs, and lakes and will also help to ensure sustainable water flows and reduce sedimentation in these areas.

Furthermore, this output is expected to benefit a total of 427,300 people (310,000 direct beneficiaries and 117,300 indirect beneficiaries) through improved livelihoods, increased access to clean drinking water, and community assets such as agricultural lands, livestock, and community infrastructure, as well as increased water availability for irrigation.

### Activity 1.1.1: Restore 14,215 ha of degraded arid-eco-pluvial landscapes in Di Khan

#### Sub-activity 1.1.1.1: Block and linear plantations will be established along identified headwater torrents and catchment areas in Di Khan.

The project will establish block and linear plantations in the Upper Indus Basin, under the Department of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa through which planting stock will be sourced and plantation establishment will be executed. The project will also provide technical assistance to communities for the management and maintenance of these plantations.

For smaller sites and where available, saplings will be sourced from and planted by community-owned and managed nurseries in the catchment areas. These nurseries are located at the following locations: 1) Manchar Lake (20 ha), 2) 14,215 ha of degraded riverine ecosystems in Di Khan region.

The project will establish block and linear plantations along identified headwater torrents and catchment areas. The activities include the planting of indigenous tree species which have close association with native biodiversity and are adapted to local climatic conditions. These plantations will increase soil moisture retention, reduce velocities, and increase infiltration by enhancing soil porosity, ultimately increasing the climate resilience of sub-catchments in Di Khan region.

The project will also provide technical assistance to communities for the management and maintenance of these plantations. The project will target plant Ghaf trees (Prosopis cineraria), Farash (Tamarix aphylla), mesquite (Prosopis juliflora) and other indigenous tree species in the catchment areas.

The project will also provide technical assistance to communities for the management and maintenance of these plantations. The project will target plant Ghaf trees (Prosopis cineraria), Farash (Tamarix aphylla), mesquite (Prosopis juliflora) and other indigenous tree species in the catchment areas.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.1.2: Restore 4,785 ha of degraded riverine ecosystems in Di Khan will be restored.

Under this sub-activity, 14,215 ha of degraded riverine ecosystems in Di Khan will be restored. The area of land that is to be restored includes agricultural and agroforestry areas. Restoring this area will reduce the impacts of climate change on agriculture and increase water availability for irrigation purposes.

The project will establish block and linear plantations along identified headwater torrents and catchment areas. These plantations will include indigenous tree species which have close association with native biodiversity and are adapted to local climatic conditions. The project will also provide technical assistance to communities for the management and maintenance of these plantations.

The project will target plant Ghaf trees (Prosopis cineraria), Farash (Tamarix aphylla), mesquite (Prosopis juliflora) and other indigenous tree species in the catchment areas. The project will also provide technical assistance to communities for the management and maintenance of these plantations.

## The Manchar and Hamal watersheds

The Manchar and Hamal watersheds cover an area of **8,057 km²** and lie in the Kohistan region. Farmers usually practice rainfed agriculture on these lands, which are fed by several streams.

### The Manchar Lake project area comprises two lakes: Manchar and Hamal, and is located in Qambar Shahdadkot District in Balochistan province of Pakistan. The Manchar Lake project area has a total water holding capacity of 105 million cubic meters. Additional water from the Kulach river, small hill torrents from the Khirthar Mountain range and small canals from the Indus River are also fed into this lake.

### The Hamal Lake project area is located in the same as both project sites.

#### Sub-Activity 1.1.2: Establish 264 km of flow paths in Badin Village, Rannak, to restore the natural hydrology of connected water systems

##### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1: Establish 264 km of flow paths in Badin Village, Rannak, to restore the natural hydrology of connected water systems

107. Restoring the flow paths of the Badin channel, which is currently filled with silt, debris, and other obstructions, will allow for a more efficient use of rainfall runoff. This will increase the amount of water available in the village by reducing the risk of water directly flooding community agricultural lands, assets and infrastructure. Excavation of the flow paths will be carried out using hand tools such as pickaxes, shovels, and hoes to remove silt, debris, and other obstructions from the flow paths using machinery and labour.

108. Furthermore, the water holding capacity created by the establishment of flow paths in Badin Village is critical for the restoration of the natural hydrology features of Rannak’s natural hydrology as the only sources of freshwater for agriculture and domestic use for Badin village are the Manchar Lake and Hamal Lake. The restoration of the natural hydrology features of Rannak’s natural functioning is therefore critically, not only to flood attenuation, but also water supplies during drought periods.

##### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.2: Fill the 2nd Badin Pond in the Rannak wetlands to enhance its water holding capacity

109. The second pond in the Rannak wetlands is currently filled with silt and debris, which reduces its water holding capacity when plants die, and trees lose their leaves it is also caused by run off from surrounding land as well as the decomposition of organic matter. This sub-activity will involve excavating the pond to remove the silt and debris and remove the build-up of silt from the leeside using local labour. This sub-activity will enhance the water holding capacity created by the establishment of flow paths in Badin Village, which is critical for the restoration of Rannak’s natural hydrology as it is the only freshwater source for Badin Village. The silt from the pond will be used to strengthen the embankments of the pond at the village level.

##### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.3: Enhance and reinforce 410 m of embankments in Badin Village to reduce erosion

110. Flood embankments provide protection for people, livestock, and community assets such as crops against water damage during flood events. The construction of flood embankments is also critical for the restoration of riverbank stability by compacting clay soils and planting of native vegetation (such as Tamarix Sp). Popular euphorbia and other shrubs are planted on the top of the embankments, to prevent erosion at the toe of embankments to reduce the erosion of embankments during flooding.

#### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.4: Establish 50 km of flow paths in Parian Village to restore natural hydrology of Karot Systems

##### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.4.1: Rehabilitation of Karot System in the Khairi watershed will be an integral part of addressing the flood risk related to Khairi hillside landslides. The rehabilitation of the Karot system has been ongoing for over a decade, and over this period, the existing capacity of the system has been reduced due to sedimentation and other factors. Consequently, the rehabilitation of the Karot System will result in damage to nearby community agricultural land and the floodplain.

111. Rehabilitation of the Karot System will improve water supply through traditional community systems of waterbeds and banks, and multiple storage ponds for irrigation purposes. The rehabilitation of the Karot system will also provide a natural flow path for the Karot Systems, which will improve freshwater flows into three nearby natural ponds, which have a combined capacity of 10 million cubic meters. This will reduce flood risk during flood events, and reduce food impacts, providing a natural flood buffer, while at the same time increasing by storage water supplies for approximately 1,000 households (5,000–7,000 people) located in Parian Village.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.2-E: Encapsulate 30,000 m² of flow paths in the existing water channels of Manchar Lake to restore the natural hydrological regime and improve ecosystem services.

#### Output 1.2-G: Green infrastructure interventions established in Pakistan's Indus Basin

112. The Indus River flows directly into the Marchant Wetland through three main river channels: Aral Wah, Mor Lak, and Kharar. These channels are characterized by high sediment loads, which cause severe flooding during monsoon seasons, leading to debris and sediment, leaving local communities vulnerable to drought, and the risk of flooding of surrounding areas is increased.

113. To complement Output 1.1’s EDA interventions, green infrastructure interventions will be established in Pakistan's Indus Basin. Green infrastructure is considered as interventions that are not naturally occurring or existing in an ecosystem but are designed using natural processes to provide a range of environmental benefits and services. The main components of these interventions will include the establishment of flood protection embankments, flood dispersal embankments, gabion bunds, and recharging basins.

114. These interventions are expected to benefit a total of 462,000 people (311,300 direct beneficiaries and 151,300 indirect beneficiaries) by reducing flood impacts on nearby communities and ecosystems through reducing soil erosion, sedimentation, and flooding in the Indus Basin. Communities living near the project area will benefit from the greening of their surroundings, increased water availability, and restoration of natural ecosystem functions essential to the livelihoods of local communities.

115. The interventions are expected to provide benefits for nearby communities by reducing soil erosion, sedimentation, and flooding in the Indus Basin. Communities living near the project area will benefit from the greening of their surroundings, increased water availability, and restoration of natural ecosystem functions essential to the livelihoods of local communities.

### Activity 1.2-I: Design and implement 127 green infrastructure interventions for flood risk reduction

#### Output 1.2-J: Green infrastructure interventions established in Pakistan's Indus Basin

116. The design and implementation of these interventions will be carried out by a specialized local engineering firm, WWP Pakistan. This activity involves providing funding to the Provincial Irrigation Departments (PID) for the design and construction of green infrastructure under the framework of WWF Pakistan’s Green Infrastructure project. The interventions include:

- i) 127 dispersal embankments in the D.I. Khan, Ramak, and Chakar Lehri Watersheds.
- ii) 4 gabion bunds across the D.I. Khan, Ramak, and Chakar Lehri Watersheds.
- iii) 7 dispersal embankments in the D.I. Khan, Ramak, and Chakar Lehri Watersheds.
- iv) 127 recharging basins across the D.I. Khan, Manchar, and Chakar Lehri Watersheds.

The type, location, quantity, and quality of these interventions will be determined by a specialized local engineering firm, WWP Pakistan.

## Sub-Activity: Design the green infrastructure measures

### 1.2.1.1: Design the green infrastructure measures

Under this sub-activity, the green infrastructure interventions under Sub-activities 1.2.1.2–1.2.1.7 will be designed and implemented based on the feasibility study (Annex 2). Specifically, these assessments determine the areas where such measures are most needed to reduce flood impacts in the Indus Basin.

### 1.2.1.2: Build flood protection embankments

In some locations, existing flood protection embankments fail to provide adequate protection for community assets and homes, particularly as air flood events continue to intensify under conditions of climate change. Six (6) flood-protection embankments will be constructed in the Indus Basin using locally available construction materials, including clay-silt, earth, stones, and gravel.

The embankments will retain floodwaters moving at least 10 km/hour and protect against flooding from a 50-year recurrence interval event. The embankments will also provide additional protection for communities in the Indus Basin against damage. Grass will be planted, and other supplementary structures established on the embankments to provide additional protection.

On the flood embankments, the riverbank slope will be armoured with stone pitching or riprap (loose stone).

### Sub-Activity 3.1.3. Build / Flood dispersal embankments

1. **Flood Dispersal Embankments:** These are low-profile embankments, earth-clay bunds, levees, watercourses, water channels, and other structures that will be built to intercept clear surface water runoff upstream of an area at risk of flooding, transport it through the floodplain, and then disperse it into a more diffuse flow path. The dispersal embankments will be built with local materials such as earth, clayey-silt, stone foam stones, and gravel in accordance with the guidelines provided by the GCF (see Annexure 23). The embankments will be armoured with stone placed in the form of riprap or stone pitching. The crest of the flood dispersal embankment will be designed to allow for a minimum water depth of 0.5 meters at the crest, which is sufficient to protect them against extreme flood flows, which are more likely to occur under future climate scenarios. The diverted path will be crossed through existing channels and/or non-perennial streams so local farmers can use the now-controlled floodwaters for irrigation purposes.
