**Sub-A-Section B-B.1: Gabion Bund**

A gabion bond is a wire cage filled with stones, boulders, rocks, or sand/silt. It is often used to stabilize and protect river banks against erosion. The use of gabions in the design of flood dispersal embankments enhances the flow control; the stepped design enhances the rate of energy dissipation in the channel. The interlocking of the gabion bonds with each other will assist in resisting hydraulic forces. Gabion bunds will be established in the catchments of Manchar (2), D.I. Khan (34, Ravi 15) and Chakwal (20). These will be designed to act as a spillway, sand diverting floodwaters (Figure 24). The gabion bond will be designed to act as a spillway, sand diverting floodwaters.

## Sub-Activity 1.2.1.5: Build 42 small retention areas.

### Sub-Activity 1.2.1.6: Build 9 larger retention areas

Retention areas are ponds where excess river discharge or floodwater is stored (Figure 25). Generally, they are constructed in the lower reaches of the river channel during periods of high discharge or flooding; the retention areas are filled which reduces the peak flow, increases infiltration, and stores water for domestic and/or agricultural purposes. During low flow conditions, the retained water can be released from the retention area to reduce downstream flood risk.

The locations where these ponds were built are those where they would have been constructed had there not been a river discharge event. For example, in areas with high groundwater levels, where there is no need for water harvesting and flood attenuation, retention areas will not be established. For other areas, the location of the retention area was determined by the topography of the site. The locations were also selected to take advantage of natural depressions and existing contours with some augmentation depending on the local conditions.

The retention areas are designed in such a way that they do not impact the river flow or groundwater. The retention areas will be established using local materials (such as stones and gravel), in natural depressions, and will have an outlet to allow for controlled release of water into the river channel and/or adjacent ecosystems – reducing any risk of environmental impact.

The detailed design of the retention areas (Sub-activity 1.2.1.7) is described below. The locations of the nine larger retention areas to be established include: 34 in Di Khan, 6 in Manchar Watershed, and 2 in Chakar Lehri Watersheds.

The design and functioning of the large retention areas are similar to those of the small retention areas.

## Sub-Activity 1.2.1.7: Build 15 recharge basins

15 recharge basins will be designed to slow down floodwaters and recharge shallow aquifers (Figure 26). They contribute to adaptation by drying and slowing floodwater flows and reducing the risk of flooding downstream. In addition, they help to cool surface water and increase its sediment layer, providing a constant water supply through riparian zones. This will also lead to an increase in local vegetation which provides habitat for wildlife and biodiversity can benefit as well. This will also lead to an increase in local vegetation which provides habitat for wildlife and biodiversity can benefit as well. This will also lead to an increase in local vegetation which provides habitat for wildlife and biodiversity can benefit as well.

Output 1.3: Strengthened community-based natural resource management to support water supply and flood attenuation services provided by EBA interventions implemented in floodplains and wetlands:

* 125. Under this activity, 4,000 ha of degraded land will be restored and rehabilitated, as well as an additional 4,000 ha under Component I) will be placed under improved community-based natural resource management systems (CBNRS). This will include the strengthening of existing CBNRS and green infrastructure interventions by the beneficiary communities, as well as strengthen the health of ecosystems. This will lead to increased water supply for domestic use, livestock watering, irrigation, and other ecosystem services at the household level in Pakistan, taking the risk for sustainably managing these resources at the household level—to create community-based natural resource management systems (CBNRS) that are sustainable at the local government level governments on the use and management of ecosystems including the EBA and green infrastructure interventions. This will be achieved by strengthening existing CBNRS and green infrastructure interventions, as well as by creating new ones in areas where they do not exist. The project will support the communities to adopt EBA and green infrastructure interventions through Watershed Watch and Ward Systems under CBOs to promote community-based natural resource management systems (CBNRS) and the relevant provincial government departments in the field of agriculture, forestry, water resources, etc., W-8 WGs and 8 Wards and Ward (Waan) Systems (Figure 28).

Activity 1.3.1: Strengthen the capacity of CBOs to adopt EBA and green infrastructure interventions and undertake community-based natural resource management systems:

Under this activity, CBOs will be strengthened to support the adoption of EBA and green infrastructure interventions in floodplains and wetlands through Watershed Watch and Ward Systems (W-8 WGs and 8 Wards) under their respective CBOs and the Provincial Forest Departments. These plans will include provisions for ecosystem services such as water supply, flood attenuation, soil conservation, and biodiversity conservation. Operational agreements terms of partnership between CBOs and the provincial forest departments will be developed to ensure the operation, maintenance, and stewardship roles among communities and Provincial Departments of Forestry of Sindh. The project will support the communities to adopt EBA and green infrastructure interventions through Watershed Watch and Ward Systems under CBOs to promote community-based natural resource management systems (CBNRS) in floodplains and wetlands. The Indus Evergreen Community Livelihoods Project (IECLP) formed eight WUGs to manage irrigation networks. This sub-activity will build on the OPG’s initial work by establishing and strengthening 100% of the WUGs in the project area, with a focus on capacity building for WUG members through training on organizational management and financial planning.

### Lack of representation from all ethnic groups
* **Lack of exposure to development projects** at higher level of authority and policy.
* **Working with CBOs to ensure better ethnic and gender representation in WUGs.**
* **Train these CBOs in organizational management, financial planning, and capacity development to secure and execute small grants from local donors.**

### Sub-Activity 3.1.2: Organize WUGs under CBOs and build their capacity for improved water resources management.
The WUGs will be established under the umbrella of local CBOs to ensure equitable access to water resources. CBOs will nominate members of WUGs from among themselves, with a focus on ensuring representation from all ethnic groups.

### Sub-Activity 3.1.3: Establish community-based water and land systems under CBOs to protect the project’s EDA and green infrastructure interventions from degradation.
The WUGs will be established under the umbrella of local CBOs to improve governance at the local level and formalize local water resource associations. The WUGs will play a key role in propagating effective water utilization and reduce waste consumption at domestic and farm levels.

131. The water users of irrigation water will be the cultivators of lands, owners of private farms, lease-holding farmers, sharecroppers, and tenants. The members of the WUGs will be selected from among these water users. The chairperson of the committee will be selected from the CBOs of the respective project sites. The head of the committee will be a chairman of the WUG. The WUGs will be established under the umbrella of local CBOs and will work in close collaboration with local community members.

The WUGs will undertake the following tasks:
* **Contribute towards the analysis of general physical conditions of the local villages, including assessment of water resources and land use.**
* **Ensure equitable distribution of drinking and irrigation water among water users:**
    * Ensure that all water users have access to clean drinking water.
    * Resolve disputes that may arise on the usage or consumption of water among water users at a local level; ensure that all water users have access to irrigation channels and drinking water supply from the members of the water user groups;
* **Provide technical support for the maintenance of irrigation canals and drinking water supply.**
    * Provide the necessary training to WUG members on water management.
    * Support the Provincial Irrigation Department with regular Operation and Maintenance (O&M) and facilitate their monitoring visits.

132. WUG participation within communities will be incentivized by linking incremental project support with WUG development activities. The CBOs will be responsible for establishing a pay-per-use system in close consultation with local communities and project team to cover operation and maintenance cost now the project is completed.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 59 OF 156

#### Community Based W&I systems are community managed enforcement networks of individuals responsible for and taking ownership of it. In this context, the improved management of ecosystems on which they depend. These W&I systems will be supported by a series of interventions that will include: i) Ecosystem protection and restoration; ii) Green infrastructure interventions including their required operation and maintenance. This will be implemented in collaboration with the Pakistan Forest Research Institute (FRI), the National Forest Policy, and the Department of Forests.

**Activity 1.3.2: Strengthen communities’ knowledge and awareness of climate change impacts and the benefits of the project’s Ecosystem protection and restoration interventions**

144. Under this activity, community knowledge and awareness of climate change impacts and the benefits of the project’s Ecosystem protection and restoration interventions will be strengthened through a multi-stakeholder strategy. The audience will include members of vulnerable communities, particularly women and children, students and teachers in schools, religious leaders, local government officials, and other stakeholders. This activity will:
- i) educating communities, students, tourists and other project stakeholders on the impacts of climate change, particularly drought and floods, and how these are being addressed by the project’s Ecosystem protection and restoration interventions; ii) implementing EDA green infrastructure and efficient community-based natural resource management; iii) informing research findings to inform policy development and implementation at national level; iv) documenting and communicating the successes and impacts of the project interventions to build a case for scaling up EDA activities in Pakistan, including through the use of social media (e.g. Facebook, Twitter), photography, short documentaries, press releases, news packages (electronic), blogs, signboards, brochures, minutes of meetings, etc.; v) providing training and capacity building on climate change adaptation to communities for community members. This project activity will be executed by WWF-Pakistan.

**Sub-Activity 1.3.2.1: Consult with tribal elders to support the sensitization of the project’s adaptation interventions**

145. Consulting with tribal elders to support the sensitization of the project’s adaptation interventions in beneficiary communities is a key component of this activity. The objective of these consultations will be to sensitize and build capacity among tribal elders on climate change impacts, particularly drought and floods, and how they are being addressed by the project’s Ecosystem protection and restoration interventions. Consultations will be held with tribal elders to: i) promote dialogue among communities on the risks and impacts of climate change; ii) share information about the project’s Ecosystem protection and restoration interventions in order to sensitize the project’s EDA green infrastructure interventions among local communities, iii) garner support for the project’s EDA green infrastructure interventions from local community members at the project’s EDA and green infrastructure interventions at the local level.

**Sub-Activity 1.3.2.2: Develop and implement awareness-raising campaigns to increase knowledge and awareness of climate change impacts and the benefits of the project’s EDA and green infrastructure interventions**

146. Under this sub-activity, awareness-raising campaigns will be developed and implemented for target communities to increase their knowledge and awareness of climate change impacts and the benefits of the project’s EDA and green infrastructure interventions. The campaign will focus on areas that are particularly relevant to the affected areas across parts of Balochistan. It will include a mix of education and communication activities, such as: i) raising awareness about the impacts of climate change; ii) promoting improved natural resource management for food and drought risk reduction; iii) raising awareness about the impacts of climate change on the project’s EDA green infrastructure interventions and how they have helped communities adapt to these changes. The campaign will be implemented through a variety of channels, including meetings with communities, including signboards, newspaper publications, local radio stations, short videos, photography and other digital media.

**Outcome 2: Enabling an enabling environment for climate action that has new procedures for implementing EDA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan**

**Output 2.1: Evidence base to support the adoption of EDA and green infrastructure interventions by the Government of Pakistan.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.3: Conduct an economic valuation of the benefits of E&Ia green infrastructure on ecosystems and their flood attenuation and water resource-related services

140\. Under this sub-activity, WWF-Pakistan will develop a framework for ecosystem services and benefits that will be conducted by WWF-Pakistan under this sub-activity using tools such as Integrated Valuation of Ecosystem Services and Trade-offs (inVEST) to assess the economic value of these services and benefits in relation to water resources management under future climate change conditions.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.4: Identify additional opportunities and sites at the national level across Pakistan for dealing with and managing flood risks, droughts and water resource-related services

144\. Once the economic value and benefits of the project’s E&Ia green infrastructure interventions have been assessed under sub-activity 2.1.3, WWF-Pakistan will identify additional opportunities and sites at the national level for dealing with and replicating these solutions across other suitable areas in Pakistan that are vulnerable to floods and droughts.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.5: Collate and store the data and information generated under sub-activities 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.1.4

The data and information generated by sub-activities 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.1.4 will be collated, managed and stored by WWF-Pakistan under this sub-activity. The data and information generated from the project’s M&E activities will be transferred to the MoCC and the FFC (under MW&I) and the Provincial Irrigation Departments as service for evidence for future flood and water resource-related services management in Pakistan.

Initially managed by WWF-Pakistan during Years 1-5 of the project, it will still be made readily available to project partners and other stakeholders at all times. This sub-activity will also provide information sharing for addressing increasingly severe floods and droughts caused by climate change across similar contexts nationally and internationally.

### Activity 2.1.6: Develop and disseminate an evidence report to national and provincial government agencies, as well as relevant decision-makers

Under Activity 2.1.6, the data and information generated under Activity 2.1.5 will be collated and synthesized into a comprehensive evidence report that will provide evidence of the project’s E&Ia green infrastructure interventions’ potential economic value and environmental and social benefits. This report will serve two main purposes:

First, it will be used to inform policy makers and decision-makers in Pakistan on how to better manage flood risks and water resources management on the proper execution of E&Ia and green infrastructure interventions to reduce the impact of floods and droughts.

Secondly, this evidence report will provide a knowledge product and decision-making tool for policymakers and planners in Pakistan to use in other decision-making processes. This evidence report will be disseminated through various channels such as:

- The MoCC, as well as other relevant national and provincial stakeholders.
- The MACC, as the NAO and chair of the FFC, will also receive this evidence report.

The MACC will use this evidence to inform its work on flood risk management and water resource-related services in Pakistan. Moreover, the MACC has limited data on the cost-effectiveness of E&Ia and green infrastructure interventions; the economic value underpinning these interventions is not well understood by policymakers or planners. The evidence report generated by these interventions and serve as a key reference for policymakers in future flood and water resources management.

147\. WWF-Pakistan will work with FFC to disseminate this evidence report to the MoCC, the Provincial Irrigation Departments, and other relevant provincial government agencies, as well as relevant national stakeholders. This will be done through meetings with these institutions, as well as other relevant national and provincial stakeholders.

The MACC, as the NAO and chair of the FFC, will also receive this evidence report from WWF-Pakistan to inform its work on flood risk management and water resource-related services in Pakistan. The MACC has limited data on the cost-effectiveness of E&Ia and green infrastructure interventions; the economic value underpinning these interventions is not well understood by policymakers or planners.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.7: Prepare an evidence report on the use and benefits of E&Ia and green infrastructure interventions

Under this sub-activity, WWF-Pakistan will lead the preparation of an evidence report on the use and benefits of E&Ia and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan. This evidence report will be based on the information informed by the economic valuation undertaken under Activity 2.1.1 and contain step-wise instructions for how to mainstream these interventions into national policies, regulations, and practices for E&Ia and green infrastructure.

The evidence report will be used for the development of updated procedures for Pakistan’s key regulations

Output 2.2: Updated procedures for implementing EDA and green infrastructure in Pakistan.

150. The National Water Policy, the National Adaptation Plan and four Provincial Adaptation Plans for the provinces of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Punjab, Balochistan and Sind do not currently include EDA and green infrastructure as a priority area. However, given that these are critical components in addressing climate change impacts and high capital expenditure and operational costs.

151. The Implementation Framework of the National Water Policy, National Adaptation Plan and four Provincial Adaptation Plans for the provinces of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Punjab, Balochistan and Sind do not currently include EDA and green infrastructure as a priority area. However, given that these are critical components in addressing climate change impacts and high capital expenditure and operational costs.

153. In addition to EDA and green infrastructure interventions not being considered in the Implementation Framework of the National Water Policy, National Adaptation Plan and four Provincial Adaptation Plans for the provinces of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Punjab, Balochistan and Sind, there is a unique opportunity for the project to also integrate the use of EDA and green infrastructure interventions into the implementation framework of the National Water Policy, National Adaptation Plan and four Provincial Adaptation Plans for the provinces of Sindh, Khuzdar, Bolan and Chagai. The updated procedures will be developed in consultation with the relevant provincial governments.

Output 2.2 will address these gaps through the creation and adoption of updated procedures for the implementation framework of the National Water Policy, the National Adaptation Plan and four Provincial Adaptation Plans for Sindh, Khuzdar, Bolan and Chagai on the proper application of EDA and green infrastructure to reduce the impacts of floods and droughts. To reduce the impacts of floods and droughts in Pakistan are proposed to be developed by the National Working Group.

## Sub-Activity 2.1.1: Establish a National Working Group to develop and adopt updated procedures for using EBA and green infrastructure interventions in flood and water resources management in Pakistan.

The National Working Group will be responsible for the development of updated procedures for Pakistan's key regulations and policies for flood and water resources management — namely the implementation of the EBA and green infrastructure interventions. The National Working Group will consist of representatives from Sindh, Khushab, Balochistan, and Punjab provinces—on the proper execution of EBA and green infrastructure interventions in flood and water resources management. These updated procedures should align with current national and provincial climate change planning frameworks. The National Working Group

### Output 3.2: Improved climate-resilient livelihoods among vulnerable target communities

175. Because the agricultural sector is one of the most vulnerable to the impacts of floods and droughts in Pakistan, improving the resilience of small-scale farmers and their families living in the Indus Basin that depend on agriculture for their livelihoods.

#### 3.2.1: Modernize extension services to address climate change

The project will involve designing and delivering a new climate-resilient extension service curriculum; training the department's staff in the use of drought-resistant crop varieties, soil management practices responsive to changing climate conditions, and other climate-resilient methods; and providing technical assistance to vulnerable farmers to adapt their practices in response to increasingly severe and frequent floods and droughts. The curriculum will include hands-on training on: (i) climate-resilient irrigation methods; (ii) the use of drought-resistant crop varieties; (iii) climate-resilient soil management methods, and (iv) other relevant topics.

176. WFP Pakistan will assist the Provincial Departments of Agriculture to modernize their extension service capacity by providing training in: (i) the use of drought-resistant crop varieties; (ii) soil management practices responsive to changing climate conditions; and (iii) other climate-resilient methods. This will allow the Provincial Agriculture Departments in the future to deliver more effective and efficient extension services that are better equipped to address the significant challenges faced by small-scale farmers.

177. Under this activity, WFP Pakistan will partner with the Provincial Departments of Agriculture to enhance their capacity for delivering climate-resilient extension services to vulnerable farmers living in the Indus Basin. This will involve designing and delivering a new climate-resilient extension service curriculum; training the department's staff in the use of drought-resistant crop varieties, soil management practices responsive to changing climate conditions, and other climate-resilient methods; and providing technical assistance to vulnerable farmers to adapt their practices in response to increasingly severe and frequent floods and droughts. The curriculum will include hands-on training on: (i) climate-resilient irrigation methods; (ii) the use of drought-resistant crop varieties; (iii) climate-resilient soil management methods, and (iv) other relevant topics.

**Sub-Activity 3.2.1.2: Strengthen the capacity of small-scale farmers and people dependent on agriculture on climate-resilient agricultural practices**

### Activity 3.2.1 will use a Farmer Field School (FFS) approach in delivering this new curriculum, whereby training sessions will be conducted during the dry season. Lessons learned from experiences in Pakistan and elsewhere indicate that such an approach leads to higher adoption rates and sustained behaviour change both within government agencies and participating farmers. The FFSs will be organized at least once a year for each of the seven-year implementation period. The FFSs will focus on the adoption of drought-resistant seed varieties into current crop farming practices as well as improved farming and soil management techniques, such as conservation agriculture (CA) and integrated water resource management (IWRM). These cohorts will be assembled during execution of the project following calls for participation from local farmers by WFP Pakistan. The FFSs will be conducted in collaboration with the provincial departments to ensure that the content aligns with strategies to adapt the climate-resilient agriculture practices covered by the curriculum.

#### Sub-Activity 3.2.1.2.1: Strengthen the capacity of small-scale farmers and people dependent on agriculture on climate-resilient agricultural practices

The Provincial Agriculture Departments, via their extension services, will be responsible for organizing FFSs in each province. The provincial departments will provide the necessary materials and equipment for the FFS trainings, including seeds, gene bags, sags, low-cost material (e.g., plastic sheets), and other inputs as required by the curriculum. The training modules and workshops for the FFSs will be shared with the Pakistan Agriculture Research Council (PARC) and community representatives to gather input before they are finalized.

The FFSs will be conducted in collaboration with the provincial departments, which will provide the necessary materials and equipment for these trainings including venues, training materials, supplies, equipment and materials, and excursions for training exercises. The FFSs will be organized at least once a year for each of the seven-year implementation period.

#### Sub-Activity 3.2.1.2.2: Strengthen the capacity of small-scale farmers and people dependent on agriculture on climate-resilient agricultural practices

Eighty-five extension agents and Department of Agriculture staff (10% women) will be trained in each province. The training curriculum will be implemented over 80 days in Year 2 of the Project, with periodic scheduled reresher trainings to ensure that the knowledge is retained by the participants. The training modules and workshops for the FFSs will be shared with the Pakistan Agriculture Research Council (PARC) and community representatives to gather input before they are finalized.

The training curriculum will be delivered in 170 days per year for the first three years of the Project, and 25 learning sessions per year thereafter. ### Sub-Activity 3.2.1: Introduce drought-resistant crop varieties

#### 184. Under this sub-activity, farmers currently heavily reliant on water-intensive crops such as rice and maize exposing them to an increasing risk of crop failure and lower yields due to climate change-related diminishing water availability will be introduced to drought-resistant crop varieties proven successful in these areas, including barley and wheat which are more resilient to drought conditions. The introduction of these drought-resistant crop varieties along with soil management practices and enhanced seeds is predicated on the economic case to be demonstrated through the farmers' plots that the new crops can provide a better return than existing crops. This will lead to increased water productivity, reduced irrigation costs and material costs, and increases in crop yields after which farmers would voluntarily purchase these drought-resistant varieties. The Project will work with the Government of Pakistan to ensure this approach is already in practice in the Provincial Agriculture Departments extending extant programs and is essential to ensure sustainability in the uptake of new farming practices limiting dependence on public or donor funds.

### Sub-Activity 3.2.1: Improve the climate resilience of soil management practices

#### 185. Under this sub-activity, water conservation techniques for improved soil conservation and productivity will be introduced to farmers in the Indus Basin. Sustainable soil management practices will include the use of bio-fertilizers, intercropping, crop rotation, minimum tillage, mulching, and use of compost. These methods will reduce production costs as well as improve soil health. The Project will work with the Government of Pakistan to ensure this approach is already in practice in the Provincial Agriculture Departments extending extant programs.

### Sub-Activity 3.2.1: Provide water storage tanks to vulnerable agricultural communities

#### 186. Under this sub-activity, rainwater storage tanks will be procured and installed, accompanied by training on water management practices for improved soil conservation and productivity in the Indus Basin. The WUCs in water scarce areas of the Indus Basin. The storage tanks will be used for agricultural purposes and livestock watering. It is anticipated that each tank will provide 100 cubic meters (m3) of water to a community, allowing the communities to ensure these meet local needs and enhance equitable access. The anticipated storage capacity of each tank is sufficient to cater to the water requirements of up to 5,152 smallholder farmers and their families. Communities which receive the water storage tanks will be identified based on vulnerability assessments conducted by the Project in collaboration with the WUCs. Each community will have a central location allowing for equal access for community members and willingness and ability of communities to assume responsibility for maintenance and operation of the tank. The Project will work with the Government of Pakistan to ensure this approach is already in practice in the Provincial Agriculture Departments extending extant programs.

### Accredited Entity

187. WWF World Fund Inc., (WWF-US) will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the project and will provide GCF Proceeds to the Executing Entity (EE), in accordance with the FAA to be entered with the GCF. The AE will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented according to the approved project design, monitoring and supervision (through reporting, audits, and annual site visits) and review and approval of the EE's annual work plan and budget. WWF-US will also provide technical assistance to the EE in accessing the relevant co-financing resources from USDAC and TCFD to the EE, providing support, guidance and backstopping to the EE for project design and implementation, including preparation of the Project Design Document (PDD), Proceeds to the EE, in line with the approved Activity Module Agreement (AMA) and anticipated Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) with the CDP. WWF-US will also serve as the AE for FF0802 Brunei for which was launched in 2018.

### Executing Entity (EE)

188. The project will be executed by one Executing Entity (EE), WWF-Pakistan. The EE will be responsible for project execution, among others, including the management of grants, recipients of goods or services, and procured partners and sub-contractors relating to the project.

189. WWF-Pakistan is a private non-profit organization under the international WWF Network and separate legal entity to WWF-US, will serve as an EE for the project. With its Head Office in Lahore, 6 regional offices, and 30 provincial offices across Pakistan, WWF-Pakistan has been working with communities and governments on significant experience working with the Government of Pakistan (GoP) in policy and design and reform, including the Water Sector Reform Program (WSRP), the National Action Plan for Climate Change (NAPCC), Drinking Water Policy. WWF-Pakistan also has a long track record implementing E&As projects in Pakistan's landscapes most vulnerable to climate change including 1) restoring and protecting, through community


---
**Page 77**
---

management arrangements, mangroves ecosystems in the Indus Delta, 2) improving watershed management and enhancing agricultural livelihoods to better withstand climate change in sub-catchments of Kharanpur District, 3) the development of a national wetland restoration strategy for Pakistan, and 4) the establishment of a National Wetlands Fund as an evidence base to support the formulation of national and sub-national wetland regulations and plans under the Pakistan Forest Policy. The project will be implemented in collaboration with WWF-Indonesia, WWF-Malaysia, Floodplains, and Indus Delta Ecoregion to identify flood damages and formulate plans for ecological restoration as part of the overall response to climate change. These include: (i) identification of priority areas for wetland rehydration, reforestation, natural regeneration through protection, construction of flood protection spurans, and restriction of livestock grazing.

190. WWF-Pakistan will be responsible for the execution of project Activities 1.1.1, 1.2.1, 1.3.1, 1.3.2, 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.2.1, 2.3.1, 3.1.1, 3.1.2, managing all procured parties and their activities, reporting to the AE, and ensuring compliance with the project implementation plan, including the procurement of goods and services as per the Terms of Reference and Fund value impacts, as described in Section B.9. WWF-Pakistan will hold GCF Projects, a new and emerging role for WWF Pakistan, and will be responsible for all aspects of project management in collaboration with WWF-US (as the AE) in respect of the GCF Projects, grant agreements with each grants, consulting agreements, procurement of goods and services, including but not limited to, technical assistance, training, and other services, such as technical assistance for the project activities, retaining responsibility for any delegated authority over the financial transactions for the project activities. WWF Pakistan will be responsible for ensuring that Pakistan's capacity to execute the project (see Annex 9 for details). This assessment determined WWF-Pakistan to have the necessary capacity to implement this project.

191. WWF-Pakistan (EE) will establish and host the Project Management Unit (PMU) within WWF-Pakistan’s office in the Federal Capital, Islamabad (Figure 28). The PMU (through the project Manager) will report to the AE. The PMU will be responsible for all aspects of project management as described above under Section B.9.1 and TCOs described below. The Project Director will be a full-time staff member of WWF-Pakistan providing 50% of LOE to the project, including responsibility for overall project implementation, coordination with the AE, and liaison with government staff where appropriate and necessary during project execution. The PMU will be responsible for overall project management in collaboration with WWF-US (as the AE) and will be responsible for all aspects of project management as described above under Section B.9.1 and TCOs described below.

The PMU will consist of a Project Director, a Program Manager, a Contract Management, or M&E Manager, an Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Manager, a Gender Manager, and a Communications Officer. 193. There will be four Provincial Implementation Units (PIUs) hosted within WWF Pakistan's regional offices located in the following locations: i) Khushab District (Northwest Pakistan); ii) South and Balochistan; iii) Manchar Lake, Thatta District (Sindh); iv) Chakwal District (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa). These units will coordinate the implementation of project activities under Components 1.2 and 3 guided by the Provincial Oversight Committees (POCs) at provincial level. The PIUs are staffed with full-time WWF Pakistan staff working on the project, each reporting to the PM in Islamabad. The Regional Managers will be responsible for: i) coordinating the implementation of project activities under Component 1.2 with provincial line agencies and managing key service providers on the implementation of project activities; ii) providing technical guidance and oversight to PIUs on the implementation of project activities; iii) ensuring compliance with policies and standards, in collaboration with the Provincial Oversight Committees (POCs); iv) undertaking monitoring and evaluation of project activities at the provincial level; v) coordinating the submission of project reports via the PMU and project partners; and vi) conducting of TDRs for provincial and field level staff and implementing partners.

The PCG will support the PMU to implement the project’s exit strategy across all levels of government, disseminate key lessons from the project, and maintain communication between the proposed project and key government line departments. This will be done through a series of workshops with provincial governments on projects in the Indus Basin. PSC 198: Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management (CRAM) Project; and PSC 200: Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management (CRAM) Project (led by UNDP), respectively. A quorum of at least 50% of the members shall be required with mandatory participation from the PMU, the PCG, and the PSC. The PCG will also provide support to the PMU in implementing the project on and on significant project developments from the PM. Members of the PSC will join the AE during annual site visits and other relevant meetings as per the requirements of the project.

197. The PSC for the project will include a representative from each of the following:

a. Ministry of Climate Change (MoCC) (Chair & GCF NDA);
b. National Water and Power Commission;
c. National Disaster Risk Management Fund (NDRMF);
d. National Agriculture Research Centre;
e. Ministry of Planning Development and Special Initiatives (MPDPSI);
f. Provincial Government Line Departments (to include one from each province: Sindh, Balochistan and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa); and
g. External Advisors (EA);
h. Community Representatives
i. Academic Institutions
j. NGOs

198. Serving under the PSC and to ensure engagement at the provincial level and key government line departments. Provincial Coordination Committees (PCCs) will be established in each of the four provinces. The PCCs will be chaired by the Provincial Planning and Development Department who will also represent their Province in the PCG and include representatives from the following:

(1) Irrigation, (2) Forest, (2) Wildlife, (4) Fisheries, (5) Environment, and (6) Agriculture.

199. The PCCs will: a) assist the PMU with the development of annual work plans and provide technical support for implementation of project activities at provincial level; b) coordinate with the provincial line departments; c) support the PMU with formalizing service based contracts with service providers who will implement project activities in their respective provinces; d) monitor progress on project activities at provincial level; e) coordinate with project M&E staff to track project progress at provincial level against the approved work plans, and f) provide feedback to the PMU regarding any issues or challenges encountered. The PCCs will also appoint a focal person who will be responsible for consulting with key experts as and when required for knowledge sharing and peer assistance in implementing project activities at provincial level. This information will be shared with the PCG. Recommendations are duly considered and incorporated at the national level.

200. Under the NPC, the Provincial Focus Points will serve as the points of contact from the provincial levels for the implementation of the project’s adaptation interventions and mainstreaming EBA and green infrastructure interventions into Pakistan’s development plans and policies. The NPC will be chaired by the focal person appointed from each department during project start-up. The PCCs and the respective focal points will provide technical support to the NPC in implementing project activities at provincial level.

#### Ministry of Climate Change is the National Designated Authority (NDA) for Pakistan. It holds the national focal point for climate change and coordinates with other relevant ministries, including MoCC and NDC commitments. The environmental division of the ministry is led by the Director General (EnvCC), and its responsibilities include: developing policies and strategies on climate change; addressing national, provincial, regional and international issues; and making it relating to sustainable development, water and sanitation, energy, agriculture, forestry, disaster management, etc., in line with the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The MoCC will be a second party and is a recipient of goals and services from the project. The MoCC will lead government efforts to develop and adopt updated procedures for Pakistan's Implementation Framework of the Kyoto Protocol, National Adaptation Plan and Four Prioritization Adaptations Plan on the execution of EIA and green infrastructure.

#### Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR) Federal Floor Commission (FFC): The FFC is an Executive Department which has been established to provide technical advice and support for water resources management. The FFC provides technical advisory services at national and sub-national levels – particularly to the MoWR and Provincial Irrigation Departments. The FFC also provides technical assistance in areas such as flood safety, irrigation, drainage, and hydrology. FFC has over thirty-seven (37) technical officers who have experience in engineering, water resources management, and environmental science. The FFC will provide technical support to the Provincial Irrigation Departments on their activities, execution of project Activity 12.1.

#### Provincial governments are responsible for the administration of Pakistan's four Provinces. Governance over natural resources is a provincial responsibility; however, the federal government has established agencies that are largely targeted for the management of ecosystems and addressing climate change threats within their respective jurisdictions. The provinces have developed policies and strategies to address these national policies and regulations. Under the project: The Provincial Departments of Forestry will be procured parties for Activities 12.3 and 12.4. The provincial departments of agriculture will be procured parties for Activities 12.5, 12.6, and 12.7. The provincial departments of agriculture will be procured parties for the projects and budgets considering their direct role in planning and implementation across key sectors. Just as recently mentioned, the FFC will provide technical support to the Provincial Irrigation Departments on their activities.

The current level of adaptation funding indicates that projected global adaptation needs are to be significantly greater than current levels of funding. For example, Pakistan’s total annual adaptation cost is estimated at US$10 billion per year. Annual adaptation and mitigation to climate change for Pakistan were estimated to range annually from more USD 25 billion to over USD 40 billion by 2030. The average annual cost of climate change adaptation needs in Pakistan range from USD 7 to 14 billion per annum on current exchange rates |

Pakistan remains a resource-constrained economy, dependent on foreign financing in the shape of loans, borrowings and grants for its development projects, including those related to infrastructure projects and funding is tied to post-disaster scenarios, including for drought and flood recovery. For instance, Pakistan received US$56 million from the World Bank’s Central Emergency Response Fund and the Central Emergency Response Fund and Pakistan’s Humanitarian Fund for largest post-disaster relief across medium-term development priorities. The total amount of aid provided by international donors to Pakistan has been provided to Pakistan over the last two decades from donors such as USAID, the European Commission, the World Bank and other bilateral agencies |
| 211. As flooding has historically been a major observed and projected climate hazard in Pakistan, significant investment is required to meet the country’s flood risk management needs. For example, the National Flood Protection Strategy (NFPS) estimates that US$393 million would be needed for flood risk reduction projects. However, due to insufficient GfF funds, foreign financing is needed to cover these costs, particularly in light of Pakistan’s limited capacity to mobilize resources from its own budget and reliance on funding than hard infrastructure alternatives. According to the Federal Flood Commission’s 2021 Annual Flood Report, the total amount of aid provided by international donors to Pakistan over the last two decades has been US$56 million, but only USD 2.8 million was released for use in funding a leasing gap of USD 32.2 million. The NFPS estimates that US$407 million would be needed annually from 2019-2024 to limit the amount of funding available. Approximately PKR 10 billion (approximately USD 50 million) has been allocated for flood risk management in Pakistan’s National Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Strategy (NDRMS) for the year from the year before likely brought by the economic downturn brought on largely by COVID-19 and only widening the gap between the country’s needs and available resources. The NFPS estimates that US$30 billion would be needed to climate exposure to climate disasters |
| 212. In addition, under the NDRMS, the NDPRM have been established to fund disaster risk reduction projects. With a total fund size of USD $393.5 million (mainly international donor funding), 50% of NDPRM’s current grant portfolio targets flood protection and 42% targets water conservation/drought mitigation. However, most of this finance used to support the country’s disaster risk reduction efforts is not being effectively utilized for climate change disaster preparedness. Instead, it is being increasingly channelled into adaptation approaches for climate-related disasters such as EAFD and CCAFS. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 84 OF 156

## Financial instrument make-up of the proposed project

The GCF grant of USD 66 million is requested to support the implementation of the proposed project's activities. As a result of WFP, USAID, and FAO funding constraints, the GCF grant will be used for the following components: (i) the provincial irrigation departments (local and provincial irrigation departments) and international (e.g., World Bank, ADB, KfW, GIZ, etc.), with USD 10 million committed to the project; (ii) the Ministry of Water and Power (MWP), with USD 25.3 million committed to the project; (iii) the provincial irrigation departments (local and provincial irrigation departments) and international (e.g., World Bank, ADB, KfW, GIZ, etc.), with USD 10 million committed to the project.

The AE has engaged private and public sector partners to mobilize resources for the US$7.8M grant. As stated in the TCFD report from the Coca-Cola Foundation (TCFD) for USEEM and a letter of interest from USAID/Pakistan, up to USD 10 million has been secured. USAID will provide USD 5 million as part of its contribution commitment.

## The conditions in Pakistan to support a rationale for maximum concessionality to be applied in investing in EAA technologies

### Market failures
Market failures tend to be skewed toward hard infrastructure as they often do not account for any negative environmental impacts and the wider benefits of ecosystem-based approaches and associated ecosystem services are underutilized. This is because there is a lack of knowledge to understand and be able to compare traditional hard infrastructure approaches to EAA alternatives.

### Trade-offs
Trade-offs between EAA technologies and hard infrastructure investments can be significant, as economically viable options do not exist due to their higher transaction costs associated with community and stakeholder engagement and technical assistance needed along social grounds.


---
**Page 88**
---

The potential to further reduce flood risk and improve water resource management but have high upfront costs and limited options for finance. Such activities have the potential to be scaled up once their potential or the ability of investments in EDA and green infrastructure interventions to address the threats of floods and droughts (resulting from climate change) and consequent increases in the demand for solutions.

219. GCF grant funding will be used to fund and demonstrate the use of EDA and green infrastructure interventions as an alternative approach to flood risk management, water resource management, and disaster risk reduction in Pakistan (Component 1). The implementation of these adaptation interventions will be used as evidence to create a replicable model for scaling up EDA and green infrastructure interventions. This will be achieved by promoting integrated strategizing, planning and policymaking. Specifically, this evidence will be used to develop and adopt updated policies and regulations for water and flood resources management in Pakistan (Component 1) — namely the Implementation Framework for the National Water Policy, the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), and Provincial Climate Change Plans (PCCPs). The project will also support the implementation of these interventions by using EDA and green infrastructure interventions to reduce the impacts of floods and droughts in Pakistan (Component 2). Additionally, the project will support the development of a replicable model for scaling up EDA and green infrastructure interventions in small enterprises identified during the DFCCD project to improve their resilience against climate change. This will be achieved by providing incubation services to 7 small businesses already identified during the DFCCD project to help them develop new products and services that are more resilient to climate change (Component 2). The project will also support the development of a replicable model for scaling up EDA and green infrastructure interventions in enterprises, and it will provide direct support to local communities to improve the sustainability of their land and water resources management practices and strengthen the climate-resilience of their agro-ecological livelihoods (Component 3).

B.6 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

220. The proposed project has been designed in close collaboration with the relevant key government stakeholders and will be implemented to ensure that sustainable adaptation benefits to the most vulnerable communities in Pakistan during and beyond the lifetime of the project. The project’s Exit Strategy is presented in this paragraph that follows, focusing on institutional learning and financial sustainability, as well as local-level sustainability.

Strengthened institutional capacity, awareness and ownership

221. In addition to the above, the project will strengthen the capacity of relevant government stakeholders — including MoCC, MoWfH (and FFC), and the Provincial Departments of Irrigation, Forestry, and Agriculture — to ensure ownership and sustainability of EDA and green infrastructure interventions for flood risk management and water resource management. The project will also generate new evidence on the benefits and cost-effectiveness of implementing EDA and green infrastructure interventions for flood and water resource management in Pakistan (Component 1). This evidence will be used as a basis for developing updated policies and reference parameters for future flood and water resources management. The evidence will also be presented to relevant stakeholders, including the Council of Common Services (CCS), as well as the National Climate Change Plan (NCCP) and Provincial Adaptation Plans (PAPs). This will ensure that these plans are aligned with the mandate of leading climate change planning in Pakistan; the Council along with the National Working Group that will be established under the project will also provide guidance on how to integrate EDA and green infrastructure into relevant regulatory frameworks for ongoing implementation and financial support beyond the lifetime of the project.

Policy and regulatory support

222. In addition, the policy and regulatory work under the project will also be used to develop updated procedures for Pakistan’s key regulations and policies for flood and water resources management — namely the Implementation Frameworks for the National Water Policy (NWP), NAP, PCCPs, and the FFC. The project will support the development of these procedures using green infrastructure to address the threats of floods and droughts by prioritizing EDA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan’s key regulations and policies for flood and water resources management. These updated procedures in these government documents (including FFC) and MoCC’s mandates for flood and water resources management will also directly support the implementation of EDA and green infrastructure interventions. The project will ensure that these interventions within these government documents will enable existing earmarked public funds that would likely otherwise be used to address climate change impacts on floods and droughts in Pakistan (Component 1) to be reallocated for the implementation and O&M of EDA and green infrastructure interventions during and beyond the project period.

## Operations and maintenance

The GCF will undertake O&M of the project's interventions for the project lifespan (30 years) through its existing resources and infrastructure to ensure the interventions function optimally and achieve the desired adaptation and mitigation outcomes. The provincial irrigation department of Di Khan is responsible for O&M and ensuring the optimal functioning of the interventions for the project lifespan. For the green infrastructure interventions, the Department of Forests and Wildlife (DFW) will be responsible for O&M and ensuring the optimal functioning of the interventions for the project lifespan. The required frequency of O&M activities required as well as the relevant responsibility(ies), are presented below.

## Project sustainability at the local level

The GoPs & C&M commitments will be supported at the community level by establishing community agreements between communities, project stakeholders, and GOBs. These agreements will outline arrangements for active community participation in QM of the project's EIA and green infrastructure interventions, ecosystem protection and management, and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities. The agreements will also establish mechanisms to monitor and protect the project’s EIA and green infrastructure from degradation, while Water User Groups (WUGs) will be established at the community level for the maintenance of the project's EIA and green infrastructure interventions (e.g., water stored in the small and large retention areas). These agreements will secure long-term protection and future replication of the project’s EIA and green infrastructure interventions at scale after project closure.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the programme/project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF Initial Investment Framework.

## OUTCOME 1: Increased resilience

### A04 - Improved livelihoods and increased access to livelihoods of most vulnerable people, communities and regions

The project will strengthen the resilience of the most vulnerable people living in Pakistan's Indus Basin — most of whom are women and children. This will be achieved by increasing their capacity to adapt to climate change impacts through implementing a combination of EDA, green infrastructure and community-based adaptation interventions in the dry season (April–June) and flood season (July–September). The project will benefit 10 million people (821,500 direct beneficiaries and 268,400 indirect beneficiaries) from climate change induced flooding. Over the City of Karachi, the EDA interventions will reduce water flow velocity by up to 30% in the dry season and up to 70% during the floodplain.

### B01 - EDA Interventions will reduce flood impacts on populations close to the intervention sites and for people living downstream

Populations close to the EDA interventions will benefit from local ecosystem services including but not limited to: reduced water flow velocity, increased groundwater recharge, improved soil moisture retention, and reduced sedimentation. Downstream communities will benefit from the slowing of upstream velocity and delayed peak flood. The downstream population will therefore decrease with increasing distance from the EDA intervention sites as their impacts and benefits are reduced.

## OUTCOME 2: Improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services

### A05 - Increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services, as described below

#### Green Infrastructure Interventions
- Will reduce flood impacts on nearby communities and ecosystems by soil erosion and river sedimentation.
- Diverting and reducing the velocity of intense flows in the torrent areas from new storage facilities will be a key feature.

#### Community-Based Adaptation Interventions
- Will increase the climate resilience of 50,720 ha of the most vulnerable agricultural lands through improved water management practices such as:
  - Drip irrigation (e.g., drip-irrigated corn varieties).
  - Improved access to extension services.
  - Water storage tanks.

#### Project Area Improvement
- Will enhance ecosystem and service functions by:
  - Restoring degraded wetlands in Di Khan, restoring 34.2 km of waterways as well as desilting and restoring channels in Bannu.
  - Reducing soil erosion through reduced runoff and sedimentation.
  - Increasing infiltration and groundwater recharge.

- Core Indicator 237 – Project will build on WFP Pakistan’s experience and foundational work developing pipeline business models.

#### Improved Policymaking and Planning
- Will improve policymaking and planning for integrated flood and water resources management at the national level, including:
  - Developing formal WUGs to resolve and prioritize alternative uses of land.
  - Strengthening institutional capacity in flood risk assessment and water resources management – applying the Implementation Framework for the National Water Policy, the NAP and Provincial Forest Policies.

## Mitigation co-benefits
240. While the proposed project is an adaptation-only project, the EBA interventions will provide mitigation co-benefits.
- These co-benefits have been calculated as follows: A total of 14.215 hectares (ha) of deforested, forested areas, and degraded lands were restored through afforestation/reforestation activities in the period from 2013 to 2016 (ANRL). ARRL CO2 removals from these activities are estimated as 26,400 tons of C02 per year while the total lifetime CO2 removal is expected to be 587,900 tons. The total CO2 removal will be used for carbon sequestration on the forest land and stand age.
- **Methods**: To estimate the total CO2 removal from afforestation, reforestation, and assisted natural regeneration, the total area was multiplied by a removal factor. To estimate the total lifetime CO2 removals, the annual removals were multiplied by the number of years the activity is expected to continue to sequester carbon.
- **Carbon stock densities** were determined for each of the different forest types using the forest inventory data at sample plots.

## Carbon stock density values
- The carbon stock density values included aboveground biomass of trees and shrubs, belowground biomass, dead wood, and litter.
- Both national and IPCC default allometric equations were used for estimating aboveground tree biomass.
- The default IPCC fraction (0.47) was applied to convert biomass to C tons.

## References
12 Pakistan's average household size of seven was used to calculate the number of beneficiaries.

* The carbon stock density value (C ton/ha) of each forest type was converted to CO2 using the conversion factor of 3.67.
* The ERFs were originally developed for the mean age of the forest. Annual ERF values were determined by dividing these factors by the mean age of each forest type.
* ERFs are used in the calculation of carbon removals by subtracting the carbon stock density (C) value of different forest states and the difference between these values when one forest state is degraded into a lower stratum (open forest cover >11%, sparsely forested canopy cover ≥50%, medium-forest canopy cover ≥70% and dense-canopy cover >70%).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 98 OF 156

## Communities in Pakistan have suffered a series of external shocks affecting their livelihoods, including the COVID-19 pandemic and climate change.

### Accelerating Climate Innovation

The project creates a significant shift in Pakistan's food and water resources management efforts by establishing a new framework for EIA and green infrastructure interventions in the Indus Basin that validate the cost efficiency, benefits, and sustainability of these adaptation and resilience measures. As the largest investment at $684 million, this project will be implemented across 12 communities in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province, where agriculture accounts for approximately 22% of the GDP and employs 44% of the labor force, and where climate change is expected to have a significant impact on agricultural productivity and food markets. Exacerbated by the COVID pandemic, almost 44% of the population has been identified as experiencing acute poverty, with 60% living in rural areas. The project will provide communities with limited or no income security or financial resources to withstand the economic disruptions of drought and flooding brought on by climate change.

### Evidence for Decision Making

The project will generate new evidence for the climate change adaptation decision-making process through a series of pilot interventions that reduce flood risk, enhance water availability, attenuating floods, conducting economic valuations of EIA and green infrastructure interventions to determine their cost efficiency, benefits, and sustainability. The project will also develop an integrated framework of additional EIA and green infrastructure interventions beyond the GCF project in the Hakra and Hadara Watersheds, in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province; the Indus River Basin, in the Punjab province; the Sir Khwai Watershed, in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa; and La Lake Creek, in Punjab Province (Activity 2.1.1).

### National Coordination

The project will develop updated procedures for the integration and execution of EIA and green infrastructure interventions beyond the GCF project in the Hakra and Hadara Watersheds, in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province; the Indus River Basin, in the Punjab province; the Sir Khwai Watershed, in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa; and La Lake Creek, in Punjab Province (Activity 2.1.1). The project will create a National Working Group chaired by the MoCC and comprised of regulators within the MoWWF and the relevant Provincial

## D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The project's interventions will contribute to the achievement of a number of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will help alleviate poverty for 50,750 farmers vulnerable to income loss from floods and drought brought on by climate change (SDG 13 Climate Action), directly helping to enhance the resilience of these communities. It will also contribute to SDG 2 Zero Hunger through improved food security and nutrition, SDG 4 Quality Education for children in flood-prone areas, SDG 5 Gender equality promotion, SDG 6 Clean water and sanitation by improving access to clean drinking water and sanitation facilities, and SDG 10 Reduced inequalities related to EIA interventions. The project will also contribute to the following co-benefits: SDG 3 Good health and well-being through improved water management practices; SDG 7 Affordable and clean energy for irrigation systems; SDG 8 Decent work and economic growth by improving livelihoods of farmers; SDG 10 Reduced inequalities in access to resources, land, and credit; SDG 12 Responsible consumption and production by reducing flood damage to crops and agricultural equipment.

### Potential for Scaling Up and Replication

The project is strongly supported with the GoF's goal of scaling up initiatives that catalyze climate impact beyond the pilot phase. The project will be a key point in demonstrating how EIA and green infrastructure interventions are cost-effective and impactful adaptation options. The GoF has already committed to expanding EIA and green infrastructure interventions across Pakistan, including the Indus River Basin, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Azo Watersheds in Sindh Province, India Dolph Reserves in Sindh Province, Iskhe Watershed in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, and the National Forest Park.

### Potential for Knowledge Sharing and Lessons Learned

The project will generate an economic valuation on the benefits of EIA and green infrastructure, proving that these interventions are cost-effective and impactful adaptation options. The GoF has already committed to expanding EIA and green infrastructure interventions across Pakistan.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (V2.0) PAGE 100 OF 156

## SDG 2 - Zero hunger: The poverty reduction benefits of the project outlined above are also expected to help reduce food insecurity in the Indus Basin.

### SDG 3 - Good health and well-being: The project will help reduce impacts of climate change on water availability, which is critical for human health. It will also improve access to clean drinking water and sanitation.

### SDG 5 - Gender equality: The challenges of climate change impacts, building resilience and gender inequalities have been recognized in the Project Design Document (Annex B). The project will address these issues by taking steps to narrow the gender gap and empower women and girls. Women are more vulnerable to food insecurity due to climate change as they have more limited access than men for resources for building resilience.

### SDG 14 - Life below water: The Project will expand women's social and economic freedoms by focusing on their role as change agents in improving the livelihoods of communities living along rivers, such as the Indus River. Only about 10% of the unique species exist today in the lower parts of the river basin.

damages to exceed $10 billion equivalent to 4% of the country’s GDP (1). The floods have destroyed huge swaths of mandated farmland which will be unusable for some time. Pakistan's economy is heavily dependent on its agricultural sector, and the loss of this land could severely reduce revenue; these recent flood events will cause significant crop revenue losses and lead to severe food shortages.

254. The floods in 2022 are Pakistan’s most recent experience with climate change but have experienced persistent extreme weather conditions over several decades. During the last decade, floods which affected more than 20 million people, approximately 2,000 lives were lost, 3,000 people were injured, 1.6 million hectares of agricultural land was damaged, and $5 billion in economic losses were reported (2). Since then, Pakistan has experienced long hikes for six consecutive years(3).

Economic and financial constraints for climate change adaptation in Pakistan

255. The country’s climate vulnerability is increasing due to the urgent need of adaptation finance to implement projects which can help reduce Pakistan's vulnerability to extreme events. The country's climate adaptation needs are estimated at $10 billion per year (4). This figure includes a range of adaptation measures, which will require contributions from international public financing sources such as the GCF. Furthermore, there is a key role for private sector investment in adaptation.

256. As described above, Pakistan already suffers immense losses from climate change impacts and evidence suggests that such losses will continue to have a significant and increasing effect on the GDP if no adaptation action is taken (5). The country’s economic development is heavily dependent upon foreign financing, which is increasingly constrained by financial constraints. Addressing these effects requires a swift increase in investment in climate change adaptation. A comprehensive approach to addressing Pakistan's climate vulnerability will require substantial additional adaptation needs are to be significantly greater than current investment levels, particularly in vulnerable developing countries.

257. Pakistan remains a resource-constrained economy, dependent on foreign financing in the shape of loans, foreign investment, and grant aid, for amongst others, infrastructure projects and assistance during post disaster situations. The country’s economic development is heavily dependent upon foreign financing, which is increasingly constrained by financial constraints. Addressing these effects requires a swift increase in investment in climate change adaptation.

258. National adaptation budgets are also constrained by the urgent need for investments in other development priorities such as education, health care, access to clean water and energy which all determine the real purchasing power of households, disproportionately affecting poor and vulnerable households that spend a larger share of their income on basic needs (6). The country’s economic development is heavily dependent upon foreign financing, which is increasingly constrained by financial constraints. Addressing these effects requires a swift increase in investment in climate change adaptation.

### Pakistan

#### Background Information

- **Population:** Over 207 million (average population density of 267 km²)
- **Human Development Index (HDI):** 0.598 (147 out of 188 countries)
- **GDP Growth Rate:** 3.5% in FY 2020/21, with a projected growth rate of 3.6%
- **Poverty Alleviation:** 38% live with multidimensional poverty
- **Gender Inequality Index (GII):** Pakistan scores 0.546 for its GII

#### Projected Impact

- **Human Development Index (HDI) Score:** 0.595, resulting in a placement of 147 out of 188 countries.
- **Poverty Alleviation:** 38% live with multidimensional poverty
- **Gender Inequality Index (GII):** Pakistan scores 0.546 for its GII

#### Projected Contribution to Poverty Reduction

- **Agricultural Livelihoods:** Reliant on ecosystem services such as water provision, soil fertility, and pollination.
- **Climate Change Impact:** Areas of Pakistan experienced temperatures 3–8°C higher than average in 2015.
