**Page 107**
---

example, in 2010-2014, five flood events destroyed 10.6 million acres of crops, negatively affecting farmers' income and land fertility. Although Pakistan has the most extensive continuous irrigation network in the world and a high level of water storage capacity, these resources are increasingly under pressure due to climate change, leading to high demand for irrigation because of increasing evaporation rates and poor water storage capacity. Furthermore, over-mechanization of agriculture has led to soil degradation and reduced crop yields. Changes in rainfall patterns and seasonal shifts in weather have resulted in climate irregularities that are making agricultural production more unpredictable and vulnerable to extreme weather events and other climate change impacting factors. All these factors compound the climate change vulnerability of agricultural communities[14].

265. Women, children with disabilities and smallholder farmers are among those who are at the most risk to the impacts of climate change. Women in Pakistan are responsible for 80% of food production and processing; they take care of farming work when men migrate for better employment opportunities, they very seldom own or have access to land, and they often lack access to credit and other financial services that would allow them to make major household purchases, thereby limiting their ability to respond to crisis situations. Women rarely own property (other than land) and are not able to access the same level of education as men; this limits their access to Government compensation if there are damages. Additionally, women also have limited access to information communication technology.

266. The impacts of climate change on food productivity pose a very serious challenge in a country where 60% of the population is food insecure (especially in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa) and almost half of the women and children are malnourished. Climate change will have significant implications for Pakistan's agricultural sector, especially in southern parts of Pakistan, though there will be minor improvements in yield in the colder northern areas. Punjab and Sindh will experience a decline in yields due to increased frequency of droughts and floods related to climate change impacts. The production of income-generating cash crops like cotton, maize, rice, sugarcane and wheat is also expected to decrease.

267. Livestock rearing constitutes 53% of Pakistan's agricultural GDP. It is dependent on climate sensitive grasslands and rangelands, which are increasingly under pressure from longer and more frequent droughts, flash floods, and increased frequency of extreme weather events such as heat waves and cold spells. In addition to farmers who earn about 40% of their income through livestock. There are regional differences with the highest proportion in Sindh (58%) followed by Punjab (37%). Livestock rearing is also a major source of employment. Climate change on West Coast has led stress resulting in lower milk and meat production and reduced reproduction. Rising temperatures will lead to increased heat stress, which will result in higher mortality rates among livestock.

268. Vulnerable communities in the Indus Basin depend on the water supply and recharge, food production, food protection, sediment control, carbon sequestration, climate regulation, and other benefits provided by ecosystems for their livelihoods. The Indus Basin is one of the most important river basins in Pakistan. It has contributed total economic value of over US$ 115 billion/year. Degradation of wetlands, forests, and pastures due to climate change will lead to increased sediment loads in rivers and reduced water availability for irrigation. These impacts are compounded by existing constraints on the Indus basin such as irrigation infrastructure and other stress (e.g., pollution). Climate change will also increase the frequency of floods which increases the vulnerability of communities and their livelihoods.

269. Strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

268. Historically, development initiatives in Pakistan's water sector have lacked the integration of adaptation, leaving the country vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased flood risk and reduced water availability for irrigation. The construction of two major dams (Tarbela and Mangla) and linking canals for storage and irrigation via the Indus River Project were initiated before the importance of climate change was recognized. Climate-sensitive needs were not well understood and hence not considered in such initiatives, leaving them vulnerable to climate change impacts such


---
**Page 108**
---

as increasing evaporation and salination. While access to information and knowledge on climate change and adaptation responses (such as EEA) have since improved in Pakistan, the water sector's regulatory and planning frameworks are still not fully aligned with the country's climate change priorities or its vulnerability to climate change impacts. There is also a disconnect between plans, regulations and mandates for water management and climate change adaptation. The lack of clarity on roles and responsibilities among stakeholders such as the Departments of Water, Irrigation and Agriculture are not clearly defined, coupled with a non-existent coordination mechanism at national level.

The situation is further exacerbated by seemingly limited public sector budgets for adaptation only 5-7% of the GoPs budget is designated for climate change adaptation, the majority of which is used for costly and high-maintenance flood protection infrastructure. The lack of institutional capacity to plan, design, finance, implement and adapting implementation, especially in the areas of EEA and green infrastructure interventions. Consequently, Pakistan's water sector has not been able to effectively mainstream EEA and green infrastructure into its planning and implementing adaptation, especially in the areas of EEA and green infrastructure interventions. Consequently, Pakistan's water sector has not been able to effectively mainstream EEA and green infrastructure into its planning and implementing adaptation, especially in the areas of EEA and green infrastructure interventions.

D.S Country assessment (max 100 words, approximate: 1 page)

1. Improving resilience to climate change hazards, particularly floods and droughts, is among the top priorities of GoP Following a Ramadan recommended exposure visit (Ramdan Adviser Mission (RAM) to China's Yangtze Basin project) and discussions with the World Bank, the Government of Pakistan (GoP) increasingly recognizes that a more disciplinary approach based on engineering solutions alone - as in the traditional flood management paradigm - is not sufficient for addressing climate change impacts related to risk floods. RAM's recommendations and the subsequent exchanges with flood management experts in the Yangtze Basin project have helped the GoP to better understand the need for an integrated approach to flood risk management. Specifically, a Development Portfolio Approach (DPA) is needed, consisting of: i) EEA interventions (e.g., restoration, rehabilitation, and strengthening of riverbank vegetation; construction of check dams and other small-scale structures such as check dams and water harvesting structures); ii) land use and resource management interventions (e.g., expanding food and water security), and iii) legislative and regulatory reforms to promote EEA and green infrastructure interventions.

The DPA has been developed at the request of the GoP specifically to act on these recommendations. A participatory approach has been taken for the design of the proposed project, including close collaboration with key stakeholders such as the Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR), Pakistan Council of Research in Water Resources (PCRWR), and other relevant national and international institutions and stakeholders particularly the MoWR, MCCG, and FFC respectively. In particular, the AE and pre-project partners have worked closely with the GoP to ensure that the project is aligned with the GoPs priorities and development agenda at a national level as well as providing timely inputs into the development of the proposal. Twenty letters of support from stakeholders including MoWR, PCRWR, FFC, MCCG, and other relevant institutions have been received by the GDA NCA. MCCG have also been received in support of the project which emphasizes the country's ownership and commitment to the proposed project. The GoP has also provided a letter of intent for the project which aligns with the key climate needs and agenda presented therein further reinforcing the ownership of the proposal by the country as presented in this section. Moreover, the GDA has highlighted the proposed project's important role of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## Contributing to national adaptation objectives by including it as a priority initiative under the country’s updated NDCs (2021)

### Coherence with National climate strategy and existing policies

The proposed project will contribute towards achieving priorities within recent GCF proposals, including the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The project is a key adaptation priority within the updated NDCs and will contribute towards the short-term (2020-2035), medium (2036-2049) and long-term (2050+) climate change objectives. These include: “reducing flood risk and floodwater”; strengthening sub-national adaptation planning, building capacity; and investing in climate-resilient infrastructure and services to support food security, water security, the environment and climate change platforms.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 107 OF 156

## Relevant Outputs

### National Forest Policy (NFP)
- To respond to the increasing demand for forest products and services while maintaining natural habitats and green areas for biodiversity conservation, the NFP aims at:
    - Increasing the area of protected forests
    - Strengthening the role of community-managed forests in mountainous regions
    - Expanding the area of national parks and wildlife sanctuaries
- The NFP is consistent with Pakistan's commitment to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and its Paris Agreement.
- It aims at:
    1. Reducing greenhouse gas emissions from forests, in view of Pakistan’s high vulnerability to climate change impacts such as extreme events, wildfires, migration and displacement
    2. Enhancing forest resilience against climate change
    3. Promoting sustainable use of natural resources and long-term sustainability

### National Water Policy (NWP)
- Provides a framework for the development of water sector policies in Pakistan.
- Focuses on issues such as climate change adaptation, water security, and poverty reduction.
- The main objectives of the NWP are to:
    1. Ensure sustainable access to safe drinking water
    2. Improve management through extension soil conservation, catchment area management, and watershed development
    3. Respond to climate change impacts and overall policy challenges

### National Water Policy (NWP)
- A national framework within the broader context of Pakistan’s master plan for sustainable development and management of water resources.
- Key objectives:
    1. Drought management with emphasis on long-term vulnerability reduction
    2. Promoting appropriate technologies for rainwater harvesting in rural as well as urban areas
    3. Encouraging beneficiary participation and public-private partnerships

### National Water Policy (NWP)
- Recognizes the importance of water resources, including surface water, groundwater, wetlands, and rivers, for human health and livelihoods.
- Key objectives:
    1. Addressing primary threats to Pakistan’s wetlands
    2. Promoting sustainable use of wetland resources at local to international levels
    3. Building Pakistan's capacity for sustainable wetland management

### National Water Policy (NWP)
- Improving understanding, perceptions and attitudes towards wetlands conservation and management.

## The National Disaster Risk Reduction Framework (NDRRF)
- A guiding framework for addressing the disaster risk management challenges in Pakistan; it covers both national and sub-national levels
- It provides a set of policy measures to ameliorate already existing vulnerabilities, and equally important measures to ensure that new risks are not created
- The NDRRF serves as a guiding framework with respect to development plans and programs to 2035

282. This will be achieved through the implementation of EEA and green infrastructure interventions, and validation of EEA and green infrastructure interventions as cost-effective and impactful adaptation options.

283. GEF financing will be used to overcome the barriers to adopting EEA and green infrastructure interventions to enhance climate resilience in Pakistan. The project will provide a suite of co-modified interventions that are expected to deliver several adaptation benefits that will contribute to achieving a paradigm shift in the country towards climate resilient development. These include: (i) flood and drought risk management; (ii) water resource management; (iii) coastal protection; (iv) disaster risk reduction; (v) ecosystem-based adaptation; (vi) enhanced livelihoods; (vii) improved land use planning; (viii) integrated interventions into Pakistan’s existing regulatory, policy and flood for climate change adaptation; (ix) capacity building; (x) knowledge sharing; (xi) awareness raising; (xii) community engagement; (xiii) Khyber, Balochistan, and Punjab; (xiv) enhance the climate resilience livelihoods of the most vulnerable target communities in Pakistan. The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach involving local governments to adapt their existing extension services to better support farmers impacted by floods and droughts caused by climate change.

## The following five key steps were used to underpin the CBA for the project:

- **Definition of the project’s interventions:** This step defined the specific actions against which impacts will be measured.
- **Comparison of the proposed project’s interventions over their lifetimes with alternative scenarios:** Two scenarios were assessed. The first scenario is a baseline scenario, and the second examines the impacts (costs and damages) and associated costs assuming climate change over the period 2015–2039.
- **Estimation of the costs and benefits associated with each intervention type:** This analysis considered costs across the lifetime of each intervention.
- **Estimation of the economic, social, and environmental benefits of the proposed interventions:** Adaptation benefits and co-benefits were quantified and valued in monetary terms as far as possible and where it is appropriate to do so. The results are presented in Table 20 below. The costs and benefits (and their likely significance) were described in either quantitative (where possible) or qualitative terms.
- **Discounting of the costs, benefits, and co-benefits:** This step involved discounting all future costs and benefits at a rate of 10% per year over a 30-year period, starting in 2024. Net benefits were calculated as the difference between the with-project and without-project scenarios.
- **Sensitivity analysis:** The results are presented to highlight the sensitivity of the outcomes to changes in the underlying assumptions, focusing on key input variables with large uncertainties.

## Table 20: Cost-benefit metrics for the proposed project (base case)

| Metric | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Total costs of interventions (US$ million, undiscounted) | $8.5 |
| Net benefits (US$ million, discounted at a rate of 10%) | $(7.4) |
| BCR | $(1) |
| EIRR | 13.6% |

## Significant benefits are associated with carbon sequestration and biodiversity and cultural services provided by EBA and green infrastructure interventions which, together, are valued at around US$2.3 million. According to the results of the CBA, the project is expected to generate net benefits over its lifetime (discounted) due to changes in input values. The results of the CBA are particularly sensitive to changes in the discount rate, the shadow price of carbon and the social cost of carbon.

The discount rate used corresponds to the average of the reported interest rate in Pakistan (2017: 4.74%) and the discount rate at a global level (2015: 3.8%). The shadow price of carbon is estimated as US$6 per tonne, which is consistent with the Central Redd+ Agriculture and Water Management in the Indo-Basin (FIP) and is also compliant with the discount rate used by the World Bank for its projects.

The social cost of carbon is estimated at US$35 per tonne. This value is based on the 2018 Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Fifth Assessment Report, which provides a range of values from US$7 to US$46 per tonne for the year 2015.

**References:**

- **Milestones Change Corporation. 2021. Guidelines for economic and beneficiary analysis. Available at: https://www.cbc.org/milestoneschange/docs/india_analysis_for_accounts_and_beneficiaries_2009.pdf**

## Application of best practices in Pakistan's water sector

### Best practices from similar projects as outlined in Section B.1 were integrated into the design of EBA and green infrastructure interventions

#### Lessons learned from key planned and ongoing investments into climate change adaptation in Pakistan’s Indus Basin, including but not limited to the following:

- **National Water and Forest Policy (NWFP)**
- **Agriculture and Water Management (led by NWFP)**
- **FPOs Scaling-up of Coastal Lake Outfall River (GLOF) Project**

Such lessons include appropriate approaches for ensuring that project interventions are designed in a manner that is consistent with the landscape-level approach. The lessons also include best practices which were applicable to design of the proposed project and will contribute to the efficiency and effectiveness of project operations.

### International best practices and lessons learned identified for the project include:

- **Incorporating adaptive management into the design of interventions**
- **Interventions that are integrated and iterative to account for the dynamic nature of landscape processes and monitoring processes are integrated and iterative to account for the dynamic nature of landscape processes and monitoring processes**
- **Involving communities in the design, implementation, and monitoring of interventions at the community level in an equitable and responsive manner to fairly distribute benefits and incentives of landscape management activities.**
- **Increasing institutional knowledge into training for smallholder farmers and local communities on climate-resilient agricultural practices, including integrated natural resources management and the adoption of multiple livelihoods:**
- **Significant lessons learned from ongoing experience with natural resources management and the adoption of multiple livelihoods:**
- **Using EIA and green infrastructure. This is evident in the Gp’s exposure visit (Former Adviser Missionary (RAM) to Pakistan, 2015) where the Gp visited a project that was designed as an integrated approach for water resource management. During this visit, the Gp observed that “a mixed-disciplinary approach based on ecosystem services and human well-being is more likely to be successful than one that does not fully handle hydrological cycles, ecosystems, and the security of people prone to food risks.”**
- **Incorporating lessons learned from ongoing experience with natural resources management and the adoption of multiple livelihoods:**
- **Developing strategies to access finance for adaptation:**
    - **Utilizing existing financial mechanisms such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other climate-resilient interventions to ensure that these interventions continue to directly respond to the adaptation needs of communities.**
    - **Engaging and collaborating extensively in a gender- and culturally-responsive way with relevant national and sub-national stakeholders, including women’s groups, to maintain buy-in to the project over the long term; and:**
    - **Implementing effective financial and project management strategies to ensure the efficient use of financial resources and avoid delays during the implementation phase.**

## E1. Project/Programme Focus

- ☐ Increase resilience (adaptation)

## E2. GCF Impact Level: Paradigm shift potential(max 600 words, approximately 1–2 pages)

This section of the logical framework is meant to help a project/programme manager and assess how it contributes to the paradigm shift described in section D.2 above by applying five dimensions of resilience.

Accordingly, for each assessment dimension (use the definition of assessment in the accompanying guidance note), describe the current state (baseline) and the potential scenario (target) and then the current state (Baseline) by using the three-point scale rating (low, medium, and high provided in the guidance note. Also describe how the project/programme will be able to shift characteristics along each of these five dimensions (see explanation of dimensions below; explanations of resilience in detail: see page 102).

### Assessment Dimension

| Description | Rating | Potential target scenario (Description) | How the project/programme will contribute (Description) |
|------------|-------|----------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|
| Scale      | Current state food management and water resources management that are often unable to reach vulnerable communities, especially in remote areas, and are costly to repair and operate. | The potential target scenario is a shift away from current scale of food and water resources management that are often unable to reach vulnerable communities, especially in remote areas, towards grey infrastructure interventions towards a more resilient food and water resources management system underpinned by ecosystem-based approaches. This will be achieved through national and sub-national levels across landscapes, cost-effective, cross-sectoral and multi-stakeholder approaches to food and water resources management and regulatory frameworks. | In addition to demonstrating the case for EDA and green infrastructure interventions at scale in Pakistan and expanding the country’s current food and water resources management to prioritize ecosystem-based approaches, the project will contribute to scaling up EDA and green infrastructure interventions across landscapes and communities with the necessary skills and knowledge to implement them effectively. |


---
**Page 116**
---

Additionally, Pakistan’s current policy and regulatory requirements for food and water resources management fail to promote E4A and green infrastructure solutions that address flood and drought risk reduction strategies and do not consider the critical role of healthy freshwater ecosystems in increasing community resilience to climate change.

The potential target scenario is having an evidence base demonstrating E4A and green infrastructure interventions as demonstrated as cost-effective, scalable, and drought risk reduction options in Pakistan. However, these interventions are not prioritized within the country’s relevant policy frameworks for water resources management (namely the National Framework for the National Water Resources Adaptation Plan (NWRAP) and Provincial Adaptation Plans of Sindh, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa provinces). This makes replication of such interventions difficult.

The project will contribute to replicating E4A and green infrastructure interventions for flood risk reduction in Pakistan by: i) implementing E4A and green infrastructure solutions in the Indus Basin; ii) generating an evidence base demonstrating the benefits; iii) using this evidence to expand policy frameworks for managing food and water resources management through national and sub-national level leaders/representatives, to apply the updated policies and strategies that channel future investments into E4A and green infrastructure interventions; iv) replicating similar adaptation strategies in other flood-prone areas of Pakistan to those demonstrated under the proposed project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 114 OF 156

## Sustainability

Because Pakistan's regulatory frameworks for flood and water resources management do not prioritize climate change adaptation and green infrastructure, they are not prioritized in government plans; there is a lack of evidence, awareness, and capacity to implement these interventions over the long term.

### Low

The potential target scenarios to secure financing from the GCF, EPA and green infrastructure investments for flood risk reduction, climate change-induced floods and droughts management, and water resources regulations and policies will include EDA and green infrastructure investments. This will enable streamlined public funds allocation to address floods and droughts in Pakistan.

### Sustainability

Sustainability of the project's interventions will be enhanced by the implementation Framework for the National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPs), and their Provincial Adaptation Plans to 2030. The NAPs will require the application of the updated framework of the National Water Policy, while MoCC in collaboration with the Environment Protection Authority (EPA) will ensure that all relevant procedures for the NAP and provincial adaptation plans are followed during implementation of EDA and green infrastructure investments.

The project's interventions will be climate change-driven and drought-resistant. These interventions instead of grey infrastructure. The framework will also provide a basis for the project's interventions beyond and during the project period (see Section 8.5 for further details).

### Outcome 1: EAIA and green infrastructure interventions will improve community-led management reducing flood risk and enhancing the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities in Pakistan's Indus Basin.

#### Government records (including those from Federal Flood Commission and disaster management authorities at district, provincial or national level) are used to determine flood extent and flood map analysis. These reports are generated at least twice per year.
- A baseline study will be undertaken during Year 1 of the project to establish the specific baseline parameters relevant to this indicator.

#### Area (ha) of flood extent reduced
| | |
|---|---|
| **Outcome 1: EAIA and green infrastructure interventions** | **Area (ha) of flood extent reduced** |

#### Assumptions
- All green infrastructure interventions are successful in reducing the flood extent.
- The total area under EAIA is 9,782 ha as per the indicative future (2020–2050) RCP 4.5 scenario.
- The peak area inundated and the depth of water calculated from this extreme event (EAIA) are 136 km² and 1 m respectively.

#### Notes
- The final target is available for this indicator as it is a combination of the impact of complex, fully integrated EAIA and green infrastructure interventions.
- Progress for installing green infrastructure interventions can be tracked using the indicators listed below: (Number of green infrastructure solutions installed).
- Flood extent was calculated from the maximum extent of inundation in the extreme flood scenario. The extreme flood scenario was based on the indicative future (2020–2050) RCP 4.5 scenario.
- The peak area inundated and the depth of water calculated from this extreme event (EAIA) are 136 km² and 1 m respectively, as they were considered to be long-term significant.

## Output 3.1: Pipeline of feasible climate-resilient businesses

### Activity 3.1.1: Develop a pipeline of sustainable climate-smart enterprises

Under this activity, due diligence and development plans will be developed for the identified businesses, and business development services will be provided to each business. Additionally, technical assistance reports will be provided to these businesses in closing investment deals including with the DFCD investments.

### Activity 3.1.2: Improve the climate resilience of vulnerable target communities

Pakistan's agricultural sector is one of the most vulnerable sectors to floods and droughts in Pakistan. This activity will help strengthen the climate resilience of smallholder farmers, particularly those living in the Indus Basin that depend on agriculture for their livelihood.

### Sub-Activity 3.1.1: Build on initial due diligence results

# E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## MAE Plan

The project includes monitoring and evaluation systems to track progress towards the planned outcomes over the five-year term of the project. A Monitoring and Evaluation Plan (MAE) Plan has been prepared for the project and is included as Annex 11 to the Funding Proposal. Fund-level monitoring and evaluation of the project will be based on the MAE Plan.

### Primary Responsibility for Monitoring & Evaluation

Primary responsibility for M&E will rest with WWF-Pakistan, through a full-time M&E Manager within the PMU. The M&E Manager will provide regular reports to the AE on project implementation, challenges, and financial status to allow the AE to monitor and evaluate the project, and to report to the GCF. The PMU will be responsible for providing technical support to WWF-Pakistan in monitoring reporting, as well as action plans for gender, stakeholder engagement, and environmental and social safeguards.

### M&E Management

The M&E Manager will be the custodian of all project-level MAE data, including the development and maintenance of a centralized database for data collation, analysis and reports. This manager will be supported by:

- **Three full-time Provincial M&E Officers (project staff) will report directly to and receive technical guidance from the M&E Manager**. These officers will provide technical support in the collection and analysis of project field data on their use. The M&E Officers will monitor the project's progress against the targets defined for each indicator, and prepare regular reports to the M&E Manager.
- **Full-time Environment and Social Safeguards (ESS) and Gender Managers (PMU staff)** will collect MAE data and provide technical support in the collection of ESS and gender-related data. These roles are held by the ESS Officer (defined as) and Gender Action Plan (defined as), respectively, under the guidance of the M&E Manager.
- **Part-time Provincial M&E Conducts** will conduct internal and final evaluations for the project in accordance with AE and GCF requirements.

### Monitoring During Implementation Phase

During the implementation phase, specific means of verification (MoVs) will be used to assess the progress against the project performance indicators presented in Section E.5 of the Funding Proposal. WRI/WWF will use MoVs as outlined below for each indicator. The list of MoVs is provided in Annex 4 - Detailed budget. The M&E process for the proposed project is described below.

### First Level

The project field teams will collect MAE data on site-specific activities based on the indicators, targets and performance measures defined by the AE. This data will be collected and collated to ensure quality assured and validated by the Provincial M&E Officers, who will conduct quarterly monitoring visits to the


---
**Page 148**
---

project sites and develop site-specific quarterly internal monitoring reports, M&E data as well as their sources will be validated by the M&E Manager who will conduct biannual field monitoring visits to the project sites.

### 297. Second level: The M&E Manager will consolidate the findings of site-specific quarterly monitoring reports (including any corrective actions taken) and prepare a consolidated report for submission to the AE, NDA, GCF, NDA, WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit, PMU and relevant project partners.

The M&E Manager will facilitate these meetings, which will be attended by the PM, ESS and Gender Managers, and Project Team Members. The M&E Manager will also prepare a consolidated report for submission to the AE on progress updates on the implementation of the EMFs (Annex 6) and Gender Action Plan (Annex 8), respectively. The M&E Manager will also prepare a consolidated report for submission to WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit, PMU and relevant project partners.

The M&E Manager will also organize at least one reflection session per year with the PM, ESS, gender and other relevant project partners on the progress of the site-specific quarterly monitoring data against their Theory of Change and Logical Framework to adaptively manage the project.


### 298. Third level: In accordance with GCF requirements, the M&E Manager will compile Annual Progress Reports (APRs) for submission to WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit (based at the Head Office), which will be reviewed by WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit (based at the Head Office) for clearance; with the findings presented at quarterly WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit meetings. The APRs will include a narrative report on progress against project milestones, progress reviews and technical oversight). After these internal presentations, the APRs will be submitted to WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit for review and clearance before being submitted to the AE. The APRs will also be shared with the AE as per specified frequencies mentioned in the signed project agreements. The APRs will explain the progress of the project against results indicators, implementation challenges/delays, achievements against targets and financial status.


#### Periodic Reports:

* **299a.** The PMU will submit an Annual Performance Report (APR) to the AE by January 31 each year of implementation, consolidating information from all EEs, subgrants, and subcontracts, including a narrative report on progress against project milestones, progress reviews and technical oversight). After these internal presentations, the APRs will be submitted to WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit for review and clearance before being submitted to the AE. The PMU will also share the APR with the AE as per specified frequencies mentioned in the signed project agreements.

* **300b.** The PMU will submit semi-annual technical reports to the AE, one at the mid-year and one at the end of the calendar year, to allow the AE to make its reports to the GCF. Additionally, the PMU will submit an AE quarterly evaluation report (QER) for submission to WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit, PMU and relevant project partners.

The QER will include a narrative report on progress against project milestones, progress reviews and technical oversight). After these internal presentations, the QERs will be submitted to WWF Pakistan’s M&E Unit for review and clearance before being submitted to the AE. The PMU will also share the QER with the AE as per specified frequencies mentioned in the signed project agreements.

The PMU will submit semi-annual financial reports (SFR) to the GCF. WWF Pakistan will be responsible for preparing the financial reports, controlling their quality and submitting them to the AE. The PMU will also submit these SFRs with the QERs to the AE.

301. The AE will carry out the following project-level monitoring and evaluation activities:

E. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), sanctions, prohibited practices, and other risks that might prevent the project/program objectives from being achieved. Also provide a description of any risk factors that are specific to this project.

For probability: High signifies high probability. Medium has medium probability. Low has negligible probability. For impact: High signifies high impact. Medium has medium impact. Low signifies low impact.

Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistle-blowers or witnesses, as well as any other illegal activities and detrimental actions.

Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

Description
The project includes a new mechanism for coordination among national agencies, provincial agencies, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to ensure the implementation of Pakistan’s Water Policy Implementation Framework (WPIF), NAP, and Provincial Adaptation Plans.

(Mitigation Measures)
The NWG will be established to lead the development of updated procedures for Pakistan's Implementation Framework for the National Water Policy, the NAP, and Provincial Adaptation Plans from Sindh, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Balochistan, and Gilgit-Baltistan. The NWG will also ensure that these procedures align with current national policies and guidelines, such as the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA), Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD), Ministry of Climate Change (MoCC), MoIRW (including FPG), the Provincial Departments of Irrigation, Forestry and Agriculture local NGOs, CSOs, and other stakeholders. The NWG will also ensure that these procedures are consistent with the expertise and capacity to influence government regulation and procedure, and/or its position within government and development organizations.

The project Steering Committee (PSC) and Provincial Oversight Committees (POCs) will be critical in facilitating participation under the NWG, securing government and community buy-in, and driving approvals/decisions at the national level. The PSC and POCs will also ensure that all stakeholders are aware of their responsibilities under the project. The Project Manager will coordinate with the PSC and NWG closely to ensure the timely formation of the NWG and its effective functioning.

Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

Description
Extreme climate events, such as floods, may disrupt project implementation. Physical accessibility to the sites could be compromised due to flooding or other extreme weather conditions. Information on disaster recovery could reduce their attention and resources for implementation of project activities.

(Mitigation Measures)
The project implementation plan (Annex 5) will include measures to address potential delays in implementing project activities due to climate events. The Project Manager will be responsible for coordinating adaptive management approaches with the PSC and NWG.


Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

Description

# Technical and operational

## Description

The communities in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa Province (KP), particularly those in the districts of Dera Ismail Khan and Chakdara Lahi, have a high degree of autonomy from the national and provincial governments. Due to their special status, the communities' Tribal Elders and leadership may not maintain proper implementation of project activities.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

WWF Pakistan has engaged with the Tribal Elders and leaders in Dera Ismail Khan and Chakdara Lahi and secured letters of commitment to participate in the project, included in Annex 6. The ESMP (Annex 6) and SSMF (Annex 6) have both established such mechanisms for ensuring that communities are involved in the project's implementation. In addition, as part of the project's overall implementation, activities in Dera Ismail Khan and the Chakdara Lahi will be executed through local Site Implementation Units with representatives from the communities. These active sites included in Section 3.0 follow the agreed-upon engagement mechanisms defined in Annexes 6 and 7.

## Selected Risk Factor: Category

### Probability

Impact

### Medium

### High

## Description

The reforestation and revegetation activities under the project may require limiting access to certain areas during planting and early maturation of saplings. Limiting access for local peoples to community areas which they traditionally used for grazing, fishing, and other uses could have a negative impact on the project and ultimately disrupt activity delivery.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

During development of the project ESMP, SSMF, and GAP communities have been fully briefed on the project activities and their potential impacts. The communities will be involved in the project's implementation through regular approval of the project and will be essential partners for communicating the project's long-term benefits to their constituents. The communities' representatives are also included as members of the Site Implementation Units, which will accommodate communities during execution of the project for any restrictions which may arise. During execution of the project, the communities will be involved in the creation of Alternative Livelihood Plans (ALPs) that can be found in Annex 6 to determine if any restrictions are necessary. The screening will assess community development activities and areas, and work with communities to create Alternative Livelihood Plans that are mutually agreed upon.

## Selected Risk Factor: Category

### Probability

Impact

### Medium

### Low

## Description

Foreign exchange rate could affect the financial viability of the project. Significant fluctuations in exchange rates between the PKR to USD has been observed. In March 2021, USD = 179 PKR; whereas during July 2022 it was 220 PKR. The expected change in foreign exchange rate is USD = 235 PKR → USD = 220 PKR → USD = 219. Depreciation or appreciation of currencies could lead to under- or over-budgeting of the budget.

A clause in the agreement should be added that "In case of significant exchange gain/loss, the implementer should ensure that the project is not adversely affected and that the project's financial viability remains within the approved financing amount. The expected change in foreign exchange rates can also be analyzed and incorporated into the project's overall budget."

## Selected Risk Factor: Category

### Probability

Impact

### Medium

### Low

## Description

Money laundering or terrorist financing leads to improper use of property, assets, or GCF funding. Project actors fail to act with due applicability on anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing laws.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

As part of its financial and operational due diligence, the AE has assessed Pakistan's "Pakistani" in the area of financial regulations, including anti-money laundering and counter-terrorism financing laws and government systems, equipment management, and the ability to comply with donor requirements. The AE will assess whether their overall policies and procedures are consistent with these laws and ensure that they are in compliance with them.

The AE's Subsidiary Agreements with the EE will require compliance with the AE's and the EE's anti-money laundering and anti-terrorist financing policies described in the Funded Activity Agreement and A&A and will now

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

302. The project will comply with GCF's Environmental and Social Policy (ESP), including the Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) and the Gender Equality Policy (GEP). It will also be in compliance with the Pakistan Water Act 1976, as detailed in the Sustained Integrated Projects and Procedures (SIPP) Guidance Note on GBA in SE Asia. The project will be designed to integrate integrated approaches to flood and drought risk management in the Indus Basin while enhancing ecosystem services and biodiversity conservation through the implementation of a pilot project for Ecosystem-Based Adaptation in Pakistan. It is expected to generate significant positive and durable social, environmental, and economic benefits.

**Page 154**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 151 OF 156

## Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) (Annex 6), including an Indigenous Peoples Participation Plan (IPPP) (Annex 7)

### Standard on the Protection of Natural Habitats

This standard is triggered by the need to protect natural habitats, which include revegetation of riverine and wetland habitat, excavation of flow paths and water channels, creating water retention areas in river systems and de-silting of rivers. The ESS will be prepared for each project area, including a plan for re-vegetated, preparing a proper plan for the excavation and dumping of excavated material, and where possible / necessary, moving the cattle / herds along on the existing buffer / retention areas to avoid digging trenches in the landscape.

### Standard on Restriction of Access and Resettlement

This standard is triggered by the possibility of access restriction to fishing or use changes in water uses if accumulated. A resettlement plan will be prepared for each project area, including a plan for re-vegetated, preparing a proper plan for the excavation and dumping of excavated material, and where possible / necessary, moving the cattle / herds along on the existing buffer / retention areas to avoid digging trenches in the landscape.

### Standard on Indigenous Peoples

This standard is triggered because there are indigenous and tribal peoples in several project landscapes—the Indigenous Mohanas of Lake Manchar and Kharal, Mor, and Pakhtoon tribes in the DFO Khan area. An Indigenous Peoples Participation Plan (IPPP) will be created as part of the ESS process. When implementation begins and landscape-level ESS Screenings have been completed, IPPPs will be created through an EPPC process for each project area. The EPPC will include a review of the proposed project activities against the criteria set out in the FP, and tribal elders have expressed their interest in participating in the project. More details about the consultation process with indigenous peoples and stakeholder engagement during implementation can be found in Annex 7 Stakeholder Engagement Plan.

### Standard on Community Health and Safety

This standard is triggered by construction activities involving small-scale construction works involving pond retention areas, gabion bunds used to stabilize riverbanks and banks, de-silting of rivers and wetland restoration. These activities may pose a risk to the health and safety of local communities. The ESS will be prepared for each project area with a strong emphasis on ensuring that all workers are provided with appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) by the contractors and that they have been trained in the use of such PPE. The selection of the firm will be required to comply with WWF’s guidance note on Labour Rights and Health and Safety.

### Standard on Cultural Heritage

This standard has been triggered on a precautionary principle only, as there are a few lines of cultural significance within the project area that are not anticipated to be within the range of project activities. The ESS landscape screening during implementation will verify this, and any project activity that may impact those areas will be avoided.

### Gender Assessment

#### 02. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

34. **Gender Assessment:** A detailed Gender Assessment was conducted on gender-related issues in the project site areas to ensure that gender is given due consideration and integrated into the design and implementation of this project. To ensure gender is given due consideration and is integrated into the design and implementation of this project, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed (see the Gender Assessment and Action Plan, Annex 8).

35. The framework for the Gender Assessment was based on an “Assessment Grid” containing 20 questions. A combination of qualitative research techniques were used to conduct the gender assessment and collect gender-specific data to identify gaps in the project design and implementation. The following key areas were covered: (i) Women’s Rights, Sexual Exploitation and Abuse and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) risks; this was to understand the roles women and men play within their communities and how they are affected by the project activities; (ii) Gender-based violence and control over the management of natural resources and the types of resources, and access to land by women and men in the project site areas; (iii) Women’s economic empowerment and access to income generating opportunities in the project site area. To collect secondary data, a literature review / desk study was conducted of the academic literature and secondary sources on gender equality and women’s rights in Pakistan, including the Constitution and international treaties signed by Pakistan. However, the position and status of women in Pakistan have historically been low compared to men due to various factors such as social norms and cultural practices which continue to exist and are constantly changing depending on many factors that can be beyond women’s control. Numerous laws have been enacted over time to protect women from gender-based violence and discrimination; however, these laws often render them ineffective. Therefore, women have generally not benefited from these declarations. As Pakistan is a patriarchal society, women are expected to stay at home and take care of the household chores and children by birth, which leads to their lower human development and more unfavorable socioeconomic circumstances, respectively.

36. **Communities in the project sites:** Communities in the project site areas have been facing the brunt of climate change with their livelihoods being severely affected due to increased frequency and intensity of natural disasters such as floods, droughts, and heat waves. In the focus group discussions, the impact of climate change was explored, and it was reported that there has been a significant increase in the number of people who have migrated from their homes because of these climatic changes. The incidence of malaria and other waterborne diseases is also increasing due to climate change. In the focus group discussions, the impact of climate change was explored, and it was reported that there has been a significant increase in the number of people who have migrated from their homes because of these climatic changes. The incidence of malaria and other waterborne diseases is also increasing due to climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 153 OF 156

**Livelihoods are a reality to different degrees in all four project sites. Therefore, it can be clearly summed up that the climate change has already negatively affected the equation and balance between people and nature.**

Women have been disproportionately impacted by climate change. They play an important role in politics, policies and programs regarding their aspirations and lives. Formal institutions fall short of facilitating women’s access to resources and services. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes over natural physical assets. Informal institutions (family, community, religion, segregation, marriage) define and affirm norms that limit women's participation in public life and political processes, restrict their economic roles, and condone domestic violence. Laws in place to support gender equality and women’s empowerment have been poorly enforced.

In the project area, women literacy level is low as compared to men. Women are fully responsible for livestock rearing, crop production, food preparation, water collection, and child care. They also collect firewood and gather food for their families and have a role in some of the tasks involved in field crops. However, women generally do not participate in decision-making processes over natural physical assets. Women have limited decision-making power over natural and management resources including crops, livestock and associated land.

No report or data sets under the title SEAH (annual exploitation abuse and harassment) were found for Project sites. The project area is characterized by a high prevalence of GBV (gender-based violence). Women are subsumed under the laws and terms of Gender Based Violence (GBV), 'honor killing' and 'domestic abuse'. Women in the project area are subjected to various forms of GBV, including sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment at different levels and at varying degrees in all provinces. The focus group discussions revealed that women are subjected to various forms of GBV such as domestic violence, sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment also exist. Even though direct questions were not asked about SEAH and GBV (gender-based violence) during the focus group discussion, women reported that they experienced sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment also exist. Even though direct questions were not asked about SEAH and GBV (gender-based violence) during the focus group discussion, men usually deny the existence of any aspects of GBV; whereas women recognize it and subtly acknowledge it as part of their daily lives.

### Gender Action Plan

The results obtained through the gender assessment and the stakeholder consultations during project design and development have been used to produce a detailed GAP for the project (see Gender Consultation Report).

- **Equity and inclusivity**: Equitably and inclusively strengthening and building the capacity of resilient and vulnerable communities, including women, youth, and people with disabilities.
- **Building capacities in tasks and interests**: Implementing training for women at separate locations, as appropriate for each site, that focus on subjects that are specifically tailored to women's interests, roles, and responsibilities. The training will be designed to build the capacity of women to participate more fully in leadership and entrepreneurship of the vulnerable groups, addressing constraints and aims and aiming to improve their livelihoods.
- **Ensures gender mainstreaming processes actively involve men and women at different levels of management**, who are responsible for ensuring that gender is considered throughout all stages of the SSSAN and SSSAN reporting mechanism.

### Strengthening the capacities of local Government departments and extension services for orientation, sensitization and mainstreaming for planning and implementing project interventions.

The GAP's implementation and monitoring will be the responsibility of the Gender Manager based in the PAU, and input from the Project Team will be required to ensure that gender is considered at each level of this landscape. This staff will work closely with the community mobilizers (male and female), ESS Manager and M&E Manager to ensure that gender is mainstreamed into all aspects of project implementation. The focus group discussions revealed that women are subjected to various forms of GBV such as domestic violence, sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment also exist. Even though direct questions were not asked about SEAH and GBV (gender-based violence) during the focus group discussion, men usually deny the existence of any aspects of GBV; whereas women recognize it and subtly acknowledge it as part of their daily lives.

(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Pakistan;
(c) In accordance with all relevant environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

5. We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

6. We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP207

Proposal name: Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate change through Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) and Green Infrastructure for integrated flood risk management

Accredited entity: World Wildlife Fund Inc. (WWF)

Country: Pakistan

Project/programme size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project has a high degree of country ownership. The project is one of the two high priority projects in Pakistan and will make a determined contribution. The project also supports the national Indus Basin Initiative. Through the use of retention areas and the protection of embankments and other flood risk areas, the project uses evidence-based approaches to address the challenges of drought and flood. The project is pertinent to the country’s priorities for long-term drought and resilience to floods. | Critical to the success of the project will be the formulation of an effective exit strategy that ensures the sustainability of projects and maintenance of extant activities. There are concerns about the feasibility in devising a scaling-up and replication strategy for the project's interventions would be advanced. The AIF must affirm to the Government of Pakistan the financing commitment towards operation and maintenance of the project’s interventions during implementation. |

The information is based on scientific studies, inclusive planning, hydrological assessments and climate analysis. The success of this project could be replicated in other locations throughout the country. The development of a proof of concept will be important for the development of the sustainability strategy and scaling up to other projects in the country.


---
**Page 166**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

The objective of the project is to transform Pakistan’s approach in flood and water resources management. The main focus of the project is on implementing ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) and green infrastructure interventions on the ground in four watersheds sites in the Indus Basin: Dera Ismail Khan, Chakar Lehri and Lake Manchar.

Annual average temperature has increased by 0.5°C over the past century with annual mean temperatures rising in recent years, with significant increases in the Sindh and Balochistan provinces. The projected increase in Pakistan’s annual mean temperature is likely to be roughly in line with the global temperature rise. Beyond a rise in temperature, climate change is already having a significant impact, including changes to the magnitude and frequency of extreme weather events such as floods and droughts. These changes will lead to an increase in the frequency and magnitude of impacts such as droughts and low flows by the end of this century. The projected increase in temperatures will also reduce water availability (primarily for agricultural and industrial purposes) will limit access for people and vulnerable communities, particularly those living in rural areas. This will have a negative impact on freshwater ecosystems, and the magnitude and occurrence of extreme weather events such as floods and droughts.

According to future climate change projections based on all four Representative Concentration Pathway scenarios of the Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), Pakistan’s annual mean temperature is projected to be approximately 1°C higher than the global average changes by 2100. Such an increase in temperatures will have a significant impact, including: (1) an increase in the intensity and frequency of extreme precipitation events; (2) an increase in the number and severity of extreme events (i.e., floods, droughts, low river flows, heatwaves, cyclonic activity, etc.), and (3) a reduction in water availability.

The accredited entity (AE), WWF or WWF-UP, has undertaken hydrological analysis as well as socio-economic studies for the project. The AE will continue to engage with communities on the ground to ensure that the project is addressing their needs and concerns.

The project aims to address the following barriers:

(a) Lack of proofs of concept for EBA and green infrastructure as viable adaptation interventions in Pakistan;

(b) No clear guidelines for EBA and green infrastructure interventions are not included in Pakistan’s National Water Policy, national adaptation plan and provincial adaptation plans; and

(c) Inadequate technical capacity across government and communities for implementing EBA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan.

**Page 167**

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Proof of concept for EBA and green infrastructure interventions as efficient and effective tools to address flood and drought risk reduction in Pakistan (total cost: USD 27 million; GCF cost: USD 9.9 million)

### Output 1.1: EBA interventions implemented to restore floodplain and watershed ecosystems

### Output 1.2: Green infrastructure interventions established in Pakistan's Indus Basin

### Output 1.3: Strengthened community-based natural resource management to support water supply and flood attenuation services provided by EBA interventions

#### General context: Proof of concept for EBA and green infrastructure in Pakistan (total cost: USD 27 million; GCF cost: USD 9.9 million)

### Component 2 will improve the capacity and enabling environment for climate action by using Outcome 1's evidence of the effectiveness and efficiency of EBA and green infrastructure


---
**Page 168**
---

interventions in improving flood and water resources management to expand Pakistan’s regulations and policies. National, subnational and community leaders and representatives will be trained to use the modified procedures. This work will allow the Government of Pakistan to meet its commitments under the GCF, and replicate similar adaptation measures in other flood- and drought-prone areas of Pakistan.

15. Component 2 comprises the following outputs:

(a) Output 2.1: Evidence base to support the adoption of EBA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan (USD 0.3 million)

(b) Output 2.2: Updated procedures for implementing EBA and green infrastructure in Pakistan (USD 0.5 million)

(c) Output 2.3: Strengthened capacity for implementing EBA and green infrastructure interventions in the Indus Basin for flood risk and water resources management.

Component 3: Enhanced community resilience and adoption of EBA and green infrastructure interventions to improve livelihoods in vulnerable communities in the Indus Basin (USD 10 million)

16. Component 3 will increase the resilience of community livelihoods in the Indus Basin by training communities and smallholder farmers on how to improve the existing agricultural practices, as well as how to adopt new EBA and green infrastructure interventions under future climate change scenarios through improved irrigation practices to conserve water, improved soil management techniques, and the adoption of drought-resistant crop varieties or new crop varieties that require less water. In addition, new opportunities for sustainable enterprises will be created in the Indus Basin, including the establishment of community savings for communities in the Indus Basin and to reduce ecosystem degradation caused by traditional livelihoods such as livestock grazing. This component will also include the development of project pipelines, the incubation of environmentally sustainable businesses and the provision of technical assistance will help to reduce ecosystem degradation and increase revenue generation for households.

17. Component 3 will be achieved through the two key outputs:

(a) Output 3.1: Pipeline of feasible climate-resilient businesses; and

(b) Output 3.2: Improved climate-resilient livelihoods among vulnerable target communities.

Monitoring and evaluation costs (total cost: USD 2.2 million, GCF cost: USD 1 million)

18. This cost will cover the relevant activities required to monitor and evaluate the project’s progress against the agreed indicators of success, including the achievement of the GCF outcome and project level, and with respect to co-benefits. The cost is about 2.9 per cent of the total cost of the project.

Project management costs (total cost: USD 3.6 million, GCF cost: USD 1.8 million)

19. This cost will support the management, coordination and implementation of the project activities, in compliance with the contractual obligations that are included or referred to in the legal agreements between the Government of Pakistan and the GCF. The cost is less than 5 per cent of the grant portion of requested funding from GCF and is compliant with the GCF policy on fees.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Impact potential
Scale: High


---
**Page 169**
---

The project will directly benefit 687,314 people, of which 48 per cent are female. The number of indirect beneficiaries is estimated to reach 7,902,501 people, which accounts for 3.5 per cent of the total population of the country.

The direct benefits will be as follows:
- (a) Direct reduction in flood risks and impacts on populations close to the intervention sites (within 3 km) and for people living downstream;
- (b) Green infrastructure interventions: localized slowing of flood waters and the subsequent reduction in peak flows, thereby reducing risk of flooding events; and
- (c) Community-based adaptation interventions: increased climate resilience by 50.75% of the most vulnerable smallholder farmers in the project sites.

The EBA interventions will provide mitigation co-benefits from reforestation, reference to Table 3.1. The total estimated carbon sequestration is 26,450 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂ eq) per year, with the total lifetime removals estimated at 1,897,928 tCO₂ eq.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: Medium to high

The project creates a paradigm shift in Pakistan's food and water resources management by providing new models of concept for EBA and green infrastructure interventions in the Indus Basin that validate the cost-efficiency, benefits and sustainability of these additional measures. The project will also provide a replicable model nationwide EBA interventions to manage flood risk, as a complement to grey infrastructure, and mainstreaming EBA into water, agriculture, natural resources management and disaster reduction strategies.

The AE is working with partners to seek financing to support the project to scale up and replicate across the country to ensure the expansion of the Recharge Pakistan initiative.


### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
Scale: High

The proposal will contribute to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 14 (Life below water) and SDG 17 (Partnerships for the goals).

The proposal aims to deliver environmental, social and economic co-benefits as shown below:
