### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### Project background
The project aims to create integrated approaches to flood and drought risk management in the Punjab Basin in Pakistan to strengthen the climate resilience of the most vulnerable rural communities while enabling a paradigm shift towards Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA).


---
**Page 173**
---

Based on the AFM’s assessment, the project is categorized as medium risk against the AE’s safeguard integrated policies and procedures. The ESMP provides general information about the profiles of the project components, including their geographical location, the archetypes of project interventions and a related methodological framework for their further screening. The ESMP also includes an overview of the baseline data collected for the project's initial scoping, the project’s environmental and social risks, and the planned mitigation measures. The ESMP outlines baselines, including indicators for assessing the biophysical impacts of the proposed interventions. The ESMP is a living document that will be updated as more information becomes available.

The ESMP also includes a conflict and security assessment to delineate the project's conflict and security-related risks and potential impacts. Given the complexity security situation in the region, the ESMP identifies the areas with high levels of insecurity and those most affected by the conflict areas, which are considered as the AE at medium security risk zones. The project site in Chakwal district is located within a medium security risk zone. The area has undergone inter-tribal conflicts and conflicts between law enforcement agencies and the separatist movement. The assessment notes sporadic incidents of violence and attacks in DI


---
**Page 174**
---

### ES3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The ESMF identifies that the works involved in the EbH and green instruction solutions, such as excavation of soil, debris, and other obstructions, may lead to the generation of waste material. The project will ensure that all waste generated is disposed of properly and that improper disposal of excavated material could lead to polluting the landscape and degrading the natural environment. To prevent this from happening, the project will implement a site-specific ESMF before the commencement of the relevant activities. The project will use the soil from the excavation as fill material for the construction works in accordance with the terms of the EIA solutions. In terms of potential pollution caused by pest use, the project will exclude activities that may lead to the generation of waste material that could be contaminated or persist as persistent organic pollutants or hazardous chemicals. Although the project will not directly promote the procurement and use of pesticides, there are chances that the project interventions, such as the introduction of new agricultural practices or a change in agricultural practices, could lead to the procurement and use of pesticides. The ESMF includes a mitigation measure for this risk which is to ensure that all relevant activities follow an integrated pest management to be followed during project implementation. The site-specific ESMF covers the project's further safety and addresses the project’s potential environmental impacts.


---
**Page 175**
---

### ES3: Community health, safety, and security

The project is expected to benefit the health and safety of the local communities by reducing the risk of water directly flooding their agricultural lands, assets, and infrastructure. However, local communities may be exposed to risks related to the project activities such as increased sedimentation in water bodies and lead to potential reduction in fish catch. Some project activities may reduce the access to water for irrigation purposes, especially for communities living downstream of the Indus River. There is a risk that some project activities may also increase the risk of land degradation and conflicts due to the restriction of access to land or resources and due to the selection of project beneficiaries. The project will mitigate these risks by implementing appropriate measures such as preventing adverse impact on water quality and supply to affected communities; ensuring the proper disposal of hazardous materials, including waste from construction activities; minimizing the use of hazardous materials; following disease prevention procedures; and implementing the project in a manner that minimizes conflicts with local communities. To further minimize or creating community conflicts, the project will further screen the risks and potential impacts on communities through the identification, design, implementation and monitoring of project activities related to the mitigation measures required for the project.

The process framework (PF) provided in the ESME, affected communities will participate in the identification, design, implementation and monitoring of project activities and the related mitigation measures. The PF also provides a framework for ensuring that the project has minimal impacts on communities. As the exact impacts of the project on community health, safety and security will be further determined during the detailed design phase of the project, and as part of project implementation, the AE is suggested to ensure site-specific assessment and mitigation measures and the communication with affected communities.

### ES5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project will exclude activities that lead to private land acquisitions, physical displacement, and voluntary or involuntary resettlement. The process framework (PF) provided in the ESME identifies that the project activities may involve novel or rigorous restrictions on the use of access to natural resources such as water, land, and forest. If this is the case, certain interventions, such as those pertaining to cleaning up the lake, could lead to changes in tenure arrangements for communities who depend directly upon these resources for their livelihoods or resources. The AE has triggered its own standard on restriction of access and resettlement and developed a process framework (PF) to guarantee transparency and equity in the planning and implementation of such activities. The PF outlines the processes for assessing the impacts on the resource-dependent local communities and their livelihoods. The PF outlines the processes for identifying the project’s direct, indirect, and cumulative impacts; delineating the prospective negative impacts of the restriction on the encompassing community; and developing appropriate mitigation measures to address these impacts.

The AE will ensure that economically displaced communities receive compensation benefits and development assistance as required by national laws and regulations. The PF also outlines how people are to be restored and improved livelihoods; and setting out the participatory monitoring arrangements and a grievance redress procedure to resolve possible disputes on restrictions on land use and access to resources. The AE will ensure that the project activities are designed in such a way as to identify the exact social impacts of the project and address such impacts through site-specific ESME mitigation measures. To further minimize or creating community conflicts, the project will redesign the specific activity/activities so that a LRP will no longer be needed.

### ES6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project is expected to produce significant conservation benefits while harmonizing with other land uses in the area. However, some project activities could negatively affect areas of critical natural habitats or any breeding ground of known rare/endangered species, and activities that result in the loss of biodiversity, alteration


---
**Page 176**
---

of the functioning of ecosystems, and introduction of new invasive alien species. The SDF identifies four types of activities considered by the project that can adversely affect biodiversity and the integrity of terrestrial or aquatic ecosystems, if not managed properly. That is, revegetation with exotic tree species may lead to a loss in native vegetation richness and ecosystem function; planting of non-native tree species being planted; the construction of retention areas or rain ponds may affect local biodiversity; while dredging can improve water quality in lakes but wetlands are often lost as a result. The project will also be required to manage sedimentation, and that temporarily increased sediment concentrations can disturb aquatic life; and trenching may lead to habitat destruction and loss of riparian vegetation. Sedimentation is expected to increase during the construction phase and then decrease over time as water quality improves.

Screening will be conducted at the site-level to screen out activities that are not consistent with the project’s environmental and social management framework, and that involve the procurement and/or use of pesticides and other chemicals specified as persistent organic pollutants (POPs) under the Stockholm Convention. The Environmental Management Plan (EMP) is suggested to identify the potential impacts on biodiversity and natural resources per each activity. The EMP will also be used to determine whether any additional mitigation measures are required for design, selection, and implementation of site-specific interventions.

The analysis of the project investment should also be informed by the environmental baseline to be developed at each site and country level.


---
**Page 177**
---

**ECS: Cultural Heritage**

The project will exclude activities that can negatively impact areas with significant cultural heritage value for individuals and communities. While no significant impact on the cultural heritage is expected from the project, the pre-feasibility study findings indicate that the Lani and Gomol River diversion point near Koi Azam may have its own standard on cultural resources to safeguard the project's potential impacts applicable to both physical and intangible cultural heritage.

**5.4 Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)**

The GCF Revised Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision BIM-2021/18 requires safeguarding from SEAH in GCF-financed projects. The policy defines sexual exploitation as the use of a person's sexuality for financial or material gain through transactional sex, commercial sex, or other forms of exchange-based gratification (GBV), honor killings and domestic abuse. During gender assessment consultations, it was noted that the project area has a high risk of GBV/SEAH. The action plan included some GBV/SEAH mitigation measures in the gender action plan. The AE has a zero-tolerance approach to sexual harassment and exploitation. It recognizes that the achievement of biodiversity conservation is vital while promoting gender equality, and makes every effort to ensure that the project does not contribute to or exacerbate GBV/SEAH. The project will identify any risks that give rise to discriminatory and exploitative gender inequalities. The AE has a zero-tolerance approach to sexual harassment and exploitation. It recognizes that the achievement of biodiversity conservation is vital while promoting gender equality, and makes every effort to ensure that the project does not contribute to or exacerbate GBV/SEAH.


---
**Page 178**
---

## Implementation arrangements

The ESMP describes the management structure and the roles and responsibilities related to project implementation and safeguards implementation.

While the GCF will provide guidance on how international best practices and standards are applied and met, WWF Pakistan, as the EE, will be responsible for the implementation of the ESMP. The EE will ensure that all parties involved in the implementation of and compliance with the ESMP: A Project Management Unit (PMU), comprised of safeguards-related experts from WWF Pakistan, will be established to oversee the overall implementation of and compliance with the ESMP. The PMU will also supervise the implementation of the ESMP and other safeguard plans at the site/landscape level. It will also be responsible for the implementation. The PMU will be accountable to a Project Coordination Unit (PCU) comprised of representatives from WWF Pakistan, GCF, and the project entity. The PCU will provide guidance on how international best practices and standards are applied and met.

## Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The project underwent extensive consultations with key stakeholders respectively during the project design phase and during the ESMP preparation phase. During the project design phase, a series of workshops were organized to discuss the project concept and its potential impacts. These consultations focused on identifying the potential adverse impacts of the project with mitigation options as well as assessing the potential benefits and how these can be enhanced through the implementation of the project activities. All the stakeholders consulted were supportive of the planned project activities.

A SAE has been prepared which summarizes the key findings from all stakeholder consultations, including the detailed information on specific safeguard issues where needed. In addition to the ES Safeguard Implementation Plan (ES-SIP), a separate Stakeholder Engagement Plan will be responsible for the on-the-ground implementation of the ESMP and related plans once they have been finalized.

## Grievance redress mechanism

The GRM provides the principles for the development of a project-specific grievance redress mechanism that will enable the project's safeguards personnel within the first six months of the project implementation. The project-based GRM will be operationalized, in addition to and independently from the EE’s own internal GRM system. This will ensure that all complaints are addressed through the GRM, which will enable all complainants going straight to the PMU with a whistle-blower mechanism for those who wish to remain anonymous.

In addition to the project-specific GRM, complainants can submit a grievance to the EE’s independent third-party Grievance Redress Mechanism (GMRM). The GMRM is an external body that will be set up by the project team, hosted by the AE. The project will provide information of the GMRM available to the public and the general public through various channels including the website and during consultations.

All the consultation sessions will be conducted in the local language of the area, and the EE will ensure that all parties involved are adequately informed about their rights as set out in the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. The GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.


---
**Page 179**
---

# Gender policy

## 4.2 Gender policy

### 38. The AIF provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal, thereby complying with the requirements of the SGP Gender Policy.

### 39. Women’s participation in Pakistan over the last three decades has been fluctuating and has seen many structural factors/changes over time. The existing numerous laws put in place pertaining to women’s rights have not been effectively implemented due to lack of political will and lack of political will is still a challenge. Women’s participation in decision-making processes remains a challenge, and formal institutions, including private and public institutions, fall short of fulfilling their obligations towards women.

### 40. Informal/community institutions, including religious spaces, define and reinforce child marriage and other harmful practices. The National Commission for the Protection of the Rights of Women (NCPW) has reported that 25.4 percent of women experienced lifetime physical violence in Pakistan. The prevalence of domestic violence is also different for women and men. A shrinking natural resource base has led to migration and shifts in livelihoods, which have a disproportionate impact on women.

### 41. The gender action plan for the Recharge Pakistan project was based on the results of the gender assessment, outlining gender-specific actions against the five thematic areas of the SGP Gender Policy. The gender action plan will be integrated into the overall project design and that stand-alone gender activities are carried out. The gender specialist will provide technical support to the AIF in developing the gender action plan and facilitate various capacity needs assessments and training at the onset of the project implementation phase. The AIF will also coordinate (on other outputs) with the National Commission for the Protection of Women, Pakistan (NCPW), which is a government body based on Gender Equality, vocational training institutions and government agencies and line departments at the national level to ensure that gender considerations are included in all relevant resource mobilization plans.

### 42. The gender-specific activities that will be implemented under outcome 1 include: (a) integrating gender considerations into the project design and implementation; (b) promoting the adoption of plant tailings from nurseries rated by local women; (c) formation of women’s groups on natural resource management, including water harvesting and rainwater harvesting; (d) training of women and men to understand what climate change is, building their leadership skills so that they will be able to lead in the implementation of climate-resilient agriculture practices; (e) linking into women’s groups on maintenance and irrigation (OMM) of green infrastructure. Under outcome 2 the AIF will (a) ensure that gender considerations are included in the resource management plan; (b) provide technical support for the development of a gender action plan; (c) sensitise relevant government line departments on gender and women’s empowerment in EBA across the project area; (d) establish a provincial-level steering committee, including the Provincial and provincial oversight committees, which will facilitate mainstreaming gender in initiatives and projects at all levels. The AIF will also provide technical support for similar programmes. Finally, under outcome 3, the AIF will (a) ensure that women and men have access to climate-resilient agriculture practices; (b) ensure that women’s groups are established to ensure that women and men are trained on climate-resilient agriculture practices covering both drought and flooding scenarios; and (c) ensure that gender issues are mainstreamed in the implementation of irrigation systems, including rainwater harvesting, water storage tanks, efficient irrigation systems, kitchen gardening, fruit orchards, fodder collection and water storage tanks (if applicable); (d) provide technical support for women’s groups on sustainable entrepreneurship; and (e) implement gender-specific diversified income-generating activities including animal husbandry, poultry farming, fish farming, horticulture, beekeeping, and post-harvest processing along with value addition), horticulture, poultry farming and agriculture.

## 4.3 Risks

### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

**61:** GCF is requested to provide as US$40 million for way of a grant, which will finance green infrastructure components under Fisk approach at the four project sites for flood risk management. The AE is providing co-financing of USD 1.8 million in kind and the United States Agency for International Development (USAID) is providing co-financing of USD $5 million each in the form of a grant. The total funding amount to USD 77.8 million.

**62:** Government of Pakistan is unable to provide any co-financing for the project owing to the diversions of funds from the budget for other projects and the country’s fiscal situation in 2021-2022.

**63:** The AE is requesting a full grant support because Fisk and green infrastructure are often not considered to be economically viable in view of the high transaction cost, and the market is skewed towards hard infrastructure without the consideration of wider benefits from EBA. The analysis of the project’s financial viability has been conducted by GCF and it has concluded that recent floods have exacerbated Pakistan's liquidity and external credit weaknesses.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

### WWF-US will serve as the AE for the project and will provide GCF proceeds to the EE.
The AE will manage project oversight, including technical, financial, and administrative monitoring and supervision (through reporting, audits and annual site visits) and review and approval of the EE's yearly workplans and budgets.

- WWF-US will be responsible for coordination under the international WWF network and a separate legal entity from WWF-IS, will serve as an EE for the project. The EE will be responsible for managing the project with its partners, beneficiaries, recipients of goods or services, procured parties and their activities, and reporting to the AE.
- WWF-US will also provide support, guidance and backstopping to the EE, monitoring the achievement of project outputs and outcomes, providing technical assistance and evaluation. WWF-US will conduct these responsibilities and disburs GCF proceeds to the EE in line with the approved workplan and budget. The EE will be responsible for having the capacity to execute the project and ensured that the EE is capable of applying WWF-US and GCF standards and policies in the execution of the project.

### Project Management Unit (PMU)
The PMU will be led by the Project Director and operate under the guidance of the Project Steering Committee described below. The PMU will report to the Project Manager, who will also be responsible for overall project management activities including oversight of day-to-day activities, coordinating with the national government and project partners, coordination with the AE, monitoring progress of contracted project activities and coordinating project execution across the four provinces.

The AE will advance funds to the EE through quarterly disbursements based on specific milestones achieved. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring compliance with WWF standard grants management policies. WWF-Pakistan will set up a dedicated project account to

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

The funding proposal is an adaptation project focusing on two results areas of the Integrated Results Management Framework: (i) climate change vulnerable people and communities; and (ii) ecosystems and ecosystem services. Through key interventions in BBA, green infrastructure and conservation, and through the national level and at Pakistan, the project aims to benefit a total of 7.0 million individuals, with 0.7 million direct and 6.3 million indirect "beneficiary" adaptation interventions.

The monitoring framework uses the templates and provides contents in line with the Integrated Results Management Framework requirements. The sections on Theory of Change and logical framework have been aligned with the suggested structure and format. A continuous feedback mechanism has also been established to ensure alignment of the logic model in terms of identification of results areas, indicators, means of verification, targets, assumptions, etc. They are all consistent with the project’s theory of change and the monitoring and reporting of project results. The information provided in different sections of the logical framework are clearly operationalized and will help to solidify adaptation methodologies, tools and practices for the communities in the country.

The assessment of adaptation benefits and beneficiaries is a strength of the funding proposal. A methodology has been proposed for the estimation of adaptation beneficiaries which provides a description of various categories of direct and indirect beneficiaries. The direct and indirect beneficiaries are distinguished under a sound assumption. The methodology has allowed for the identification of different groups of people, including vulnerable approaches to reach them during implementation, as well as monitoring and reporting on adaptation beneficiaries.

Implementation timetable: the implementation timetable has been provided in the required structure and format. The milestones and deliverables that are envisaged at different stages of project implementation have been reasonably planned. Adherence to the proposed implementation timetable will facilitate smooth implementation of the project as well as timely delivery of the results at planned intervals.

(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all permits/licenses needed by it; and

(c) Delivery by the Accredited Entity to GCF of a certificate or a legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, issued by the Executing Entity and addressed to the GCF Secretariat, confirming that the Executing Entity is authorized to implement the Project in the Host Country; and

(d) Completion of the GCF Secretariat's legal due diligence on the Funding Proposal.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP207

## Proposal name: Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate change through Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) and Green Infrastructure for irrigation and flood risk management

### Accredited entity: World Wildlife Fund, Inc. (WWF)

#### Country/ies: Pakistan
#### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

1. The proposed project will take place in Pakistan, a country characterized by a diversity of ecosystems, climate zones and landscapes (approximately 40 per cent rough mountainous terrain). Agriculture is the main economic activity with an economy long based on agriculture. Pakistan is now considered a semi-industrialized country with a growing service sector. However, Pakistan faces significant socioeconomic development and environmental protection challenges. The numbers of people living below the poverty line (21%) are higher than in India (17%), China (8%) or Bangladesh (30%); 45%, 69% and 71% respectively. Most of Pakistan’s population, estimated at 225.1 million, live in the Indus River basin, an area highly exposed to climate change.

2. The project targets two catchments of the Indus River basin, with interventions at four sites, namely Dera Ismail Khan and Ramkot located in the Bhit Kathiawar plain; Kharo Chan and Khushab located in the Thal desert; and Shandur and Saindak respectively in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province, and the Balochistan and Sindh provinces, the Indus River basin. These sites are characterized by high vulnerability to climate change impacts and communities are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, and because there are opportunities for adaptation benefit through ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) and green infrastructure development. The project will directly benefit 105,248 people in the selected sites.

3. The total cost required to implement the project over its 7-year duration is USD $7,848,396, including USD $6,000,005 in the form of a grant from the GCF. The remainder will consist of USD 10 million from United States Agency for International Development (USAID) and the Government of Canada, as well as in-kind contributions from WWF Pakistan estimated at USD $848,391.

4. Pakistan’s vulnerability to climate change is largely due to its geographical location and socio-economic conditions.

### Impact potential

Scale: Medium to high

1.2


---
**Page 188**

## The project interventions will directly benefit an estimated total of 407,238 people, including 517,614 men (52% per cent of the total population) and 129,622 women (48 per cent of the total population), as well as indirectly benefiting 7,024,361 people, including 5,656,644 men (80.5 per cent of the population) and 3,373,693 women (48 per cent of total population) in Pakistan's Indus basin.

### By strengthening the adaptive capacity of institutions and stakeholders at different administrative levels, the project will help to reduce flood risk and drought vulnerability and consequently have positive impacts in the management of large-scale floods and droughts in the targeted regions. The project will also support the construction of check dams and other sites to redirect and contain the flow of water, in order to minimize downstream flash floods and to facilitate infiltration to replenish groundwater resources and help address droughts. These interventions will be complemented by capacity building for disaster risk management, rehabilitation of watersheds, floodplains and wetlands, and green (and grey-green) infrastructure development. The project will also support the implementation of climate-based adaptation interventions that will support vulnerable smallholder farmers to adopt drought-resilient agricultural practices, including improved water harvesting, sustainable irrigation and soil management, and to enhance irrigation through new water storage tanks.

### The proposed project should also contribute to changes in the regulatory and procedural framework for flood risk and drought vulnerability reduction by promoting land and water resources management strategies (e.g., expanding flood and water resources regulations and procedures) that are consistent with climate change adaptation interventions. The implementation of the regulations and procedures developed under the project could help to reduce flood risk and drought vulnerability, and consequently contribute to positive impacts on the socio-economic systems facing the socioeconomic development challenges posed by floods and droughts in the targeted regions.

### Adaptation impact potential is rated as Medium to high.

#### 13.0 Paradigm shift potential: Scale: Medium to high

The proposed project aims to bring about a change in approach to planning for the management of flood risk and drought vulnerability in Pakistan. Results will be most likely achieved through the implementation of the Eha and green infrastructure interventions in other regions of the world, it intends to foster an anticipatory and integrated approach to the management of floods and droughts by promoting land and water resources management strategies (e.g., expanding flood and water resources regulations and procedures) that are consistent with climate change adaptation interventions. In addition, to facilitate this change in the approach of the Government of Pakistan, the project envisages setting up a new coordination structure, the National Working Group, Chaired by the Ministry of Climate Change, which will be responsible for coordinating the implementation of its executive department, the Federal Flood Commission, as well as the relevant provincial departments and other stakeholders. The project also aims to support the development and adoption of the procedures integrating the interventions promoted by the project.

The proposed project will create an enabling environment for the long-term adoption of flood risk management strategies in Pakistan. It is expected that the project will provide evidence on the efficiency and effectiveness of Eha and green infrastructure interventions, and promote their replication at national level to reduce flood risk and drought vulnerability in Pakistan. In addition, the project will contribute to building community capacity on the issue. All this is likely to encourage the adoption of the proposed interventions by other stakeholders.

### Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

23. Pakistan is very exposed and vulnerable to floods and droughts, and it is ranked as the eighth most flood-prone country in the world according to the Global Climate Risk Index (2000–2019). The exceptional floods of 2010 and 2012 clearly demonstrated the country's


---
**Page 191**
---

great vulnerability to these extremes, slow-onset climatic events, which are set to intensify with climate change. The aforementioned floods caused considerable material and human damage, forcing the country to devote its meager resources to repairing the damage caused. For example, in 2019, the government had planned to invest PKR 35 billion in flood mitigation originally envisaged for the proposed project; as resources have been diverted to emergencies linked to the major floods of 2022.

Potential future climate risks, as well as those related to loss and damage caused by adverse weather conditions induced by climate change, are enormous. They are estimated at several hundred billion rupees annually. Given Pakistan’s limited fiscal space and its inability to create a Ministry of Climate Change, has funds in the national budget for adaptation actions, it is unable to meet the immense needs in this field because of the urgent actions required by other development challenges and the national emergency resulting from the impacts of climate change (particularly floods). The country is also not able to access international funding. As a result, the country is obliged to turn to international public funding sources such as the Green Climate Fund.

21. Although climate change information and adaptation responses such as EBA exist, Pakistan’s water resources sector is still unable to integrate them into its planning processes, making it difficult for the country to adapt effectively to climate change. The increasing climate changes into water resources management is due in particular to a weak institutional capacity of the sector to address these challenges. To overcome this challenge, the project will address these shortcomings/gaps by implementing activities such as EBA and green and grey-green infrastructure development, and by improving the enabling environment to scale up such activities elsewhere in the country.

24. Needs of the recipients is rated as High.


### 1.6 Country ownership Scale: High

The proposed project contributes to a set of objectives and priorities defined in the National Climate Change Policy, the framework document for addressing the current and future climate change impacts on Pakistan’s development. The project will contribute directly to the objective of pursuing sustained economic growth by appropriately addressing the challenge of climate change. More specifically, outputs 2.1 and 2.2 contribute to the objective of integrating climate change considerations into national planning processes; outputs 3.1, 3.2 and 3.3 contribute to the objective of focusing on pro-poor sensitive adaptation; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3 and 1.4 contribute to the objective of enhancing institutional capacity for climate change adaptation in the face of challenges posed by climate change; outputs 1.1, 2.1, 2.2, 2.3, 2.4 and 3.2 contribute to the objective of reducing vulnerability to the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as floods, droughts and tropical storms; output 1.5 contributes to the objective of enhancing institutional capacity for climate change decision-making and coordination mechanisms on climate change; and output 3.3 contributes to the objective of facilitating effective use of the opportunities, particularly financial, available both nationally and internationally under the Paris Agreement and other international agreements. The National Climate Change Policy, the National Wetlands Policy, the National Disaster Risk Management Framework, the National Water Resources Policy, and the National Forest Policy are all relevant.

26. An extensive and inclusive stakeholder consultation process was used to formulate the proposed project. These stakeholders included the Ministry of Climate Change; representatives from national government departments (e.g., the Federal Board of Revenue, the Federal Board of Revenue (the Federal Flood Commission), the National Disaster Management Authority and the Ministry of Planning Development and Special Initiatives; provincial government departments covering

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: Medium to high

On the basis of the results of the cost-benefit analysis carried out in the context of the proposed project, the package of interventions envisaged is cost-beneficial. The total project investment is estimated at USD 10 million, which represents a net present value (NPV) ratio of 1:1 and an economic internal rate of return of approximately 13.6 per cent.

The project has been designed to ensure the availability of funds to absorb effects after the end of fiscal year 2022, but the AEF is able to make a small contribution and to secure an additional USD 10 million in co-financing from Coca-Cola and USAID, bringing the GCF to its full amount.

To inform the design of EBA and green-and-green-grey infrastructure interventions, the proposed project includes a series of preparatory activities including an optimised set of adaptation measures that are called both "EBA" and "green-grey", but which are not all "narrowly greengrey", and can be considered to include soil stabilisation, erosion control, water harvesting, sedimentation basins, dispersal embankments, gabion baskets, retention areas and recharge basins.

In addition to the information gathered during the stakeholder consultation process, the results of the feasibility study carried out by a Chinese company (from China) and Afghanistan greatly guided the design of the project's interventions. Indeed, the approach used in the case of the Dushanbe project was followed for most of the green infrastructure and EBA interventions considered by the proposed project.

According to the Working Group II contribution to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Special Report on Global Warming of 1.5°C, the "underestimation of impacts considerably but there is medium agreement regarding their magnitude" [Donaldson et al., 2014; Nolte et al., 2018b], and "limited evidence and low agreement on the economic efficiency and long-term benefits".


---
**Page 193**
---

term effectiveness? This issue was raised during the question and answer session with the AE. In response to the question the AE provided a list of peer-reviewed literature and reports to support the efficacy results of the proposed interventions. However, some of them did not directly address the issue.

Given that the interventions envisaged will be screened again as indicated in the project document, it would be important to take into account further scientific references confirming their effectiveness. These include studies on the availability of groundwater resources of the Indus basin and the potential for aquifer recharge using monsoon floodwaters.

Furthermore, establishing evidence of the effectiveness of the Eha and green infrastructure interventions is a fundamental element of the proposed project. However, the implementation of these interventions will be based on the technical guidelines that will be developed. According to the answer provided in the questions submitted by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) during the above-mentioned question-and-answer session, this aspect will be taken into account from the earliest stages of the proposed project. With this in mind, it is strongly recommended that the AE present a detailed plan for the implementation of these interventions.

Efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project is rated as Medium to High.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Based on the above explanation, ITAP endorses the funding proposal "Recharge Pakistan: Building climate resilience to enhance water security through Ecosystem Adaptation (Eha) and Green Infrastructure for integrated flood risk management".

The ITAP recommends the following improvements to the funding proposal:

(a) The formulation, from the very first stage of the proposed project, of a realistic and sustainable strategy and plan for mobilizing resources for actions integrating Eha, green and grey infrastructure.

(b) The formulation, at the early stage of the project implementation, of a clear protocol for assessing the efficiency and effectiveness of Eha and green and grey-green infrastructure interventions.

(c) Making an additional effort to take into account scientific and technical references in the design of the Eha, green and grey-green infrastructure interventions; and

(d) The use of appropriate mitigation measures (e.g., construction of detention ponds resulting from the probability of standing water), it is important to carefully reassess these risks and that ensure appropriate mitigation measures are put in place.

Considering the nature of the proposal has largely satisfied all the GCF's operation criteria, the above recommendations are left to the discretion of the AE.

# Preface

For the project team that has delivered this assessment, it was much more a labor of love than a routine consulting assignment. The core team gave much more time and energy than originally intended to ensure that the assessment was as comprehensive and accurate as possible. Exploring the subject was very close to our hearts. In addition to their vast combined experience in water, environment, gender equality, and women’s rights, all members of the core team have been actively involved in the global and Pakistani women's movement and in women-in-research initiatives for many years. They are not only experts but also long-time activists in these areas to inform and enrich this assessment.

# Executive Summary

The World Wildlife Fund (WWF) / Green Climate Fund (GCF) on behalf of and in collaboration with the Government of Pakistan, is preparing a proposal for the project Recharge Pakistan: Building Resilience to Climate Change through ecosystem-based adaptation for Integrated Flood Risk Management.

In this context, the WWF-Recharge Pakistan project has conducted a Gender Analysis and developed a Gender Action Plan to ensure that gender mainstreaming is integrated into the design process of the project.

The project's proposed activities will be implemented under three components:

- **Component 1:** Propose concepts for EBA and green infrastructure interventions as efficient adaptation measures in response to climate change, particularly in Pakistan.
- **Component 2:** Enable a paradigm shift towards EBA and green infrastructure in Pakistan.
- **Component 3:** Enhance community resilience and adoption of EBA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan's Indus Basin.

As a result of WWF-Pakistan’s engagements during Concept Note and Funding Proposal development with national (MoCC and MoWRI), local (provincial irrigation departments) and international partners, the project has secured co-financing from the GCF. The total amount is US$7 million in government co-finance from the NDRM (~US$37 million) and MoWRI (~US$29 million) committed to the project to complement the GCF grant. While “US$10 million” was initially proposed as co-financing, the remaining amount of the project to complement the GCF grant, the recent 2022 floods have required that these funds be repurposed for immediate flood recovery and response efforts in Pakistan. The Government of Pakistan can commit significant adaptation finance at this time. Despite these constraints, the GCF remains committed to the proposed project but is unable to co-finance any co-financing for the project as it has already been committed to other projects with higher costs recovery from the 2022 floods.

As a result of the above mentioned co-financing no longer being available for the project, the project has been scaled down from 6 sites (Manchar, Khairana, Taulak, Chakar Leh, Di Khan and Ramsak) to four sites (Manchar, Chakar Leh, Di Khan and Ramsak). This gender assessment was undertaken in order to ensure that the project is gender responsive. The framework for the Gender Analysis was based on an Assessment Grid with 26 questions, outlining the secondary and primary data requirements. The following steps were designed and implemented:

- **Development of Assessment Grid**
- **Literature Review and Secondary Data covering the status of women in Pakistan, global and regional context, gender equality, EBA and climate change-based interventions (including sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment) situation, policies of relevant sectors, examples of relevant projects, women’s participation and leadership in the environment sector, impact or climate change, and participation and women's leadership in WWF**


---
**Page 205**
---

* Development of Assessment Tools and Guidelines (household survey, focus group discussions [FGDs] and key informant interviews [KII])
* Pre-Testing and Finalization of Tools / Guidelines
* Data Collection through 15 FGDs, 527 households and 10 key informants
* Analysis and Reporting
* Gender Action Plan

### Status of Women in Pakistan

The position and status of women in Pakistan has a checkered history. Over the last three decades, there have been numerous legislative initiatives aimed at improving their standing, varying changes depending on many structural factors. Numerous laws have been promulgated ensuring equality for women but these are not implemented effectively due to lack of resources which renders them largely ineffective. Very little attention is placed on the crucial nexus of culture, region and policy development and implementation of laws. The plethora of laws, negative socio-economic conditions, and patriarchal attitudes in society continue to be a major reason why they that consistently favor men and place women at disadvantage.

Due to some of the above-mentioned reasons, women in Pakistan (or have inadequate) capabilities to participate effectively in education, decision making processes, political/governance spaces, and in policies and programs. Formal institutions include both private and public sectors, which are dominated by men. Women lack access to physical capital i.e. education, skills, health facilities, loans and physical assets. Informal institutions thereby become the main source of livelihood for women. These institutions have a bias towards marriage/childhood over education and employment, prescribe gendered roles, and perpetuate domestic violence/gender based violence. Laws have therefore not had the expected impact.

The World Economic Forum Global Gender Gap Report of 2022 found that Pakistan is the second worst country in the world in terms of gender parity; ranking Pakistan 145 out of 146 countries surveyed.

Pakistan is among the countries that are most vulnerable to the risks associated with climate change and ranks 8th on the Global Climate Risk Index 2021.

Pakistan's women are further disadvantaged because 9.5% of the women agricultural workers pay a cost for their work, compared to only 3.4% of men. Women have had their rights largely ignored, suffer from greater time poverty, and are far more vulnerable to exploitation than men. Women’s ownership of land, and control over physical assets is minimal: only a percentage of women own a homestead.

Unfortunately, the recent Gender Policy Framework (March 2022) of the Government of Pakistan needs to do more close to the gender gaps and propel the levels and scope of action required in order to achieve parity. The framework has been criticized for its lack of detail; that the recent floods of 2022 “serve as a case in point, pushing back many of the gains made by the women’s movement over the past two decades.” It lacks strong governance, weak response mechanisms and plans that are made without the gender lens.

The experiences of the 2005 earthquake, and the floods of 2010 and 2011 have shown that the patriarchal attitudes in society continue to be a major reason why they favor men and victimize the women instead. Instead, become even stronger in emergencies. The floods of 2022 have once again


---
**Page 206**
---

exposed women to increased risk of gender-based violence and discrimination which could lead to sexual abuse, harassment, exploitation, and early and forced marriages. The United Nations Population Fund* noted that there are almost 650,000 pregnant women in flood-affected areas who need immediate assistance. Women living with disabilities and those women are particularly vulnerable and face numerous health and safety risks. With the lack of adequate infrastructure and limited access to essential services and goods, women and girls will be largely cut off from this support they need.

However, the presence of the National Commission on the Status of Women, Provincial Commissions, the Women’s Caucus in Parliament and NGOs working on women’s rights, gender equality and human rights can provide a platform for addressing GBV and SEAH. The project should make links with these organizations and work with them proactively to develop strategies and plans that will ensure that women and girls are not left behind. Local government offices, extension services, law enforcement and justice offices will be key to helping women access to adequate services and support.

Gender Based Violence (GBV), Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH)

According to Global Database on violence against women, 24.5% of women experienced lifetime physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence between 2017 and 2018. There is no confirmed national level comprehensive statistics on GBV and SEAH. Post-2022 floods in Pakistan have increased the risk of GBV and SEAH, especially for women and girls living in multi-lateral donors, but no specific study has been conducted that focuses purely on GBV and SEAH. The National Commission on the Status of Women (NCSW) in Pakistan has also be said safety that SEAH is subsumed under the terms Gender Based Violence (GBV), ‘honour killing’ and dowry related violence. These are all forms of GBV. GBV is often linked to maintaining power structures, traditional norms, customary laws, and access to resources, all resulting in the oppression of women and gender inequalities.

Another dimension of GBV is that environmental pressures on the environment and its natural resources increase gender inequalities and power imbalances in communities and households coping with resource scarcity, climate change, food insecurity, water scarcity, land degradation, deforestation, biodiversity degradation, climate change and the impact of COVID-19 has led to an increase in GBV and SEAH.

In terms of legislation. Pakistan is signatory to international conventions, and has many progressive and supportive laws that could provide holistic migration to and address GBV and SEAH. However, the implementation of these laws is often hampered by weak enforcement. The lack of effective implementation of these laws and the counterproductive nature of some Ordinances counter the progressive laws a thus setting back any progress already made. The net effect of this situation is that women are not protected from GBV and SEAH. Awareness of these laws in relation to gender issues can be measured according to the Gender Integration Continuum given below. The scale has been coined to measure the level of responsiveness of legislation to gender equality, with 1 being least responsive and 5 addressing specifically climate change, insecurity (GBV and SEAH) and gender nexus.

*<https://pakistan.unfpa.org/mn/news/women-and-girls-bearing-brunt-pakistan-monsoon-floods>


---
**Page 207**
---

# Gender Integration Continuum

| FEMINIST INCLUSIVE | GENDER IGNORANT | GENDER SENSITIVE | GENDER RESPONSIVE | GENDER TRANSFORMATIVE |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Unequal gender roles and norms are upheld. | Ignores unequal gender roles, but does not address discrimination. | Recognizes unequal gender roles, but does not actively address them. | Recognizes unequal gender roles and actively addresses them through activity combat. | Addresses the deep roots of unequal gender roles and norms. |

Based on the Gender Integration scale, the following policies and laws are advantageous and supportive to the project.

| Low | Advantage | Gender Integration |
|---|---|---|
| Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP) | CCGAP addresses gender-specific issues in agriculture, food security, biodiversity, climate change, water and sanitation. | Gender Responsive: The policy is an integral part of the country’s National Climate Change Policy Framework. It provides a framework for interventions specific to those covered under the Philippine National Climate Change Action Plan (PNCCAP). |
| National Gender Policy Framework | The policy identifies and maps the major employment and gender gaps in a country. | Gender Responsive: While the policy gives a general overview of gender equality, it does not address the challenges. |
| Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act 2013 | The law identifies that 80% of the female victims of violence are from internally displaced persons (IDPs) and internally displaced women in barangays are sexually harassed. | Gender Responsive: While the law does provide protection for women, it does not address SHAR at workplaces. |
| Domestic Violence Act of 2013 (Republic Act No. 9262) | The Domestic Violence SOI makes violence against women a criminal offense and provides for its investigation and prosecution. | Gender Responsive: The law is responsive to the needs of women in the community, but places more on reporting and punishment than on name of victim. |
| Killing Laws Amendments Bill | The law criminalizes the act of violence against gender and sexual minorities. | Gender Responsive: The law is responsive to the needs of women in the community, and their household and country in general. |

The focus group discussions have revealed the existence of various forms of gender-based violence in the field sites. Even though direct questions were not asked about SEAH to communities, the discussion has brought out that there are many forms of gender-based approach using trained psychologists due to the sensitive nature of these questions), physical violence, emotional and psychological abuse, sexual harassment, and other forms of violence within their community. Disrespect for women has also been cited which indicates a deeper insidious side of violence against women. This can be seen in the lack of respect that men have towards women and their families. The focus group discussions have revealed that there is a need to bring real change to the lives of the women in the community, and their household and country in general.


---
**Page 208**
---

Reducing violence and SEAH against women and girls requires a community-based, multi-pronged approach, and sustained engagement with multiple stakeholders. To be effective these initiatives will have to address social norms on gender roles and the acceptability of violence in relationships, including within traditional feudal societies, of the kind where WWF is attempting its Recharge Karachi initiatives.

Community engagement is vital to reduce GBV and SEAH and identify victims and survivors that need help. In order to do this community members (men, women, elders, youth, religious figures, etc.) must be engaged in a participatory manner. This can include community leaders and journalists, local health personnel, school teachers etc have to be sensitive to the issues, supportive of GBV/SEAH survivors and able to refer those who need help. More roles may also be found: for example, nearest lawyers, legal aid centres, shelters etc.

GBV and SEAH are challenging yet necessary to address. The reasons are ingrained in traditional social norms, lack of access to justice, economic programs, laws and policies, and increasing poverty. Redress needs actual action and systems on the ground, not just training or awareness raising. GBV/SEAH survivors have multiple intersecting vulnerabilities that need immediate shelter (sometimes for month or years), medical support, legal aid, economic support, community support, rehabilitation, education for their children and psycho-social support. The organization believes that a holistic approach is needed, either in one program or cross-cutting approach to cover these components. However, the organization believes that GBV/SEAH survivors should be protected from violence and abuse regardless of whether populations are protected by the state. Despite GBV and SEAH being critical to women rights, prevention and response mechanisms for GBV and SEAH are not well developed in Pakistan. It is believed that preventive measures are in place to mitigate future GBV and SEAH related instances in the project area. The organization will work with partners to identify specific actions to proactively recognize and address issues related to GBV and SEAH.

GBV and SEAH will be addressed in the GCF process through various strategies and recurring thematic experiences. The team will focus on ensuring that all partners are engaging with participants (to influence better laws), especially the Women’s Caucus and women parliamentarians. This team should also engage with the National Commission on the Status of Women (NCSW) and other relevant stakeholders such as the Pakistan Institute for Human Rights (PIHR), the Human Rights Commission (HRC) and the Provincial Commissions on Human Rights (PCHRs). The GCF will provide a strong basis for training, capacity building and awareness raising in GBV/SEAH. The GCF will also have SEAH cases. The GCF and WWF’s own policies and documents form a strong basis for training, capacity building and awareness raising in GBV/SEAH. The GCF will also have GBV concerns in its projects. Other strategies to ensure challenges relating to GBV and SEAH the project are: (i) the team will engage with the PMU, who has a strong resource specialist in the PMU, who has strong and proven expertise in working on issues of GBV and SEAH/HR; (ii) the GCF will provide a strong basis for training, capacity building and awareness raising in GBV/SEAH. The GCF will also have SEAH cases. The GCF will be the resource person in the project who will conduct needs assessment, provide training (including recruitment/trainerment trainings) and support capacity building of all project staff including community members; (iii) the GCF will provide a strong basis for awareness raising, project implementation, which will be reported through the gender-responsive and survivor-centred approach to GBV/SEAH in the context of the project. (See Section 13.3 & Annex X for detailed guidance on SEAH Risk Mitigation).

Gender Considerations in Global & Pakistan Climate Change & Environment Policies

The challenges of climate change impacts, building resilience and countering gender inequalities are all interlinked.


---
**Page 209**
---

By exacerbating inequality, climate change slows progress toward gender equality and thus impedes efforts to achieve wider goals like poverty reduction and sustainable development. Conversely, gender inequality can worsen the impacts of climate change and further propel the former. Therefore, it is essential that governments and other stakeholders work with civil society groups to narrow the gender gap and empower women who can help counteract the impacts of climate change.

Women continue to face serious challenges amid threats to climate degradation, particularly in occupations that are dependent on natural environments or geographic locations and where climate stress may cause natural hazards. Agriculture, water, and forestry are among the sectors most affected by climate change. Women are more likely than men to work as full-time labor or secondary workers in addition to their dependence on natural environments for livelihoods. This makes them particularly vulnerable to climate change-induced migration, and frequent natural disasters triggered by climate stress produce a precarious survival situation for women.

As women are disproportionately impacted by the adverse effects of climate change, Pakistan ratified the Paris Agreement (2015) which mandates gender equity and justice in climate action by member states.

The Ministry of Climate Change. Government of Pakistan has developed the Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP) in partnership with International Union for the Conservation of Nature (IUCN). Pakistan. The CCGAP demonstrates the commitment by the Government of Pakistan to mainstream gender equality into its climate change planning and programming, with the priority sectors identified in the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) of 2021. It is an important step toward achieving a key objective of the NCCP to focus on pro-poor gender sensitive adaptation.

The National Climate Change Policy updated 2021 is the current policy which has a specific focus on gender equality and women empowerment, recognizing that women are disproportionately vulnerable, and that rural women are likely to be strongly affected by climate change. Much progress has been made in setting ambitious targets for adaptation and mitigation, there is recognition of the need to address the disproportionate vulnerability of women and girls with differentiated burdens in climate stress. The recently updated National Climate Change Policy and the NCCP 2021 have a strong focus on gender equality and women's differential burden in climate emergencies and how to help them.

Therefore, it can be said that the policy environment is feasible for positive reinforcement of gender mainstreaming in Pakistan’s climate change policies and programs. The GCF and WWF's gender policies are both good instruments to guide the design, and implementation of the intended WWF project in Pakistan.


### Socio-economic Profile of Communities

Any gender assessment requires a socio economic and environmental context within which gender issues can be understood correctly and the most suitable and effective interventions developed. This is particularly true for women who are often excluded from decision-making that is community based where, in addition to national and provincial situation, the local poverty levels and social norms play an important role.

The Socio-economic picture that emerges of the communities in the six WWF sites shows clearly that they are very poor, indebted and live from hand-to-mouth. They are not much used to accounting for money handling and are not very accurate in terms of their income and expenditures.


---
**Page 210**
---

We also see that mostly men are involved in work that is usually paid (when they get the work), women usually look after domestic chores and are unpaid. In some instances, women and men work together on small-scale enterprises or agriculture, but even then, women often do not report working in any professions or carrying any jobs. While men left their communities for labor work elsewhere, women tend to remain.

The low numbers of women respondents also suggest that they do not spend money of any kind exclusively on themselves. Women do not have much say in decision-making or household spending, and may not even be aware of how much is being spent by the men of the family on their livelihoods.

Most of the people there reported unemployment while men and some women have skills and professions (traditionally through the decades), currently they do not have a means of income. The traditional economy that has sustained these communities for generations (source of income (fisheries, agriculture, livestock and handicrafts)) is dwindling and degrading – through climate change and other factors. Given this context, designing livelihoods with socio-economic profiles of the poorest communities in the six project sites will be a challenge, so that earning opportunities for men and women have to be an integral part of the design of the project.

### Gender Roles in the Context of Local Ecosystem

Climate change has already negatively affected the equation and balance between people and nature. The rapid changes are visible and the feared migrations, poverty and loss of livelihoods are real possibilities that need to be addressed now.

The impact of climate change in the form of changing weather patterns and extreme temperatures are changes that are a reality for the women and men in the project sites. Adaptation strategies will have to be developed to address these challenges which need to be designed with the participation of the community including the women. Shrinking natural resource base has led to migration and a shift from traditional livelihoods to manual labor. The women in particular, are aware and mind their own survival struggles for survival. Alternative livelihoods are now needed.

The tendency of local landowning elites to misrepresent their ability to manipulate resources in difficult times means that meager environmental and ecosystem resources will continue to be denied to the landless and the poor, especially women.

WWF has to be careful in the way the gender dialect is used, because it can lead away from the plight of both poor men and women in the face of elite capture. The focus should not only be on building gender parity between community women and men, but in providing interventions that are equitable for all. This means that WWF must work with communities and poorly resourced government institutions that render poor men and women bereft of their traditional livelihoods and access to resources. It is important that the existing entitlements remain and they continue to access environmental resources for their water and food security.

As for the government, it is clear from the interviews of functionaries that they are mostly clueless on gender terminology; policies and actions would require a lot of attention in the intended project design and implementation of the Gender Action Plan.


---
**Page 211**
---

# Gender Mainstreaming in WWF

WWF already has a strong Gender policy and follows the GGF Gender Policy Guidelines.

These already cover most very important gender issues for gender mainstreaming.

WWF also has several projects that focus on women and support women's empowerment, but gender data is scattered over different reports and websites. An Annual Gender Report covering all the current projects with disaggregated data, number of male and female beneficiaries, etc., should be developed to provide a comprehensive picture of progress for gender mainstreaming in future WWF projects.

However, it was seen that women staff members were not fully aware of these resources or used them effectively. A training module on how men and women should be developed and integrated into the orientation process for both existing and new employees. Gender concepts, WWF's gender policy, and gender analysis tools should be included in this training.

Gender analysis training in designing gender projects, collection of data, and reporting should also be provided to all staff members. This will increase the knowledge and capacity of the staff for gender responsive and gender transformative programming.

A gender focal person designated to ensure that gender policies are translated through the organization at all levels and to organize and deliver the required training would be very helpful.

And finally, there are several women in WWF at different strata of the organization, but only a few in leadership and decision-making positions. Therefore, these women should be trained and provided with opportunities for moving towards senior positions, and a clear career path should be offered to women as they progress within WWF.


# Gender Action Plan

Based on the gender assessment results, stakeholder consultation recommendations, and the Kit, the project aims to mainstream gender using a gender-responsive approach throughout all four project sites. The following are some of the key activities required in the four project sites to ensure the project interventions are gender-responsive:

# Background

The World Wildlife Fund (WWF) / Green Climate Fund (GCF) on behalf of and in collaboration with the Government of Pakistan is implementing a project: Recharge Pakistan - Building Resilience to Climate Change through Watershed Restoration for Integrated Flood Risk Management. This project brings a paradigm shift towards managing water resources, particularly in the Indus River Basin. The Indus River, the lifeline of water-stressed Pakistan causes regular floods exacerbated by climate change, and the country relies on expensive infrastructural flood management measures with limited efficacy.

# Gender Policy Statement

The GCF Accredited Entity requires that all GCF projects comply with WWF Gender Policy in the design and development phases as well as in their action plan. WWF adopted its Gender policy in 2011, and this policy reflects WWF's ongoing commitment to ensuring gender equity and integrating a gender perspective in its policies, programs, and projects, as well as in its own institutional practices.

The WWF Global Network Policy: Gender Policy Statement 2011 states that "the policy statement is to ensure that WWF’s conservation policies, programs and activities benefit women and men equally. The policy statement also outlines WWF's commitment to strengthen the social dimensions of its projects, programs, and policy work." It describes how WWF will integrate gender equity into its conservation goals, footprint goals and outlines WWF commitment to integrate a gender perspective in our programmatic and operational structures and procedures.

The policy outlines WWF’s belief in promoting gender equity and integrating a gender perspective throughout the organization. The policy statement is designed to ensure an organizational culture and work environment where management and other staff are aware of discrimination and responsible for preventing it at work.

# Introduction

In this context, the WWF Recharge Pakistan project has conducted a Gender Analysis and developed a Gender Action Plan to ensure that gender mainstreaming is integrated into the design phase of the project, which reflects WWF's continuing commitment to its policy and future action.

The Recharge Pakistan Project has a three-pronged approach which includes: ecosystem-based flood management through wetland restoration, hill torrent management, and green infrastructure interventions; enhanced groundwater recharge through climate-smart, climate-adapted livelihoods, and risk reduction; and a paradigm shift to scale up the Ecosystem-Based Flood Management approach. The proposed project will have multiple, long-term, wide-ranging benefits for Pakistan, including:

a) EBA that improves resilience to flooding and drought;
b) Increased groundwater recharge which can be more easily store and use floodwater;
c) Enhanced recharge of groundwater through wetland restoration interventions, and hill torrent management interventions, e.g. watered restoration, check dams, and terracing.

# Gender Assessment Approach

A Gender analysis is a methodology to examine the differences in roles and norms for women and men within communities, the different levels of power they hold, their differing needs, constraints, and opportunities, and the impact of these differences on their lives. Gender analysis also attempts to explain the mechanisms by which gender inequality is maintained or exacerbated.

The aim was to identify opportunities for both men and women in relation to climate change adaptation and promote more equitable management of and benefits from, the use and protection of natural resources.

To conduct a Gender Analysis and develop a Gender Action Plan, a gender assessment of the project sites was conducted to collect gender-specific data to identify gaps, opportunities, and challenges related to gender equality. The gender assessment was conducted in the Recharge Pakistan project sites. Decision-making processes among women and men at the community level were examined with respect to access to land, water, and other forms of resources; access to land by women and men in the project area; how benefits from projects and other opportunities are shared as part of the implementation of the gender analysis.

The framework for the Gender Analysis was based on an Assessment Grid™ prepared by IRS Global. This Grid had 25 questions to be answered by the assessment. A literature review of the status of women’s rights in Pakistan, including domestic violence, dowry and harassment - SEAH; women's participation and leadership in the environment sectors; impact of climate change, and participation and women's leadership in WWF. Primary data collection then followed.

## 1.4 Research Methodology

Both quantitative and qualitative data collection techniques were employed, using research tools such as a household survey, focus group discussions (FGDs) and key informant interviews (KIIs). The details of the development of the Methodology are given below.

### 1.4.1 Development of Assessment Grid

An Assessment Grid to conduct the Gender Analysis was developed at the beginning of the assignment. This grid was used as a tool for data collection, and it developed a template to be followed for the assignment. All the questions to be answered were identified, as were the sources of


---
**Page 214**
---

primary and secondary information. Methods to access the information and data collection instruments were also developed and the Assessment Grid was shared and agreed with WWF (Annex A1).

When the Gender Assessment Grid was discussed with WWF, the question of exploring gender-based violence in the context of flood risk management was raised by the team. The research team conveyed to the WWF team that to ask direct questions on these sensitive issues in small communities through household survey or in focus group discussions would spread rumors and cause panic among women and men. It was suggested that the assessment team will close ranks. It was suggested by the assessment team that a separate investigation should be conducted for gender-based violence, which could require additional budget. WWF advised the research team to go ahead as planned with indirect questions through FGDs and use existing reports for overview.

### 1.4.2 Literature Review

A literature review was conducted of the academic literature and development sector reports covering legal frameworks, laws, policies, institutional practices on gender equality, women’s empowerment in the wider socio-cultural context, that will support and guide the gender mainstreaming process. The following key projects and documents were used for document analysis: the National Gender Policy Framework 2022; the Climate Change Policy 2012; Reaching the Marginalized Women (RMW) Project; and the Community-Based Adaptation for Integrated Flood Risk Management, WWF Annual Reports 2019, 2020, and 2021. The literature review was also guided by the Gender Assessment Grid (Annex A3).

### 1.4.3 Development of Assessment Tools and Guidelines

Based on the Assessment Grid the following tools were developed:

- **Gender Assessment Family Survey Form**
- WWF Household (HH) Survey Tool
- Interview Guides for Government Officials
- Focus Group Discussion Guide for Men and Women (including members of community groups or community-based organizations, if existing in the area)
- GID Guide for Moderators
- Guidelines for Moderator, Note Taker and Login in charge.

All the research tools were developed in English and relevant tools were translated into Urdu. All survey tools are presented in Annexes A5 – A8. Consent forms in English and Urdu were also used to ensure that all participants gave their consent before being interviewed.

### 1.4.4 Pre-Testing and Finalization of Tools / Guidelines

The tools and guidelines were pre-tested, and changes incorporated. The pre-testing was carried out in Halig Lake and around Manchar Lake in Sindh. This was also an opportunity for training of the research team members.

The pre-tested tools were shared and discussed with WWF team members in several online meetings and then finalized in a meeting at the WWF office in Islamabad.

# Chapter 2: Status of Women in Pakistan

## Constitutional Rights of Women in Pakistan

According to the Constitution of Pakistan all citizens are equal and there should be no discrimination on any ground whatsoever. All women are guaranteed fundamental rights and are considered equal citizens in all respects.

### Laws in Pakistan

All laws in Pakistan include the Constitution of Pakistan (1973), all current statutory laws, including Islamic laws; personal laws covering other religions; India-British treaties made by the federal and provincial governments of Pakistan; and Muslim Family Laws Ordinance 1965 (a very progressive law). Shariah laws introduced under General Zia-ul-Haq, as well as the international conventions and treaties to which Pakistan is a signatory, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), Beijing Platform for Action, ILO Conventions, and Child Rights Convention) and other human rights treaties that have been signed and ratified.

## Pakistan as Signatory to International Conventions and Treaties

As a UN member state, Pakistan is signatory to the 2030 Agenda - 'In Sustainable Development Goals' (SDGs). The country has also ratified CEDAW. Pakistan stands among one of the first countries to formally endorse the 2030 Agenda through a unanimous parliamentary resolution in 2015. Pakistan is also a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action, ILO conventions, and Child Rights Conventions, all directed to ensure women's rightful place in society.

The Alternative CSO report to CEDAW 2016 states that Pakistan has passed numerous laws and formulated policies on gender equality. However, these laws are often either lacking enabling laws, regulations, rules, and action to be effective. And the WEP Global Gender Gap 2021 report indicates that in reality the status of women in Pakistan remains low.

## Women's Quotes
