# Women’s Rights Policies and Legislation

The history of laws affecting women’s rights and empowerment in Pakistan involves a complex pattern of progress and setbacks. The state's efforts to articulate a definition of women’s rights complicated by the need to balance divergent views on the place of women in Pakistani society.

In 2002, the first National Policy for Development and Empowerment of Women was formulated, a landmark for securing women’s rights, and ensuring statutory provisions for gender equality. The policy is based on the principles of human dignity, equality, and justice. It lays down the guiding principles, gives key policy measures specifically addressing the empowerment dimensions in the social, economic, and political fields. The policy is a statement of intent by the government to ensure that women enjoy equal rights with men for development and empowerment of women with the policy providing the guidelines and will ensure in the overall framework of governance of the government that a gender perspective is reflected in all national policies and plans.

This Policy has not been updated, but it still provides a historical perspective of the recognition for women’s rights in Pakistan.

At the policy level various other progressive laws supporting women's empowerment have also been passed. These are: The Anti-Sexual Harassment Act; Anti Women Practices Act; Acid Crimes and Control Act; National Commission on Status of Women Act; and the Domestic Violence (Prevention, Protection and Redressal) Act. These laws aim to protect the rights of women for equality in all spheres of life. They also provide a framework for protection against violence for women, as manifested in the laws, policies, and institutional practices on one hand and social norms and practices on the other. The laws have been passed with the inclusion of progressive provisions and some progressive legislation has been enacted e.g.-anti-women practices, 'honour' killing, rape, domestic violence, sexual harassment at work and public places, which


---
**Page 220**
---

all form women’s protection legislation, and other landmark legislation includes the Sindh Home-based Workers Act 2018, Sindh Women Agricultural Workers Rights Act 2019, and the Electoral Act 2017[6].

### National and Provincial Commissions on the Status of Women (NCSW)

Historically, the establishment of the National Commission on the Status of Women in 2002, was another positive measure towards women’s empowerment, since the NCSW is a statutory body with constitutional powers. The NCSW has been recognized by international bodies such as the Government of Pakistan like the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action 1995; and National Plan of Action 2006.

The National Commission on the Status of Women (NCSW) was established with the specific purpose to monitor, evaluate and report on the implementation of policies and programmes for women’s development and gender equality, to review laws, rules and regulations affecting the status of women in Pakistan. The NCSW also aims at providing a forum for discussion of women’s rights and individual grievances, encourage and sponsor research to generate information about women’s issues, monitor and evaluate progress on gender issues, to advocate and maintain interaction and dialogue with NGOs, experts and individuals in society at the national, regional and international level[7].

The NCSW, a custodian of women’s rights did not have a Chairperson at the Federal level, or members at the provincial level since 2013; and the current Chairperson was appointed in July 2021.

Provincial Commissions on the Status of Women are now functioning in all four provinces and six Provincial Districts. These were established for some years.

### National Gender Policy Framework (NGPF) 2022

The National Gender Policy Framework was launched in March 2022, it recognizes the importance of gender equality, and Pakistan’s commitment to the SDGs and Pakistan’s poor status on the WEF 2021 Global Gender Gap Index from 151 place to 153, out of 156 countries. The NGPF aims at addressing the existing gaps in women’s representation across the gender gaps in wages and labor force participation and the negative impacts of covid-19 pandemic.

The report states that increasing economic participation of 48 percent of the national population is the main priority of the Government, while addressing legal barriers for women’s economic empowerment. The NGPF also highlights the fact that the average income of an average woman in Pakistan is 16 percent less than the income of the average man.

In terms of legal frameworks, the document states that considerable progress has been made at the national and provincial level; but effort will have to be made for its implementation, compliance and enforcement. The lack of adequate monitoring, evaluation, and audit of data and monitoring has also led to weak implementation, and an absence of support mechanisms. Poor coordination between different stakeholders is another major challenge.

The document also mentions the persistent gap between women and men in their access to resources, opportunities, and decision-making power within households and communities.

The report identifies six key priorities and policy actions: governance, equality and quality in education, employment and economic empowerment, agency, political participation and meaningful engagement, safety and security, and health and well-being. It proposes to

# Gender Gap in Pakistan

The World Economic Forum Global Gender Gap Report of 2021[25]. Pakistan is the second worst country in the world in terms of gender parity, ranking Pakistan 145 out of 146 countries worldwide. Pakistan has closed 56.4 percent of the gender gap in 2022 — the highest overall level of convergence among South Asian countries.

Pakistan’s GII score is 0.389, which ranks it at 145 on economic participation and opportunity, 143 on health and survival, 135 on educational attainment, and 167 on political empowerment. Pakistan ranked 2nd lowest in the Gender Development Index out of 181 ranked countries and 28th on the Gender Inequality Index[2].

### Pakistan is highly discriminatory towards women

The literacy level of men is 71.1 percent compared to 46.5 percent for women[26], indicating significant disparities in access to educational opportunities and possibilities for meaningful contributions to the economy. Women are less likely than men to be economically active or working age, only 22.8 percent participate in paid labour[20]. Their retention in the labour market is further challenged by competing family priorities, inflexible workplace policies and structural barriers such as lack of childcare facilities, which disproportionately affect women and men in the country. Negative social values, discriminatory legislation, cultural practices, the lack of female representation at all levels of government and absence of political will, continued

with women’s low political, economic, and social status in society, continues to perpetuate a cycle of oppression for women.

As the fifth most populous country in the world, Pakistan stands at 229 million people of which 49 percent are women. However, only 22.6 percent of these women are employed, compared to 84.6 among men. This gender wage gap is even more pronounced when it comes with largest economic gender gap at 31.6 percent. Additionally, only 25 percent of technical roles and 5 percent of leadership roles in Pakistan are occupied by women.

The Gender Wage Gap Income of an average woman in Pakistan is less than 16 percent of the income earned by men. According to a report by the World Bank, every year Pakistani women earn a median wage of PKR 7,000 per month (USD $35) compared to male counterparts who earn a median monthly salary of PKR 42,800 (USD $316). The average wage gap alone costs Pakistan PKR 500 billion annually, and this money is even more valuable because women invest 90 percent of their income back into their families, vs. 35 percent by men instead.

Women’s participation in social processes remains constrained due to the norms that persist though there are variations in their application determined by rural-urban and geographical location. Women’s political participation is also low; political parties have not been responsive enough still not commensurate with their share of the population. Almost 12 million women are not registered voters, while only 30 percent of women (ages 18 years and above) do claim CNICs.

### Conclusions

The position and status of women in Pakistan has a checkered history. In 1987, two women’s rights pioneers wrote a seminal book entitled *Women of Pakistan: Two Steps Forward, One Step Back*. The authors argued that the progress made by women in Pakistan since independence was not linear but rather cyclical. They noted that while the status of women has progressed, remained static, regressed, and constantly changes depending on factors that are beyond their control. Numerous laws have been promulgated ensuring women’s rights and protection from violence; however, the absence of political will renders them ineffective.

While it has been articulated since 1987 very little attention is placed on the crucial nexus of culture, religion, and law in the implementation of laws. The plethora of laws, norms and practices have become concrete in Pakistan's patriarchal society in a way that they consolidate women’s roles around economic subservience.

Women lack exposure concerning knowledge skills, information about opportunities, continued role in politics, and in policies and programs regarding their aspirations and lives. Formal education is not available to all women; the quality of education is poor under capital (e.g., education, skills, health facilities, loans and physical assets). Informal institutions (family, community, religion, segregation, marriage) define and affirm norms that give precedence to men in decision-making processes. Women are expected to assume traditional roles, and condom domestic violence/gender-based violence. Laws have therefore not had the expected impact.

The experiences of the 2005 earthquake and the floods of 2010 and 2011 have shown that the patriarchal norms that shield men from their crimes against women, and victimize the women instead, become even stronger in emergencies.


---
**Page 223**
---

In remote rural settings and under climate change scenarios, WWF would do well to watch out for these tendencies, as they are insidious and ingrained to the extent that women will blame themselves for their vulnerability even if they exult the virtues of women's place at home or not remunerating her for economic work.

Unfortunately the recent Gender Policy Framework (March 2022) is not comprehensive enough to close the gender gaps and propel the levels and scope of action required to counter anti-women violence.

## Chapter 3: Gender Based Violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH)

Human rights and sustainable development are inseparable as the fundamental principle of Agenda 2030 for Sustainable Development. To achieve this goal, we must eliminate gender-based discrimination, violence, and oppression***(Dr. Shireen M Mazari; recent former Minister Human Rights, Government of Pakistan).

While we searched diligence, no report or data sets under the title SEAH (sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment) per se were found for Pakistan.

We looked at definitions of the SEAH components and then tried to find them within other expressions used in the UN reports.

Sexual Violence is used as an umbrella term for sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment as quoted in DIFC UK report 2018**. It includes acts of a sexual nature against one or more persons by another person or persons, whether or not involving force, threat or coercion, such as that caused by fear of violence, duress, detention, psychological pressure, or any other form of constraint or coercion, or the promise of a coercive environment or such persons or persons' incapacity to give genuine consent.

According to the UN, forms of sexual violence include rape, attempted rape, forced prostitution, sexual exploitation and abuse, trafficking for the purpose of sexual exploitation, child pornography, forced pregnancy, forced marriage, forced prostitution, forced public nudity, forced virginity testing etc.

Sexual Exploitation, as defined by the UN, constitutes any actual or attempted abuse of a person's rights to physical integrity through coercion, deception, inducement, economic pressure monetarily, socially or politically from the sexual exploitation of another. It is a broad term, but it includes acts such as prostitution, forced marriage, child pornography and trafficking.

Sexual Abuse, also as defined by the UN, means the actual or threatened physical intrusion of a sexual nature, whether by force under unequal or coercive conditions. All sexual activity with children is considered under the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child as any person under the age of 18 is considered to be sexual abuse, regardless of the age of implied or consent fully.

2) National Gender Policy Framework, Ministry of Planning Development and Special Initiatives Government of Pakistan 2022

Sexual Harassment is defined as any form of unwanted verbal, non-verbal or physical conduct of a sexual nature with the purpose or effect of violating the dignity of a person, in particular women and girls, and creating an environment that they find hostile or offensive.

Gender Based Violence (GBV) is any harm or potential of harm perpetrated against a person's will on account of their gender identity. GBV takes many different expressions of violence including physical, sexual and emotional abuse sexual harassment; stalking; rape; including 'torture'; domestic violence; intimate partner violence; forced marriage; dowry-related violence (FVR); child marriage; human trafficking; and female genital mutilation (FGM)²⁰.

It can be safely said that in Pakistan Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) are subsumed under the broader category of GBV. The term GBV is used to describe sexual abuse. According to a recent study, it is estimated that one in three women and girls globally will experience physical or sexual violence by an intimate partner at some point in their lives. Gender norms and laws GBV and SEAH occur in all societies as a means of control, oppression and exploitation of women that further reinforces gender inequality. GBV and SEAH are also perpetuated through the intersectionality of other factors such as socio-economic status, customary laws, and access to resources.

Another dimension of GBV is the connection with employment and linkages have recently been established between GBV and poverty²¹. Pressures on the environment and its natural resources increase gender inequalities and power imbalances in communities, thereby coping with resource scarcity, climate change, and environmental degradation.


### 3.1 Global Overview

International frameworks, SDG Goal 5 on Gender Equality, and women's rights frameworks such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) have been established to address GBV. However, the intersectionality of the complex issue and culture context-specific which has made it difficult to be effectively addressed to date.

Failure to address this issue also entails a significant economic, social, and environmental cost for the future. Numerous studies have shown that children growing up with violence are more likely to become violent adults²². The World Bank²³ states that gender-based violence affects women and girls of all socio-economic backgrounds; therefore, this issue needs to be addressed in both developing and developed countries.

More than one in three women worldwide experience sexual or domestic violence—more than 1.3 billion women, eclipsing the number of people affected by HIV/AIDS, measles, Ebola, and malaria combined. The idea of a binding norm on violence against women and girls was first

### Green Climate Fund Gender Policy and Related GBV and SEAH Guidelines

In the context of the WWF Recharge Pakistan project, there are several policies available for reference: the Green Climate Fund Gender Policy**, the WWF Gender Policy 2011**, and the WWF Guidance Note on Gender-Based Violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) for Global Environment Facility (GEF) and Green Climate Fund (GCF) Projects**. They all recognize gender relations, roles and responsibilities which influence women's and men's access to resources, opportunities, and information and knowledge.

The GCF Gender Policy is closely aligned with the United Nations' Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 5, which makes explicit commitments to gender equality.

Similarly, the WWF Gender Policy is consistent with WWF’s Mission, Guiding Principles and Code of Ethics. It is an integral part of WWF's social policies and position statements, including indigenous peoples’ rights and gender equality. The WWF Gender Policy supports existing commitments to gender equality for those countries which are signatories to the United Nations CEDAW and the Universal Declaration of Human Rights.

Moreover, the WWF Guidance Note on GBV and SEAH for GEF and GCF Projects establishes a framework for identifying and mitigating gender-based violence (GBV) related G/SEAH risks in projects, and provides suggestions, recommendations, and further resources for project teams working on GBV/SEAH related to projects**. It outlines WWF's


---
**Page 226**
---

General Principles: Respect for Human Rights, Compliance with WWF’s Environment and Social Safeguards Framework, Applicable Laws, and provides definitions for GBV and SEAH which can be recognized in project sites, and activities. The Guidance note identifies potential perpetrators of GBV to identify GBV/SEAH impacts and assess GBV/SEAH risks, and recommends a monitoring plan for all projects, which includes reporting on gender and various safeguards.

### 3.3 GBV and SEAH in Pakistan

In Pakistan, the GBV and SEAH issues continue to remain a challenge despite the passing of integral laws against violence against women and children. The issue of domestic violence, sexual abuse, sexual assault and sexual harassment (SEAH) against women and girls is also included under the definition of Gender Based Violence. SEAH is also included in the term 'domestic violence' and 'honor killings'.

However, monitoring sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) as a challenge as often times, the victims are reluctant to report such incidents because they fear that if such incidents are known to have occurred, denial is common. In police stations, SEAH is often reported under "kidnapping", because that is how the girls themselves prefer the incident to be viewed. The incidence of sexual abuse and harassment (SEAH) against women and children has to be monitored and obtained over a lengthy period of time with families and individuals in a very sensitive situation. There are no reliable statistics on the prevalence of SEAH in this process. Some of the studies quoted here have used such targeted methods to obtain data.

A national study on Gender Based Violence concluded that 'gender-based violence is a living reality in present day Pakistan'[1], despite the multiple actions taken the prevalence of all forms of violence against women and children remains high. The study found that 50% of married female victims according to the Human Rights Commission of Pakistan (HRCP), HRCP also recorded 2,684 acid attacks and 365 incidents of sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) against the same group.

The HRCP Annual Report 2020[2] states that human rights abuses to target women include child marriage and honor killings, which are largely centered on controlling women's agency. The report also highlights that women are more likely than men to be victims of sexual violence and domestic violence. Actual figures for GBV are probably higher as family members are often the perpetrators, and there is a stigma attached to reporting on sexual violence and SEAH.

The Pakistan Demographic and Health Survey (PDHS) 2017-2018 on domestic violence revealed that women who received help from friends or neighbors were more likely to seek help or advice; they were more likely to approach their own or their husband's family. Reporting of gender-based crimes is low because when a woman complains she gets blamed, and the lack of proper legal support makes it difficult for her to report such incidents.

For many women, prevailing attitudes, lack of awareness of support or protection, perceived police inaction, and a lack of confidence in the criminal justice system inhibits them from approaching the authorities.

The National Commission on the Status of Women Annual Report 2018-19 also reports almost 1,000 honor killings every year, and forms of violence include domestic violence, SEAH, forced marriages, and acid attacks.

A study by International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN), Margins for the Future Program (MFF) shows that as traditional means of livelihood from fisheries decline pressures on fisherfolk to search for alternative sources of income arises. And with the loss of livelihood comes a loss in social status, which is often accompanied by a loss of power and the ability to be the provider for the family leads to changing the dynamics at the household level and other forms of violence against women. Thus, women are not equal participants in the fisheries sector just like the agriculture sector.

The National Gender Policy Framework 2022 has provided data on the status of GBV during COVID-19 pandemic with GBV including sexual violence against young adolescent boys and girls has grown.

The fact that GBV is not integrated in social protection or public health responses or education or training makes it difficult to address the root causes of this issue. The lack of comprehensive legal frameworks, inadequate implementation of existing laws, and weak enforcement mechanisms have been identified as major challenges. Thus, there is a need for the implementation of existing laws and developing specific interventions to address the issue of GBV removal.

Projects addressing climate impacts to build resilience, such as Recharge Pakistan, have the ability to build the capacity of women to exercise agency in identifying issues, participate in the decision-making process, and take action. This can lead to improved social empowerment through informed and powerful decision-making. Without the integration of gender equality into these projects, the corresponding risks experienced by such vulnerable groups cannot be deemed effective or efficient.

Gender based crimes, violence against women, and domestic violence have deep social, psychological, and mental effects on women. These destroy a woman’s confidence, her respect in society, and her ability to participate fully as an individual in all spheres of life. The structures which believe that women are subservient to men and have no human rights as an individual.

### 3.4 Legislation on GBV and SEAH

Legislation on GBV against women and girls is reflected in the federal and provincial laws for the protection of women:

- Anti Rape Laws (Criminal Amendment Bill) 2018.
- Child Marriage Restraint Act, 1929.
- Foreigners Act, 1946 and Foreigners Order, 1951.
- Muslim Women’s Dowry Protection Act, 1973.
- Dowry and Bridal Gifts (Restricion) Act, 1976


---
**Page 228**
---

* Anti-Honour Killing Law (Criminal Amendment Bill) 2015.
* Anti-Terrorism Act 1997.
* Prevention and Control of Human Trafficking Ordinance, 2002.
* Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2010.
* The Protection Against Harassment at the Workplace Act, 2010.
* Criminal Law Amendment Act, 2010.
* Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2011.
* The Prevention of Anti-Women Practices - Criminal Law (Third Amendment) Act, 2011.
* Domestic Violence Ordinance, 2013.
* Protection for Women (Criminal Law Amendment) Act, 2016.

The Hudood Ordinances promulgated by a military dictator in 1979 brought in several anti-women laws that became an impediment in reducing GBV and SEAH. Some of the laws listed above were enacted to replace these ordinances. The Hudood Ordinance Act, 2006 repealed the Protection for Women Act 2006 removed rape from the hudood ordinances and put it back in the criminal code.

However, implementation of protective laws remains a persistent challenge. There are limited services such as shelters, helplines, and legal aid for women survivors of violence and SEAH, especially in rural areas. In addition to this, some organizations have focused on establishing helplines, women police stations, hotlines and protection centers that try to support women after they have experienced violence and SEAH.

In addition, GBV and SEAH specific data is inadequate since most survivors do not disclose their experiences or seek help from the authorities. The problems of women who live under plethora of tribal, feudal, Islamic, and Indo-British laws are often so varied and local in nature that they cannot always be solved through solutions exported from other parts of the world.

The problems of women living in urban areas remain difficult and both women's empowerment and gender equality remain a challenge[26].

### 3.5 GBV and SEAH in the Provinces

Sindh

The women in Sindh, like other women of Pakistan, suffer from discriminatory attitudes, laws, traditions and practices in a male dominated patriarchal society. The result is reflected in the low indicators of women's empowerment and gender equality in the province. This has led to increasing numbers of honor killings (mostly for SEAH reasons) and declining legal and social status. Discrimination against women in Sindh results from cultural norms, violence, sexual violence, economic and physical exploitation, restricted social mobility, and psychological abuse[27].

The Sindh Human Rights Commission (SHRC) found in its initial assessment report 2021 that cases of gender-based violence and other human rights abuses increased across the province during the lockdown imposed to restrict the spread of Covid-19. And according to the


---
**Page 229**

# Punjab

In Punjab the Pakistan Demographic and Health Survey 2017-18 indicates that 34 percent of married women have experienced physical, sexual, or emotional violence from their spouses.

The most common type of spousal violence is emotional violence (26 percent), followed by physical violence (25 percent) and sexual violence (19 percent). The survey also found that 70 percent of married women who experienced physical violence, 48 percent of those who experienced sexual violence, and 26 percent of married women who have experienced spousal physical or sexual violence during pregnancy, and 56 percent women never sought help or disclosed GBV due to societal-cultural norms. Only 13 percent of the women in Punjab received health care and psycho-social support services, and in Punjab the prevalence of GBV is 20 percent.

Punjab's Women's Economic and Social Well-being Survey 2018 is the only one in the country that provides a baseline for the province. The other official source is the Pakistan Demographic and Health Survey (PDHS) which does not provide data on violence against women, although it does show married women and a similar percentage of young men think a husband is justified in beating his wife.

# Khyber Pakhtunkhwa (KPK)

In KPK given the traditional social and cultural issues, few GBV survivors seek support in response to violence. Women crises centers are also exist in major cities of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa under the umbrella of Pakistan Women Development Program (PWDP). The Constitution protection mandate Khyber Pakhtunkhwa has a comprehensive mechanism for GBV survivor services and referral mechanisms to other provinces. While the Government of Pakistan has passed various laws to prevent violence, and to protect women from violence.

The conviction rate is very low for violence against women.

Resources and services for women survivors of GBV are minimal sources. When encountered by a case of GBV, health care providers are not aware on how to best respond given that there are few referral and response mechanisms in place to address their immediate or on- going needs. Often the GBV survivor is left with no choice but to return home, which can lead to long-lasting health, emotional and psychosocial problems that survivors face. Such multifaceted challenges faced by GBV survivors are compounded when they seek help from a health facility, knowledge of which is often limited among the staff of the health facility where GBV survivors come for help and support12.

# Baluchistan

In 2014, a breakdown of provinces by various VAW categories revealed that Balochistan accounted for merely 6.28 percent of the overall cases of women's kidnappings, 2.25 percent of abductions, 0.95 percent of overall rape instances, 2.25 percent of suicide, 10.8 percent of honor killings, 6.07 percent of dowry-related deaths, and 34.5 percent of acid-throwing cases and 1.11 percent of miscellaneous other cases of VAW. 3.6 GBV and SEAH in WWF Recharge Pakistan Project Sites

An analysis of WWF background documents indicates that there is no data or information on GBV or SEAH in the Recharge Pakistan field sites. So, this current research will probably be the first to provide such information. The lack of data on GBV and SEAH in Pakistan regarding GBV and SEAH is alarming, and exists in different levels in all provinces, so it can be inferred that the problem is widespread.

Investigating what is related to GBV and SEAH is difficult as it is a sensitive issue and, in most cases, men usually deny the existence of any aspect of GBV, whereas women recognize it subtly acknowledge it as part of traditional family norms. During the focus group discussions, women reported that they experienced violence from their husbands regarding issues which might women face within the home? And what kind of dangers and issues might women face outside the home? Women also mentioned that they were not allowed to go out with friends or relatives and that women faced "danger" within their homes, in contrast to half the number of women who said that they did not experience any danger. This finding reflects the perception of danger and women's sensitivity to possible violence.

Types of violence reported were 'scolding' women, and both men and women acknowledged this, but physical violence was more common among men than among women.

In terms of violence outside the home, both men and women has similar responses claiming that women did not face any danger outside their homes. The possible reason for this is because women's mobility is restricted to the house and women stay at home. An interesting finding from the focus group discussions was that most respondents said women, whereas almost half the female respondents said the men did not respect them.

These contradictions reflect the national data on gender-based violence, which needs to be taken into consideration when planning interventions for the Recharge Pakistan project.

### 3.7 Conclusion

The data from official sources confirms the prevalence of GBV and SEAH all over Pakistan. Unreported cases are probably higher; legislation is not always effective or efficient, and dominant mass attitudes perceive gender-based and sexual violence as acceptable, and women's rights to education and employment are also affected by poverty, environmental degradation, climate change and the impact of c2018 has led to an increase in GBV and SEAH. The government should consider these factors when designing Pakistan project interventions must consider it as designs and implements its program.

In terms of legislation, Pakistan is signatory to international conventions and has many progressive laws on gender equality, including the Hudood Ordinance and SEAH. Unfortunately other laws that remain on the books (such as the Hudood Ordinances) counter the progressive laws

and reflect the mind sets of the law enforcement personnel and people serving in the judiciary.

The net result is that women rarely get the justice and redress they need.


The focus group discussions have revealed the existence of various forms of gender-based violence in the field sites, which means planned interventions should take these factors into consideration. The most common form of gender-based violence is sexual harassment, sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment also exist. Even though direct questions were not asked about SEAH, physical violence has been mentioned by women respondents. Disrespect for women’s rights and their autonomy is a major issue in the field sites. Gender equality needs to be addressed if gender empowerment, and gender mainstreaming is to bring real change to the lives of women.


Reducing violence and SEAH against women and girls requires a community-based, multi-pronged approach, and sustained engagement with multiple stakeholders. To be effective, these initiatives would have to address social norms on gender roles and the acceptability of violence.

Community engagement is vital to reduce GBV and SEAH and identify victims and survivors that need help. In order to do this community members (men, women, elders, youth, religious figures, traditional leaders, etc.) should be sensitized about GBV and SEAH. The police, local health personnel, etc have to be sensitized to the issues; supportive laws, cultural positive norms, and values where violence can be found. For example, nearest lawyers, legal aid centres, shelters, etc.


GBV and SEAH are challenging yet necessary to address. The issues are ingrained in traditional practices and social norms that need to change. These issues are also linked with increasing poverty. Redress needs actual action and systems on the ground, not just training. Women who have experienced GBV and SEAH often require immediate support; they need immediate shelter (sometimes for months or years), medical support, legal aid, economic empowerment, psychosocial support, and sometimes even psycho-social counseling over extended periods. WWFP does not currently have a team or program or cross-cutting approach to cover these components. However, the organization believes that women who are displaced by conflict, disaster, or climate change, and other vulnerable populations are protected by the state. For this purpose, the GUP includes a multi-facile staffing arrangement with a gender focal person at the national level, as well as increased interaction with local communities and key stakeholders at national and sub-national level for implementation of GBV and SEAH.


Pakistan’s women, more than 30 million are agricultural workers and play a substantial role in food production and food security, but are largely unpaid, disadvantaged; suffer from greater time poverty, lower levels of education, and limited access to credit. Conflict and disaster vulnerability only exacerbate these disadvantages. Disadvantage. Recharge Pakistan is limited to scope to address these vulnerabilities in entirety, however, the program has the potential to financially empower women through microfinance schemes, support for small and medium enterprises, and small urban zones to build small business; inclusion in planning for future climate challenges.


The project should therefore dedicate enough resources (and capacities) within the WWFP team working on constantly engaging with parliamentarians (to influence better laws), especially those related to GBV and SEAH. The project should also engage with the National Commission on the Status of Women (NCSW), the Provincial Commissions on the Status of Women (PCSWs), the Human Rights Commission (HRC) and the Provincial

# Chapter 4: Gender Considerations in Global and Pakistani Climate Change and Environment Policies

## Training Manual on Gender and Climate Change, IUCN, UNDP, GGCA, 2009

**Climate change and gender inequalities are inextricably linked. By exacerbating inequality overall, climate change slows progress toward gender equality and thus impedes efforts to achieve the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). The reverse is also true: addressing gender can lessen the impacts of climate change. Meanwhile, taking steps to narrow the gender gap and empower women can help reduce these impacts.**

The nexus between women and the environment emerged on the mid-1980s when research and studies on women's roles, responsibilities, and interaction with the natural resources became more prominent. This recognition has led to a greater understanding of women's position in water management, forests, and conservation leading to a growing body of knowledge about the importance of gender equality for sustainable development. The recognition that women are managers of the natural resource by virtue of being primarily responsible for food production and household needs has been increasingly acknowledged. This has provided evidence and gender has been incorporated into national policies, project planning, and implementation. Gender mainstreaming (GM) was introduced in 1994 under the Global Programme on gender and development (GAD) and more emphasis was placed on recognizing women's roles as managers of natural resources. A greater understanding of women's relationship with the natural resource, and within the debate over sustainable development has also been established.

### 4.1 Global Overview

Climate Change is a persisting global challenge and the nexus between Gender and Climate Change has now been acknowledged and recognized at local, national, and international levels. Women's roles in managing resources have changed considerably and simultaneously the relationships between women and environment have changed considerably and simultaneously the relationship between women and environmental conditions has changed. The impact of global climate change conditions, its impact on people and the way in which they adapt and cope with these changes is increasingly understood at the local level.

Existing studies[1] and research on gender and environment have focused on specific issues such as gender roles in water management, forests etc., and the analytical frameworks to understand how women's vulnerability to climate change has been increasing. The international commitments and gender responsive policy has led to greater mainstreaming of gender into environmental policies at national level.

Policy measures at global and national level have recognized that gender equality is essential to achieving climate change goals, and since 2008, several references to gender have entered the UNFCCC[2] and other international agreements. However, while gender is important for everyone, it is not gender neutral; since most of the poor in the world are women, the impact

### 4.2 Women and Environment Nexus in Green Climate Fund (GCF) and WWF Gender Policies

The Green Climate Fund Policy recognizes the linkages between gender and environment and emphasizes the impact of climate change on women.

"It clearly states that "the impacts of climate change can exacerbate existing gender inequalities. The Gender Policy acknowledges that climate change initiatives are more sustainable when they take into account the needs, rights, and responsibilities of men and women[4]. Women's empowerment considerations are integrated into the design and implementation of projects and programs to ensure that women have access to information on how to adapt to the solution to climate change and should, therefore, be effectively engaged in discussions and decisions that affect them"[5].

The policy reflects the SDG’s commitment to gender equality, the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). It is consistent with international arrangements in particular the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the International Labour Organization's core conventions and other relevant instruments.

Similarly, WWF as a conservation organization also recognizes the linkages between natural resource management, the importance of the sustainable use of natural resources, and the complex interplay between gender equality and environmental sustainability. WWF works to manage natural resources, and how gender relations influence households and communities decisions on access to natural resources.

"The WWF Gender Policy supports the existing commitments to gender equality for those countries which are signatories to the United Nations Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Universal Declaration of Human Rights"[6].

### 4.3 Gender Considerations in Environment, Climate Change and Water Policies in Pakistan

Pakistan is among the countries that are most vulnerable to the risks associated with climate change and ranks 8th on the Global Climate Risk Index 2021.

Women continue to face serious challenges amid threats to climate degradation, particularly in occupations that are dependent on natural environments or geographic locations and where climate change has a direct impact. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change and women are heavily engaged in these sectors as full-time workers. Women are also more vulnerable than men because they have fewer options for alternative livelihoods and rural environments for sustenance. Declining crop yields disrupted water availability, rural out-migration, and food insecurity. The vulnerability of women to climate stress produces a precarious survival scenario for communities, especially women.

Pakistan's women are further disadvantaged because 9.1 million agricultural workers play a crucial role in the country’s economy but are not covered by social security schemes. Women suffer from greater time poverty, and are far more vulnerable to exploitation than men. Women’s ownership of land, and control over physical assets is minimal: only 1 percentage of women report owning land or other property. Women represent just 72% of the total workforce in Pakistan and are disproportionately impacted by the adverse effects of climate change. Pakistan ratified the Paris Agreement (2015) which mandates gender equity and justice in climate action by member states.

#### 4.3.1 Climate Change Gender Action Plan (ccGAP)

The Pakistan ccGAP is designed to strengthen the implementation of national climate change policies and strategies, and to ensure that women’s voices are heard as set out in the NCCP by ensuring that women are included in all stages of planning, development, and implementation of climate action in the core priority sectors of the plan. These sectors are:

1. Disaster Risk Reduction
2. Agriculture and Food Security
3. Forests and Biodiversity
4. Integrated Water Resource Management
5. Water & Sanitation, and,
6. Energy & Transport

Gender considerations are an essential element that can prohibit an individual’s access to resilience by its very nature of dictation by social norms and cultural values in particular areas. In Pakistan, the situation is further complicated because women are not only economically vulnerable but also biologically vulnerable as men to the horrific impact of climate-induced disasters but are deemed so due to the cultural norms, behaviors, and social standing they are awarded in Pakistan. The Constitution of Pakistan (1973) guarantees equal rights for all citizens of Pakistan, which affirms that all citizens are equal under the law and there shall be no discrimination on any grounds.

The ccGAP demonstrates the commitment by the Government of Pakistan to ensure the inclusion and leadership of women in climate action. While aligned with the priority sectors identified in the NCCP, the Pakistan ccGAP is a stand-alone document. It is an important step towards achieving key objectives of the NCCP to focus on pro-poor gender sensitive

[10] Climate Equity: Women as Agents of Change, NC20 Pakistan, UNDP, March 2022. Accessed at: <https://www.un.org.pk/sites/default/files/nc20_pakistan_women_access_water_climate_change>


---
**Page 236**
---

The National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) 2012 / Updated 2021

The main policy on climate change that includes a focus on Gender is the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP). The NCCP provides a framework for addressing the issues that Pakistan faces or will face in the future due to climate change. It sets out a clear roadmap for the development of Action Plans for national efforts on adaptation and mitigation, setting out a clear pathway for integrating gender equality into all aspects of policy making.

The National Climate Change Policy 2021 has been updated and has a specific focus on gender equality. Women are often viewed as vulnerable to climate change but they are also the solution. They are often the ones with first-hand knowledge of it. They are coming face to face with it.

Women's vulnerability is due to their disproportionate representation in rural communities, and people disproportionately as they are marginalized, vulnerable and have the least capacity to adapt to climate change. Women are more vulnerable to climate change as most rural women are engaged in the agriculture sector, which is highly climate sensitive. Climate change is expected to increase the work of agriculture production and other subsistence activities that women perform. Women also face greater risks from natural disasters on women. Further, women are found to be more vulnerable during extreme climate events and disasters.

* * *

**40**


---
**Page 237**
---

Pakistan is presently developing a National Climate Change Gender Action Plan (NCCGAP), because it fully recognizes that women are powerful agents of change. To address the gender aspects of vulnerability from climate change, the policy has several policy measures to mainstream gender perspectives into climate change policies at national and regional levels.

In response to the challenges of climate change in recognition of women’s diverse knowledge, leadership, and constraints in relation to the environment, the Climate Change Gender Action Plan (ccGAP) includes Water and WASH as a priority sector, aligned with the National Water Policy (NWP), 2018, and the National Flood Management Committee (NFMC).

### 4.3.2 National Water Policy of Pakistan 2018

The National Water Policy of Pakistan (NWP) describes the situation in the country as moving towards a situation of water shortage and by contrast, a threat of food insecurity. The situation calls for an urgent need to address the issue of water scarcity. Since Pakistan’s water resources are inextricably linked with climate and the impending climate change scenario has serious implications for Pakistan’s water resources, the objective of this National Water Policy is to take immediate action to mitigate these risks by formulating strategies, policies, and guidelines for a comprehensive plan of action in response to these risks.

Pakistan's economy is a 'water economy' with 60 percent of the population directly engaged in agriculture. The NWP aims at addressing water scarcity issues on these sectors. Approximately 95 percent of surface water and almost all fresh groundwater in Pakistan is currently used for agricultural purposes.

The constitution of Pakistan states that "no person shall be deprived of life or liberty save in accordance with law" (Article 9). Water is a basic need for living, which implies that it is the responsibility of the government to provide access to safe drinking water. However, if consumers are available but right to safe drinking water still needs to be recognized in clear terms in the constitution of Pakistan. In Water Act of Federally Administered Tribal Areas (FATA) and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa (KP), it is clearly stated that water belongs to the people; since FATA has been merged into the province of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, the act has become secondary.

In January 2021, an amendment to The Sind Water Management Ordinance. 2002 to include great women participation in water governance was passed. The Sind Water Management (Amendment) Ordinance, 2021, provides for the establishment of a women’s forum, 300 course associations, over 350 farmer organisations, and 14 area water boards in the province.

### Conclusions

The challenges of climate change impacts, building resilience and gender inequalities are inextricably linked. As exacerbating inequality, climate change slows progress toward gender equality and thus impedes efforts to achieve wider goals like poverty reduction and sustainable development. Women’s groups have requested women’s inclusion on the impacts of climate change


---
**Page 238**
---

further propel the 'feminization' of violence, poverty, marginalization and exclusion. Therefore,
taking steps to narrow the gender gap and empower women can help counter the impacts of
climate change.

The National Climate Change Policy 2012 updated 2021 is the only current policy which has a
specific focus on women's vulnerability and gender-sensitive objectives in relation to climate
change, and rural communities, and that rural women are likely to be strongly affected by climate
change. The other policies related to the environment do not have a strong focus on women,
which has been highlighted as a gap.

To mitigate the risks and make communities more resilient, Pakistan needs to put women right
at the Centre of the policy framework on climate change. Some progress has been made over
recent years in terms of gender mainstreaming in ecosystem and resilience-based programming
and mitigation, calling for a reappraisal of women's vulnerability and gender-sensitive objectives,
which are currently not adequately addressed.

The GCF policy and WWF’s gender policy are both good instruments to guide the design, and
implementation of the intended Women project in Pakistan.

# Chapter 5: Gender Roles in the Context of Local Ecosystems

This chapter is based on the qualitative and quantitative primary data that was obtained from the six sites in four provinces identified by WWF as intended locations for ecosystem-based project development. The focus group discussions were conducted with men and women respondents, and the results are juxtaposed here to see a comprehensive picture of local gender relations in the specific context of natural resource management.

## 5.1 Relationship of Women and Men to Natural Resources

Men and women in the focus group discussions were asked to identify the natural resources around their area. The respondents identified forests, land, grazing areas, rivers, canals, and lakes.

However, both men and women said that all resources were under the control of government or feudal landlords or rich people – tube wells, forests, agricultural land and fish – and that they felt rather helpless.

More than half of both men and women spoke of equal access to the (marine) resources. Others spoke of no access or access since everything belonged to the wadis/landlords. In Sindh it is government control with 'contractors brought in, and local people thrown off. Few owned land or had any say over natural resources. Access to fishing has been denied in recent years. Feudal control seems to be strengthening.

In terms of custodial rights or responsibilities, stewardship and governance roles towards natural resources, a majority did not report any custodial roles by communities or government.

In terms of indigenous rights and systems for using and sharing natural resources and environmental management practices, the respondents reported that there were no such systems. Very few instances water sharing for agriculture was reported, and also small infrastructure put up by government.

Traditional knowledge systems and practices still in use include rain-fed ponds, karez and wells.

## 5.2 Access and Control of Natural Resources

According to the respondents, powerful political landholders have increased their control over the natural resources since the introduction of feudalism. The respondents also said that since everything belonged to the landlords the respondents had little or no access to natural resources. Only a few respondents owned land, and those who did were males.

In Sindh on respondent said, "it is government control with contractors who are brought in is take over all the resources." In Balochistan, access to water for agriculture has also been denied by the fisherfolk, and feudal control appears to be becoming stronger.

However, gender differentials are positive, and both males and females said they had equal access to natural resources, indicating a balance of equity within the community.


---
**Page 240**
---

# Degradation of Natural Resources

Because of degradation and depletion of the natural resources, decline in environmental and ecology, livelihoods are shifting from fisheries and agriculture to manual labour in towns and cities. Livestock however has not declined in the same way, and women continue with livestock rearing.

Due to rampant ecological decline the lack of environmental goods have gone down drastically. Women (as opposed to livelihoods) about half the men and women can only partially depend on rainwater, water sources, common land, firewood, fodder, fuel. Some respondents said "nothing is free here."

# Resource Scarcity: Water for Women and Men to Water

Water was also identified as a resource scarce in rainfall, ponds, hill torrents, groundwater although saline and brackish or water from borings, wells, and tube wells, indicating the importance of water to the respondents. Both men and women in all the field sites across all the provinces were affected by this scarcity.

Differences emerged where for men water was important for irrigation, and for women water was a priority for drinking.

From household data we saw that more than fifty percent of households got their water from within km, while one quarter had a range of one kilometer and the remaining quarter between 2-10 kms. But not all nearest water sources necessarily have sweet water fit for drinking.

According to the respondents in the FGDs drinking water was largely unavailable in the villages, and had to be brought from outside their areas, bought from vendors, or collected from long distances. The scarcity of water has led to a decline in fish production. Fishers of approximately 30 years ago, compared to their present situation of poverty, "a poisoned lake," a decline in fish population, and the disappearance of migratory birds. One woman said, "I have never seen sweet water since my birth." And older women mentioned seeing migratory birds in their youth, but not for the last 30 years.

# 5.6 Impact of Climate Change

The impact of climate change was explored in the focus group discussions and changes in weather patterns were reported. In the past there was more rain, more water for crops, a better yield, more food on the second harvest. Now seasons are longer with extreme temperatures and short rains. Rainfall patterns have changed, some areas have dried up, and the water table has gone down.

Key government respondents pointed out the changes in last 20 years - temperature rise, less water (both surface and groundwater), irrigation has been affected, reduction in forest area, people are migrating out either seasonally or permanently.

Communities have been affected in many ways: fishing has gone down, and peoples' livelihoods have changed. In some areas the trees have died off (trees gone, vegetation dwindling) there are more dust storms. Availability of food has also declined.

Rainfall patterns have changed, either more or less rain and at odd times, negatively affecting cultivation. In Manchar area it was reported that poisonous water of the lake has huge impacts on the communities.

Migrations were reported in Balochistan due to change in weather patterns, denudation of vegetation cover and decrease in groundwater.


---
**Page 242**
---

# Protection of Natural Resources

According to the respondents to protect the natural resources, the government should provide free, clean drinking water, small ponds for irrigation and water storage. Pollution into rivers and canals should be stopped. Fishermen should be provided with adequate fishing equipment. Seeds should be provided to those that have lost their fishing and agriculture-based livelihoods and all cutting of forests should be stopped.

# Participation of Women in Government Projects

As part of the data collection, government representatives from the Irrigation Department and Planning Commission were interviewed. In total 10 respondents were interviewed at the Key Informants meeting; six males and four females were employed in all of the provincial departments.

In Balochistan, and KP, the government representatives said that they were aware of women working in their departments but they did not have any female officers or employees in senior positions. In Sindh and Punjab, the respondents said that they were unaware of any women in their departments. The government representatives from all four provinces said that they had never consulted at the design stage and said that projects were already designed and given to them to implement without consulting with the communities.

In Balochistan, KP, and Sindh, the government representatives said that they did not consult women as they were not perceived as relevant to infrastructure there was no need to consult women as the work related largely to engineering and they did not use social mobilization in their jobs. Only one respondent in Punjab said that he knew of a few women in higher positions in departments but he did not know if they had any role or responsibilities. None of them mentioned any gender equality considerations in the projects they implemented, and all of them thought that women were not involved in the planning process.

However, they did believe that women were the main beneficiaries of development and infrastructure projects, as these projects provided them with safe water and better irrigation meant more food and income for their families.

# Roles and Responsibilities of Government Departments in the Provinces

Most of the government respondents from all four provinces said that they worked field staff with very specific responsibilities. They had no role or authority to plan and implement projects within their departments. They could only talk about own roles and functions. Only two respondents mentioned that they were involved in planning and implementation.

Based on responsibilities articulated by respondents we can see that the range of activities they work on cannot be construed as nature-based solutions.

Only in the Punjab project area is there UNICEF funded project in place so we can see proper planning, monitoring and impact following international/UNICEF rules. In the project sites of three provinces no such projects are implemented, and only routine departmental activities seem to be in place.

No social mobilization mechanism or instruments exist within any department to execute a social mobilization plan. Only in Punjab where a UNICEF project is implemented is there a community mobilization mechanism through CBOs.


---
**Page 243**
---

# 5.10 Management of Water and Environmental Resources

Most of the government officials mentioned groundwater, tube wells, solar pumps as the main sources of water for domestic use, while in Balochistan a government water supply system identified as the main sources of water for domestic use. Tube wells and canals/barrage are the main sources of irrigation water. In KPK there were 40 solar tube wells also in use to irrigate 1000-acre land.

Drinking water quality is mostly not so good, saline/saline/khich, bitter, yellow and bad in taste. In only one area of Purlab irrigation water testing facility exists, while in Balochistan and Sindh government officials mentioned that irrigation department is responsible for testing of quality of irrigation water.

Due to water pollution drinking water quality is affected in most parts of the areas, whereas in March 2018 water is potable and not fit for use by people. Soil erosion problems are mostly chemical fertilizer, pesticides and industrial wastes while mixing of sewage water is a chief problem. Water pollution in KPK, Sindh and Balochistan area of Purlab irrigation water is also reported by government officials because the river has no crops, no rice chemicals fertilizer and no industry near the fields.

Government officials said that solutions are to reduce use of pesticides, and chemical fertilizer, separate drinking water lines from canals/irrigation water; do not release industrial waste into canals. Reverse Osmosis (RO) and filtration plants that provide drinking water.

Government officials mentioned a list of diseases associated with water pollution: diarrhea, cholera, typhoid fever, dysentery, skin disease, eye infection, etc. While destruction of crops, reduction in fish catch and decrease in crop production, reduction of land fertility and economic loss are mentioned as economic risks associated with water reduction and pollution.

Decline in crop production, reduced land fertility, migration of communities and livelihoods shifting from agriculture and fishing to manual labour in nearby cities, are listed as impact of water pollution. These problems are more severe in KPK and Balochistan. Government officials talked of economic crises occurring during floods and drought.

## 5.11 Conclusions

It can be clearly concluded that climate change has already negatively affected the equation and balance between people and nature. The real changes are visible and the forced migrations, poverty and loss of livelihoods are a reality to different degrees in all project sites.

The impact of climate change in the form of changing weather patterns and extreme temperatures are changes that are a reality for the women and men in the project sites. Adaptation to these changes and interventions for addressing these changes need to be designed and implemented with the participation of the communities concerned. Solutions are now needed. Shrinking natural resource base has led to migration and a shift from traditional livelihoods to manual labour, while those who have migrated back home face daily struggles for survival. These issues are all part of the challenges that the Rekha Palli Bachao project is addressing.

The tendency of elites to control resources and their ability to manipulate government functionaries in difficult times means that major environmental and ecosystem resources will continue to be denied to the landless and the poor, especially women.


---
**Page 244**
---

WWF has to be careful in the way the gender dialectic is used, because it can lead away from the plight of both poor men and women in the face of elite capture. The focus should not only be on building gender parity between community women and men, but in providing interventions that empower them to access resources for their water and food security. It is important to strengthen institutions that render poor men and women benefit of their traditional livelihoods. Extra care is needed to ensure that the rights of the communities remain and they continue to access common pool resources for their water and food security.

As for the government, it is clear from the interviews of functionaries that they are mostly clueless on gender terminology, policies and actions and would require a lot of attention in the intended project design and implementation of the Gender Action Plan.


---
**Page 245**

# Chapter 6: Socio-Economic Profile

Any gender assessment requires a socio-economic and environmental context within which gender issues can be understood correctly and the most suitable and effective interventions developed. This chapter presents the socio-economic profile of the case study communities that are community based, where, in addition to national and provincial situation, the local poverty and other socio-economic conditions have been taken into consideration.

In the case of this gender assessment the socio-economic information was collected from households through a quantitative instrument that was developed and pretested by IRS Global, Inc. The data were collected during the baseline survey conducted as part of the data gathering exercise managed by their field teams in the six project sites designated by WWF. We have duly acknowledged the contribution of DevCon.

Information has been extracted from 327 households. Of these men respondents provided information for 311 households and women respondents for 316 households. Please note that several tables below are multiple response.


## Age-Sex Distribution and Marital Status

53 percent of all people recorded are 20 years or below, in keeping with national trends. While 45 percent are between the ages of 21-45, only 4.5 percent are over 45 years, and that too mostly women. It is quite usual for people in rural settings of Pakistan to not know birthdays and given their age, the data also includes a smaller proportion of people who can be bread earners.

Male female ratio is 52:48 reflecting the national figures. Marital status of people age 16 years and above is about three quarters; 83 percent of women and 70 percent of men being married.

When we review this data in juxtaposition with the FGDs data we see that most of the people there reported unemployment. So except for government employees, women and some workers who work as professionals or through small businesses currently they do not have a means of income. When read with other findings we can see that the environment for their livelihood (especially for those who are involved in agriculture, livestock and handicrafts) is dwindling and degrading – through climate change, privatization and government policies.

FGDs further show that mostly men are involved in work that is usually paid (when they get the work), and women usually look after domestic chores and are unpaid. In some instances, women who do not have any means of income are forced to leave their communities. When such women did not report working in any profession or carrying any jobs. While men left their communities for labor work elsewhere, women tend to remain.

## 6.4 Household Ownership and Construction

A total of 84 percent of the households have their own houses, but this should be taken with a pinch of salt because in some cases, landless people (who do share cropping) are "allowed" to build their non-permanent kaacha/houses on the land owned by landowners. They typically live there for as long as they can and may be displaced from these "owned" houses at any time by the people who actually own the land.

Kaacha houses refer to structures built with mud/clay, wood parts and roofs thatched with local reeds or grasses. The more permanent kaacha/houses are those built with bricks, some cement or concrete and some piping. Sem pakka is a mix of both styles.

### 6.5 Household Expenditure

From field exposure we have seen that except for government servants, men and in particular women, are unable to provide a reliable estimate of their monthly or annual income because they do not get regular income, but they were able to give broad estimates of what they spend on. The majority of women reported that they do not know how much money is spent by any kind reinforces the findings from the FGDs which show that women do not have much say in decision making and are often unaware or only partially aware of how much is being spent by the men of the family on key households expenditures.

### 6.9 Activities of Men and Women Inside and Outside Home

Respondents were asked to state what activities the men of the family did inside the house and outside the house. They were then asked about what activities women undertook within and outside the house.

In terms of how the roles of men and women have changed over the last 20 years, the FGDs showed that previously men and women worked together in fisheries and agriculture. Now men have more income than before and are able to buy goods for their families. It was reported that in recent years men help in fetching water, collecting firewood and look after livestock.

### Conclusions

The Socio-economic picture that emerges of the communities in the six WWF sites shows clearly that most people are very poor, indebted and live from hand-to-mouth. While many of the indicators are typical of rural areas of Pakistan (education, access to public services, poor water and sanitation), many others point to poverty, loss of livelihoods, no opportunities and sense of hopelessness.

Except for government servants, men and in particular women, cannot provide estimate of their monthly or annual income because they do not get regular income. Women do not seem to be aware that they are working and earning money for the family, and may not even be aware of how much is being spent by the men of the family on key household items.

Mostly men are involved in work that is usually paid (when they get the work), and women usually look after domestic chores and are unpaid. In some instances, women and men worked together for a while but then stopped working as a couple because women did not report working in any professions or carrying any jobs. While men left their communities for labor work elsewhere, women tend to remain.


---
**Page 251**
---

The environment for their traditional professions and means of income (fisheries, agriculture, livestock and handicrafts) is dwindling and degrading - through climate change, privatization and government take-over.

Lifting the socio-economic profiles of the poorest communities in the six project sites will be a challenge, so that earning opportunities for men and women have to be an integral part of the design of the project.


---
**Page 252**
---

# Chapter 7: Gender Mainstreaming in WWF

## 7.1 WWF Gender Policy

WWF adopted its Gender Policy in 2011. This policy reflects WWF's ongoing commitment to equity and integrating a gender perspective in its policies, programs, and projects, as well as in its overall work.

The WWF Global Network Policy: Gender Policy Statement 2011 states that "The policy statement is to ensure that WWF’s conservation policies, programs and activities benefit women and men equally. This policy statement sets out a framework for WWF’s global commitment to strengthen the social dimensions of its projects, programs, and policy work." It describes how WWF will integrate gender equality into its conservation goals and strategies, land and footprint goals and outlines WWF's commitment to integrate a gender perspective in our policies and programs. The policy statement also sets out a framework for WWF’s global commitment to promoting gender equity and integrating a gender perspective 'in projects and programs and to ensure that women and men are equally represented at all levels of the organization'. It describes how this will be achieved through the application of a gender awareness and analysis in the project cycle, including design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. The policy statement also sets out a framework for creating an enabling work environment where management and other staff are aware of what constitutes discriminatory practices and how to address them, such as harassment in the workplace, unfair job offers and present discriminatory practices including stereotyping and sexual harassment.

In this context the WWF Recharge Pakistan project has conducted this Gender Analysis and developed a Gender Action Plan to ensure that gender mainstreaming is built into the design phase of the project which reflects WWF's continuing commitment to its policy and future action.


## 7.2 Review of WWF Annual Reports 2019-2021

A brief review of WWF Pakistan’s Annual Report over a period of three years 2019-2021 mentions gender in several places and specifically women's inclusion across six areas of focus: "Examples include projects which have included women, and supported women's empowerment were described. Examples include the Kharpur Watershed project focusing on women, support to cluster organizations for women’s empowerment, women’s participation in the promotion and diversification of livelihoods in Balochistan™, the Food and Markets projects where 835 farming households including working women were supported with gender trainings to develop their skill so they could contribute more effectively to household income."

The 2021 WWF installed one rainwater harvesting system at Government College Women University Sialkot, to offset the use of groundwater during the rainy season, illustrating support to women in agriculture. Another example is the project on coastal resilience and adaptation in the Indus Delta engaging women 'sheroes', a community-focused plantation to enhance coastal climate resilience and provide a livelihood to the community. Similarly, 106 women
