### 7.3 WWF Gender Projects

Three gender project reports were received from WWF and reviewed to understand to what extent gender was addressed among WWF's overall projects. A brief analysis of these reports indicates that skill training to increase women’s income generation and potential for alternative livelihoods is a key focus area in WWF’s gender projects.

The following four gender specific projects in the Livelihood Improvement & Gender Empowerment Initiatives, which focus on increasing women’s economic empowerment are highlighted:

1. **Indus Eco Region Community Livelihood Project 2019** integrates women by promoting livelihoods and income generation opportunities for women through training and capacity building.
2. **Sindhi Women Empowerment Project (SWEP)** is a project that aims to empower women in the Sindh 2 Grow Oxfam project Annual Report monitors gender outcomes and impact as well.

However, the gender data is scattered and although it exists across reports and projects, there is no hard evidence of gender mainstreaming for beneficiaries, indicators of success, and impact on women to record and understand the extent of gender mainstreaming within WWF’s programs.

The Implementation Completion and results report for the Indus Eco Region Community Livelihood Project 2019[1] is helpful because it has some overlap with the current GCF project. This project integrates women into the project by promoting alternative livelihoods. This comprises training in block printing, stitching, and craft management to develop a sustainable fishing practices, enhancing income generation opportunities for youth, women, and the elderly. The project also aims at developing cottage industries and developing effective and sustainable linkages to markets and local governments.

A review of this project document indicates an overlap with the field sites of the Recharge Pakistan Project and could provide a good basis to build on the outcomes of the Indus Eco Region Community Livelihood Project 2019. According to the Community Livelihood Project report, at project closure, the project developed women’s capacity in alternative livelihoods and income generation was modest. Direct female project beneficiaries were 17 percent of women HHs. The project developed women’s capacity in alternative livelihoods and income generation was modest.

The project aimed at developing women’s capacity in alternative livelihoods and income generation was modest. Direct female project beneficiaries were 17 percent of women HHs. The project developed women’s capacity in alternative livelihoods and income generation was modest. Women were provided training in block printing, stitching and craft management. Under the Indus Eco Region Community Livelihood Units (ILPUs) were given to ten women at Nara Canal, Keenyar Lake, Manchar Lake and Cholur Reservoir. Gender mainstreaming was an important aspect of the project.

### 7.4 Case Studies from the Field

A review of Case Studies from the field also demonstrate how WWF has improved women’s economic and social status as Case Study I “Micro Nurseries Run by Women[2]” has led to income generation for rural women, giving them a sense of empowerment and enabling women


---
**Page 254**
---

to choose how to spend their income, and learn how to manage small nurseries. Similarly,
Case Study 2, a WWF – Better Cotton Initiative® clearly shows the impact on women's
livelihoods through income generation by cultivating tree nurseries and selling the saplings to
generate revenue for themselves and their families.

WWF’s gender mainstreaming approach in its projects includes women participation, with
gender responsive interventions, and recognise the ingrained gender bias in rural society which
prevents women’s participation in decision making. And as part of their program WWF also
increases women’s access to resources and opportunities for income generation.

### 7.5 Focus Group Discussion with WWF's Women Staff

To continue a further analysis of gender mainstreaming, and opportunities for women's
leadership within WWF, a focused group discussion was held with a cross section of female staff from different departments in Pakistan. The participants were all women who had worked with WWF between one and five years (and one who had been there considerably longer) and represented the following areas: Community Development, Environment, Forests,
Food and Markets, Recharge Pakistan, Audits, and Program Development. The positions of the
staff were Managers, Coordinators, Senior Project Officers, Research Associates, and a Gender Coordinator.

The FGD[58] addressed recruitment procedures, opportunities for gender training, gender
sensitization of men and women on gender issues in Pakistan, numbers of men and women in
gender roles within WWF, the role of women in decision making, changes in the position of
women within the organization in the last ten years, potential for an enabling environment in
the workplace and challenges for staff and women's leadership.

#### 7.5.1 Recruitment, Initiation and Orientation

The FGD showed that recruitment and initiation of staff into WWF is in response to
advertisements in newspapers where candidates are shortlisted, interviewed, tested, and called for a final interview. The process can be as simple as a phone call or as complex as an
“interview panel” depending on the requirements of the position. The respondents were unaware if WWF is an “equal opportunity employer,” but they were aware that there was no gender discrimination in positions advertised. From their experience the respondents stated that there is no bias in the hiring process and that women are hired for all types of projects, because travel to remote field sites is not considered a barrier for employing women.

Orientation to WWF’s rules and procedures was a regular practice and an Orientation
package was provided to new staff members. The package included information about the
workplace and how to address it as part of the Orientation package, but gender orientation is
not part of the orientation process. In projects if there are donor requirements for gender
training then this is arranged separately by the project team. There were no specific WWF staff
Gender sensitization i.e., the status of women and men in Pakistan is not provided as part of a
package.

#### 7.5.2 Familiarity with WWF’s Gender Policy

[58] <https://www.bettercotton.org.uk/uk/>

The Focus Group Discussion was based around 14 questions and was conducted through a zoom meeting ensuring the participants complete confidentiality of their responses. The names of the participants remain anonymous.

*Only one woman had worked with WWF for longer than five years.*

58


---
**Page 255**
---

Blunt gender concepts are not taught, and the definition of a gender specialist was perceived as "a person who can communicate with both men and women in the agricultural sector's farming families." And it appeared that gender integration is in response to donor requirements rather than an organizational priority. Women were not included in the decision-making process for addressing sexual harassment or discrimination in the workplace. It is not formally presented at the time of orientation, training, or induction. However, women are involved in activities such as gender policy through the organization, to programs and projects. However, the respondents said that women staff are always involved in project team discussions, and women are included in the planning process. Women's participation was also mentioned when it comes to women's empowerment. This was mentioned in reference to when the head of a department is a woman.

WWF's global gender policy, and the GCF policy was new information to the participants who were interviewed. The majority of respondents said that they had not heard about this. All staff follows the WWF global gender policy, and all except one respondent had not heard of these gender policies. However, the lack of awareness of the WWF's global gender policy within WWF's female staff reflects a gap in the organization towards gender integration and mainstreaming.

### 7.5.3 Gender Design and Development

Project design and development takes place in individual departments, and projects are developed according to donor requirements, with technical support if needed from other departments. If there is no need for the donor; then gender modules are developed for implementation in the communities.

Addressing gender in the programs, or within the organization comes from the head of the department. The perception of women's empowerment at WWF is that women's empowerment is perceived as driven with a top-down approach; and male managers make the main decisions on how to address gender issues. Women are not involved in decision-making processes, and there is no formal space for women in WWF to voice their opinions, to hear and share ideas that they can put forward in the projects.

However, in complete contrast to their experiences the respondents spoke of WWF's grassroots approach to gender integration where a Strategic Five-Year plan where gender is integrated, gender empowerment is a primary component, gender norms, cultural sensitivity, empowerment, and women's rights are addressed. Women's participation in decision-making and women's engagement in projects is include in the strategic planning process. Gender action plans, strategies, and activities are developed by the women themselves as part of the planning process. And the women staff also emphasized the strong involvement of women in agricultural extension services. The respondents said that the role and contribution of women was recognised and encouraged at the field, community, and project level, but not within the office workplace.

The project staff also commented that women in the communities are involved in advocacy activities to speak at district forums, and community women's representation and leadership is supported and promoted. The respondents thought that WWF had developed its own set of gender policies and strategies and said, we are getting there and someday soon we will be a gender sensitive organization.


---
**Page 256**
---

The WWF Organogram as of [shows that there are] 31 Senior Managerial staff in WWF, of which 10 are women. The Head of WWF is a man, and in the second tier there are six Senior Management positions of which one woman is the Head/Senior Manager of Communications, in the Lahore office. In the third tier, among seven Managers and Coordinators, there is one woman in the position of Coordinator Livelihood Improvement and Gender Empowerment, in the Lahore office. In the fourth tier, among seven Managers and Coordinators there are two women: one is a Senior Manager of Environment Education and Communication in the Lahore office, and one woman is who is Coordinator Policy and Subnational Partnerships in Islamabad. In the fifth tier, among six Managers and Coordinators there is one woman who is Head/Senior Manager in the Climate and Energy / Corporate Partnership Program in Islamabad. In the sixth tier, among five Managers and Coordinators there is one woman as Manager People Development in the Islamabad office, and one Coordinator EED program in Lahore. In the seventh tier, among four Managers and Coordinators there are two women as Coordinator Freshwater Program in Sialkot, and a Senior Office Communication and Benefits Officer in Lahore. In the eighth tier, among three Managers and Coordinators there is only 3 percent women in WWF, and women are scattered throughout the organisation; but in the sixth and seventh tier there are only women (Annex A12).

Information received from WWF regarding the number of men to women in the organisation indicates that in the first tier there are 40 men for every woman. In the Management tier there are 38 men to 4 women, in Finance and Administration there are 145 men and 6 women, 1 man to 72 women. In WWF Pakistan there are 90 men and 150 women.

The percentage of men is 9 percent to 9 percent women[2].

### Work Environment

Further discussion on an enabling work environment revealed a day care center at the Head Office which was not functional, lack of a prayer room for women, and the absence of a private room for women to go if they were feeling unwell were mentioned. Women's health during pregnancies, or reproductive health issues were not recognized, and the absence of empathy for women who had children in their care was noted. The perception that having a child after she got married was perceived as an impediment to her work and ability to do the job effectively was also noted.

A lack of gender sensitivity towards women travelling alone with male drivers, and examples of the lack of respect shown by some men towards women who were traveling in remote villages where they often had to spend days in the communities during their menstrual cycle were commented upon. The provision of a day care center for children of employees was not viewed favorably, and although an official Maternity Leave policy exists, it was not always implemented with full support to the woman concerned.

The WWF Employees Rules 2022[3] contains a Maternity policy and a Paternity policy. The Maternity Leave is 15 days for women employees who are permitted Maternity Leave with a salary for 3 months. The leave includes weekends and any public holidays and can be availed twice during service.

The Paternity Leave is 7 days for male employees, which is permitted Paternity Leave with salary equivalent to 5 workdays. The leave can only be availed twice during service.

7.5.8 Changes in Women’s Position in Last 10 Years

And yes, there have been positive changes in women’s position in the last ten years within WWF. A few women joined the organization in certain projects a few years ago, but recently more women have joined the organization, and a positive change in attitude from male colleagues has also been observed. Women are now seen as equal team members; supportive. Travel is comfortable, and hotels, and safe places to stay were appreciated by the respondents. Women have also relocated to different cities for the sake of the job, and this is accepted within WWF. The women have also been given more responsibilities in the field, breaking stereotype roles which has led to greater acceptance. In most cases it was stated that if the head of a project is female, she can make decisions on her own without having to go through office protocol, and it was the responsibility of the women to speak for herself, and for her management team.

From the FGDs it appeared that there is a clear dichotomy between women’s roles in projects and within the organization. Women participate in decisions related to project design and activities but are not involved in decision-making at higher levels. There were also concerns about the perception that women can travel long distances and work in remote areas is not a barrier to their work. However, within the organisation the situation is different, and an enabling environment for women’s participation needs to be created. Women need to be encouraged to speak up and voice their opinions on improving their working environment. These written concerns were also echoed by the FGDs. It was observed that most of these women are supported by their senior managers, and if gender responsive mechanisms are developed, greater understanding of a gender sensitive environment can be created within WWF.

### 7.6 Conclusions

WWF has a strong Gender policy and follows the IUCN Gender Policy Guidelines. It also has several projects which focus on how to support women’s employment, but gender data is scattered across different reports and websites, and an Annual Gender Report covering all the current projects with disaggregated data, number of male and female beneficiaries and impact on their lives would be a good way forward.

A review of WWF's projects, and their interaction at the local level with the communities indicates women’s inclusion and participation, with gender sensitive interventions, and addressing gender inequalities. The organization has also been successful in increasing women participation in decision making. And WWF also increases women’s awareness of the importance of gender equality in relation to conservation of biodiversity, ecosystems and natural resources. Work in the field and projects has a large component of gender and responds to donor requirements.

However, within the FGDs respondents from the FGDs indicated that gender trainings for both men and women are absent and should be developed and integrated into the Orientation process for a new employee. Gender concepts, WWF’s gender policies, and gender equality in relation to conservation of biodiversity, ecosystems and natural resources and the importance of gender in WWF as an institution should be part of the initiation process.

Reproductive health issues within the office and the field are not always recognised or supported by the organization. The respondents indicated that reproductive health related concerns should be considered and become a part of formal institutional protocols. The legal requirements under the Population Act 2015, and the Sexual Offences Act 2010, should also be understood and all staff should be informed, and the mechanisms for recourse within the law should be established in the organization.


---
**Page 258**
---

Similarly, to develop an enabling environment a gender focal point should be designated to ensure that gender policies are translated through the organization at all levels. And this person should have a strong gender background and experience in gender issues.

In terms of increasing awareness of gender issues, gender analysis training in collection of data, tabulation, and reporting would increase the understanding and capacity of the staff to gender-responsive programming.

And finally, there are several women in WWF at different strata of the organization, but only a few in leadership and decision-making positions. Therefore, these women should be trained and provided with opportunities for leadership roles and decision-making positions, and a clear career path should be offered to women as they progress within WWF.

62

# Gender Action Plan:

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the Recharge Pakistan project is developed based on the results and recommendations from the Gender Assessment. The GAP contains specific actions against each activity that aim to ensure gender issues are adequately addressed and bring noticeable benefits to women and men, especially in vulnerable groups. It aims to ensure that both men and women have equal opportunities to participate in, and benefit from, the project activities.

The project activities will contribute towards gender mainstreaming in the country by:
- Promoting gender equality
- Establishing strategic plans, and to develop and implement community-level awareness campaigns on climate change impacts and the projects' adaptation measures.
- Strengthening the capacity of local communities to undertake community-based natural resource management.
- Improving climate resilience of the vulnerable groups through agricultural livelihood activities and capacity building of vulnerable communities to understand and respond to climate change.
- Building capacity by key stakeholders including Government line departments, CBOs, and local communities to apply a gender lens into their own programs.

The Gender Specialist (Manager, Gender) based at the Project Management Unit (PMU) will lead the implementation of the GAP. In addition, the Gender Specialist will conduct a needs/capacity assessment at the onset of the project implementation phase to evaluate the level and frequency of training and support that PMU staff need in order to effectively implement gender mainstreaming activities. The Gender Specialist will also provide technical assistance on gender mainstreaming, how to recognize and address GBV and SEAH issues using the preventive structures put in place in the project to respond efficiently and appropriately.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is being implemented through a team of 12 staff members who are part of the PMU. The team consists of 6 male and 6 female staff to facilitate implementation of the GAP. In addition, each of the five thematic areas has been assigned one Coordinator, Gender Empowerment and Livelihood Development Unit (Di Khan and Lamwari will be covered by one Coordinator) have been budgeted for this purpose. The PMU has also hired a gender focal person who will provide technical assistance on gender mainstreaming and offer maximum interaction with local communities and key stakeholders at national and sub-national levels.

Additionally, cooperating organizations such as National Commissions on the status of Women; Women Development Department (WDD), Council on Gender Equality, Vocational Training Institutions, Government agencies and line departments at national and sub-national levels working on Gender Mainstreaming have also been engaged in this project. The PMU has called upon them with the implementation of these activities.

**Note:**
To avoid duplication, beneficiaries will only be targeted once inspection of how many activities they participated in.
While setting the targets, the social and cultural norms are kept in mind to ensure representation of participation across each site.
Percentage representation of women will be ensured from the very beginning of each activity, and no ramp time shall be required to attain the targets.

# Output Activities Gender Specific Actions Responsible Indicators and Targets Timeline Budget

## Component 2: Enhanced community resilience and adoption of EBA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan's Indus Basin.

### Outcome 3: Enhanced resilience of community livelihoods in the Indus Basin.

#### Output 3.1: Pipeline of feasible climate-resilient businesses

##### Activity 3.1.1:
- **Description:** Development of 7 business building workshops focused on sustainable climate-resilient businesses.
- **Gender Specific Actions:** Ensure women and men are equally represented when carrying out these activities.
- **Project Team:** Women and Gender Project Team
- **Consultants:** Cooperating organizations
- **Coordinators:** Local government departments

##### Indicators a:
- **Indicator a1: Number of women attended capacity building workshops on bankable projects**
  - Target: 80 women (20 from each sector)
  - Indicator b: Number of people adopting businesses supported under the project
  - Target: Up to 2 women-led businesses (out of 7)

##### Timeline:
- Q3Y1 – Q4Y7

##### Budget:
Covered by output budget.
